Nuclear power killed fewer people in seventy years than coal kills before lunch on a Tuesday.

You probably knew that, vaguely, in the way you vaguely know that seatbelts save lives or that smoking causes cancer. You filed it under “technically true but not actionable” and moved on. Most people do.

Here is what you probably do not know.

You do not know that 44% of the world’s uranium enrichment capacity sits inside Russia, and that the West has no way to replace it before the early 2030s. You do not know that the entire Western conversion supply chain, the step between mining uranium and enriching it, runs through three buildings on two continents operating at 91% utilization with zero buffer for disruption. You do not know that a single landlocked Central Asian country produces 39% of the world’s uranium, that more than half its output now flows to Chinese buyers under long-term contracts, and that 90% of its exports historically travel by rail through Russia.

You do not know that the US natural gas price is the single largest determinant of whether Constellation Energy’s nuclear fleet is worth $50 billion or $150 billion. You do not know that interest during construction, not reactor physics, not materials science, not regulatory obstruction, is the mechanism that turned every Western nuclear project of the past two decades into a financial catastrophe. You do not know that the difference between China building a reactor in five years for $2.7 billion and France building one in seventeen years for $23.7 billion is not technology. It is institutional coherence. Same concrete. Same steel. Same physics. Different result.

You do not know that the spot uranium price, the number that shows up on financial websites, is a sideshow derived from a handful of weekly trades, sometimes just one, while 85-90% of the real market moves through private bilateral contracts that will not become public for months. You do not know that when enrichment prices quadruple (they did), the rational response for utilities is to use *more* uranium per kilogram of fuel, not less, creating a second-order demand tailwind that most supply models ignore entirely. You do not know that Form Energy is building 100-hour iron-air batteries that could narrow the addressable market for new nuclear construction by 2030, a risk the bulls have not priced and the bears have not articulated clearly.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!8njG!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5c92d868-6a4b-48b2-be0e-55d406ce512f_2070x1158.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!8njG!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5c92d868-6a4b-48b2-be0e-55d406ce512f_2070x1158.png)

This primer exists because the gap between “nuclear is coming back” and “here is exactly how the fuel cycle works, where the chokepoints are, who controls them, what the bear cases are, and how to actually position a portfolio” is approximately the size of the Grand Canyon. Most of what has been written to fill that gap is either too technical for generalists, too shallow for professionals, or too ideological to be useful for either.

We wrote 45,000 words to fix that.

What follows is a first-principles walkthrough of nuclear power, from the physics of fission to the structure of the uranium market to the geopolitics of enrichment to the investable universe, written for the kind of person who wants to actually understand what they own and why they own it. We assume you are intelligent but not yet an expert. By the time you finish, you will be dangerous.

### What is in this Primer?

**Part 1** builds the demand case: why global electricity consumption is accelerating, why intermittent renewables cannot fill the gap alone, and why nuclear’s physics gives it a structural advantage no other clean energy source can match.

**Parts 2 and 3** take you inside the machine: how fission works, why reactor designs matter, and the five-stage fuel cycle supply chain where every bottleneck has a name, a price, and an owner.

**Parts 4 and 5** map the market and the geopolitics: why uranium does not trade like any commodity you have seen before, where the structural deficit is real (and where the bulls overstate it), and why 44% of global enrichment capacity sitting inside Russia is the most underpriced risk in energy.

**Parts 6 and 7** confront the hard questions: why China builds reactors in five years and the West takes seventeen, whether NRC reform changes the equation, what the safety record actually says, and why SMRs are five to ten years behind the narrative.

**Parts 8 and 9** deploy everything: the investable universe across five distinct markets, the TSCS portfolio framework, the five propositions every nuclear position maps to, and every bear case we could find, including the ones the bulls skip.

## **Part 1: Why Nuclear, Why Now**

### **1.1 The Dirtiest Clean Energy Story Ever Told**

To understand why nuclear power is about to have its moment, you need to understand why it lost its moment in the first place.

In 1996, nuclear power generated 17.6% of the world’s electricity. The atom was winning. France had gone all-in during the 1970s oil shocks and built 58 reactors in roughly two decades, a feat of industrial coordination that makes China’s Belt and Road look like a school fundraiser. The United States had 104 operating reactors. Japan was building them like Toyota builds Corollas. The future looked fissile.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!cmJT!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F89dc04e2-982b-4c75-a1a6-c750cece0e7c_1405x779.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!cmJT!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F89dc04e2-982b-4c75-a1a6-c750cece0e7c_1405x779.png)

Then the world collectively lost its nerve.

Three Mile Island in 1979 started the rot in America, not because the accident was catastrophic (it wasn’t; the containment held, nobody died, the maximum offsite dose was equivalent to a chest X-ray), but because it was *televised*. The China Syndrome had hit theaters twelve days earlier. Hollywood could not have scripted a better PR disaster for the nuclear industry if it had tried. And it did try, repeatedly, for the next four decades.

Chernobyl in 1986 was the real kill shot. An RBMK reactor, a Soviet design with no containment building and a positive void coefficient (meaning it gets *more* reactive as it loses coolant, which is roughly the engineering equivalent of designing a car whose brakes make it accelerate), blew its lid during a safety test conducted by operators who had disabled most of the safety systems. The explosion and subsequent graphite fire spread radioactive material across Europe. The political fallout was worse than the literal fallout. Italy shut down its entire nuclear program. Germany began its long, slow march toward Atomausstieg. Sweden held a referendum. The global nuclear pipeline didn’t just slow down; it froze.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!GjeE!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Faba68b21-13ba-4e5c-8315-c2f63b1c19ba_1405x782.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!GjeE!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Faba68b21-13ba-4e5c-8315-c2f63b1c19ba_1405x782.png)

Then Fukushima in 2011 finished the job. A magnitude 9.0 earthquake followed by a 14-meter tsunami overwhelmed the backup diesel generators at Fukushima Daiichi, and without cooling, three cores melted down. Japan shut down all 54 of its reactors within 14 months. Germany accelerated its nuclear exit. Global nuclear capacity flatlined. The share of world electricity from nuclear dropped from that 17.6% peak to roughly 9%, where it has sat, comatose, for over a decade.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!4BZp!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fb67599f6-141d-4f41-ad83-f3fd4976f436_1405x788.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!4BZp!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fb67599f6-141d-4f41-ad83-f3fd4976f436_1405x788.png)

Here is the punchline that almost nobody in the general public understands: across all three of these accidents, spanning three decades and three different reactor designs on three different continents, the total confirmed death toll from acute radiation exposure is fewer than 50 people. All from Chernobyl.

The WHO’s most aggressive long-term projections estimate around 4,000 additional cancer deaths over the lifetimes of the most exposed populations, though the linear no-threshold model underlying that estimate is itself debated among radiation biologists (we will examine this in Part 7).

The one clearly documented radiogenic outcome is a sharp increase in thyroid cancer among children exposed to iodine-131 in the immediate aftermath, most of whom survived with treatment, but which remains the strongest evidence that Chernobyl caused measurable population-level harm beyond the acute deaths.

That is a tragedy. It is also fewer people than coal kills every single day through air pollution. The WHO estimates that outdoor air pollution, of which coal combustion is a leading contributor, kills roughly 4.2 million people per year globally.

Nuclear power killed fewer people in 70 years than coal kills before lunch on a Tuesday.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!TZ1f!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F0adc1825-8125-493f-a50b-c431242f897e_1405x784.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!TZ1f!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F0adc1825-8125-493f-a50b-c431242f897e_1405x784.png)

But radiation is invisible and scary, and coal smoke is familiar and slow, so here we are. The human brain is not wired to assess risk rationally. It is wired to assess risk *emotionally*. And few things trigger primal fear like the word “nuclear.” The mushroom cloud. The Geiger counter. The hazmat suit. The entire aesthetic of nuclear catastrophe has been baked into our cultural firmware since Hiroshima, and no amount of statistical evidence has been able to overwrite it.

Until now.

Something changed. And what changed was not public sentiment, not some grassroots awakening to the comparative safety data. What changed was the electricity bill.

### **1.2 The Grid is Hungry and Nobody Has Enough Food**

Here is the situation, stated plainly.

Global electricity demand grew by roughly 4.3% in 2024 (subsequently revised to 4.4% in the IEA’s Mid-Year Update), crossing 30,000 TWh for the first time in history. That is over 1,200 TWh of incremental demand in a single year.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!pyx3!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F47e76cd9-15e9-46d6-8369-bd2a57113caf_1405x788.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!pyx3!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F47e76cd9-15e9-46d6-8369-bd2a57113caf_1405x788.png)

To put that in perspective, 1,172 TWh is roughly the entire annual electricity consumption of Germany and France combined. The world added the equivalent of those two countries’ power demand in one year, and it barely made the news.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!a6Sn!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4dcef89d-082c-408a-9f82-cf63a7976245_1405x777.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!a6Sn!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4dcef89d-082c-408a-9f82-cf63a7976245_1405x777.png)

This was not a one-off. The IEA projects that global electricity demand will continue accelerating through the end of the decade, driven by a convergence of structural forces that are not cyclical and are not going away.

Force number one: electrification. Every internal combustion engine that becomes an EV is a new mouth at the electricity table. Every gas furnace replaced by a heat pump. Every industrial process that moves from fossil fuels to electric power. The world has decided, for better or worse, that the path to decarbonization runs through the electrical grid. This is probably correct. But nobody seems to have done the math on what happens when you try to electrify everything while simultaneously retiring the power plants that currently keep the lights on.

Force number two: air conditioning. This one is underappreciated. There are roughly 2.8 billion people living in tropical and subtropical regions who do not yet have air conditioning. As incomes rise in India, Southeast Asia, and Sub-Saharan Africa, the demand for cooling will be staggering. The IEA estimates that global cooling demand could triple by 2050. Air conditioning is not optional in a 45-degree heatwave. It is a survival technology. And it is extraordinarily power-hungry.

Force number three, and this is the one that broke the dam: artificial intelligence.

Data center electricity consumption is projected to more than double to approximately 945 TWh by 2030. That is roughly equivalent to the entire electricity consumption of Japan. AI-optimized servers alone are growing at roughly 30% per year in power consumption. In the United States, data centers are on track to account for nearly half of all electricity demand growth between now and 2030. Read that again. Half. The US economy will consume more electricity for processing data by the end of the decade than for manufacturing aluminum, steel, cement, and chemicals combined.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!TGWe!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe4cb49bc-62f1-4e6c-aeb1-cf8ed230df58_1405x778.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!TGWe!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe4cb49bc-62f1-4e6c-aeb1-cf8ed230df58_1405x778.png)

This is not a forecast from some breathless AI startup trying to raise a Series B. This is the IEA’s base case projection, published in April 2025.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!KmkM!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc7220a87-6e29-464a-b226-68253388cdd3_1405x780.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!KmkM!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc7220a87-6e29-464a-b226-68253388cdd3_1405x780.png)

And here is the problem: you cannot power a data center with hopes and vibes. You cannot power it intermittently. When Microsoft or Google or Amazon builds a 500MW hyperscale campus, it needs 500MW of power available 24 hours a day, 7 days a week, 365 days a year. Not “500MW when the wind blows” or “500MW between 10am and 4pm on a sunny day.” Five hundred megawatts, all the time, no exceptions, with contractual penalties if the power goes out.

This brings us to the core physics problem that the entire energy transition has been trying very hard to ignore.

### **1.3 The Capacity Factor Problem, or Why Solar Panels Don’t Work at Night**

There is a concept in the power industry called “capacity factor.” It is the single most important number for understanding why the energy mix looks the way it does, and it is the number that renewable energy advocates most desperately wish did not exist.

Capacity factor is the ratio of actual electricity generated over a given period to the maximum possible generation if the plant ran at full output 100% of the time. It tells you how often a power source actually shows up for work.

Here are the 2024 numbers for the United States:

Nuclear: ~92%, Natural gas (combined cycle): ~57-61% (varying by year; 2024 EIA data showed ~60.5%), Coal: ~43%, Wind: ~34%, Solar: ~23%

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!AZyy!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fec903df9-02f1-4fe5-b2ce-264e5572564d_1405x751.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!AZyy!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fec903df9-02f1-4fe5-b2ce-264e5572564d_1405x751.png)

Read those numbers carefully. A nuclear plant runs at full tilt more than 90% of the time. It only stops for scheduled refueling every 18 to 24 months, and even those outages are getting shorter. The US fleet hit 92% capacity factor in 2024 and has not dropped below 90% in any year since 2012. That is not a theoretical number. That is operational performance across 94 reactors, many of which are 40+ years old.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!T9y_!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fb5a985f9-e61a-4ce9-b1d3-017ac18d429e_1405x705.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!T9y_!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fb5a985f9-e61a-4ce9-b1d3-017ac18d429e_1405x705.png)

Solar, meanwhile, manages 23%. Not because the panels are broken. Not because the maintenance crew is lazy. Because the sun goes down. Every single night. And it is dim in the morning. And it is dim in the evening. And clouds exist. And winter exists. That 23% is a hard physical constraint dictated by the rotation of the Earth and the tilt of its axis. No amount of engineering innovation changes the fact that Ohio in January receives roughly one-third the solar irradiance of Arizona in July.

Wind does better at 34%, but with a different problem: unpredictability. The sun at least has the decency to follow a schedule. Wind does whatever it wants. A wind drought, which is exactly what it sounds like, can last days or even weeks. In January 2024, large parts of Europe experienced a multi-day period of low wind coinciding with a cold snap, which is of course exactly when you need the most electricity. This is called “Dunkelflaute” in German, literally “dark doldrums,” and it is not a theoretical risk. It is a recurring meteorological reality.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!yCXl!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F6a8e18c2-278e-4b0b-8965-1aba40619d41_1405x774.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!yCXl!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F6a8e18c2-278e-4b0b-8965-1aba40619d41_1405x774.png)

Now, the standard rebuttal here is “batteries.” And yes, batteries are improving rapidly and costs are falling. But let us do some basic arithmetic. A Tesla Megapack, one of the most commercially deployed utility-scale batteries, stores about 3.9 MWh of energy. A single 1GW nuclear reactor generates roughly 24,000 MWh per day. To store one day of that reactor’s output in Megapacks, you would need approximately 6,150 of them, covering something like 120 acres of land, at a cost of roughly $7-9 billion at current installed pricing (down from $10-12 billion just two years ago, as battery costs continue to fall, though this only reinforces how enormous the storage challenge remains). And that gives you one day of backup. Dunkelflaute can last a week.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Sb--!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F8e4756a8-c415-46f4-812b-11ff3f2ac1c0_1405x795.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Sb--!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F8e4756a8-c415-46f4-812b-11ff3f2ac1c0_1405x795.png)

The math simply does not work for grid-scale seasonal storage. Not today, not with any battery chemistry currently in production, not with any chemistry on the near-term horizon. Batteries are excellent for short-duration storage (4-8 hours, maybe 12). They are a rounding error on the scale of multi-day or seasonal storage.

So if you need a power source that runs all the time, produces no carbon emissions, fits on a relatively small footprint of land, and can be located close to demand centers, your list of options is extremely short.

You have hydropower, which is excellent but geographically constrained. You cannot build a dam where there is no river, and most of the best sites are already taken.

You have geothermal, which is promising but even more geographically constrained, and is decades away from being available at scale outside of Iceland, parts of East Africa, and the western US.

And you have nuclear.

That is it. That is the list.

#### **1.3.1 How Nuclear Fits Into Grids**

A necessary footnote on how nuclear fits into grids with growing renewable penetration: nuclear’s traditional role as “baseload” (running flat-out, 24/7, never adjusting output) is increasingly awkward in electricity systems where solar and wind provide cheap but variable generation.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!co-m!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5052a2c5-095a-4eb2-8aac-3b1fd30fdd98_1405x791.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!co-m!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5052a2c5-095a-4eb2-8aac-3b1fd30fdd98_1405x791.png)

In markets with high renewable penetration (California, parts of Germany, South Australia), midday electricity prices can go negative when solar output peaks. A nuclear plant running at full power into a negative-price market is losing money on every MWh it generates. France has already begun operating some of its reactors in “load-following” mode, ramping output up and down to complement variable renewables, but this reduces capacity factor (and therefore revenue) and introduces thermal cycling that accelerates wear on reactor components.

The IAEA and the US National Renewable Energy Laboratory (NREL) are studying “nuclear-renewable hybrid systems” that pair reactors with industrial heat applications, hydrogen production, or desalination during periods of low electricity prices, allowing the reactor to run at full thermal power but redirect the heat to non-electric uses. These are promising concepts but largely unproven at commercial scale.

For investors, the grid integration question matters because the 92% capacity factor that makes nuclear’s economics so compelling was achieved in a world where nuclear ran as baseload against coal and gas. In a future grid where solar provides the cheapest daytime MWh and wind provides cheap-but-variable nighttime MWh, nuclear’s capacity factor could decline not because the reactors stop working but because the economics of running them at full power in every hour deteriorate. This is a slow-moving risk, most relevant on a 15-30 year horizon, but it should be part of any long-duration nuclear investment thesis.

The counter-argument: data centers and industrial electrification are creating a new category of 24/7 demand that is perfectly matched to nuclear’s flat output profile. A data center does not care about the duck curve. It wants the same 500 MW at noon as it does at midnight. This demand profile effectively resurrects the baseload model that high-renewable grids were eroding, and it is the primary reason tech companies are signing nuclear PPAs rather than renewable PPAs. The grid integration risk is real for merchant nuclear plants selling into wholesale markets. It is far less relevant for contracted nuclear plants selling behind-the-meter or under long-term PPAs to industrial offtakers. Structure your portfolio accordingly.

### **1.4 The Energy Density Cheat Code**

Before we go further, it is worth pausing on the physics, because the physics is the reason nuclear exists at all, and it is the reason nuclear will always have a structural advantage over every chemical fuel source on Earth.

Chemical energy, the kind you get from burning coal or gas or oil, comes from rearranging electrons. When carbon combusts with oxygen, electrons shift between atomic orbitals, and the energy difference is released as heat. This is the same basic process whether you are burning wood, gasoline, or natural gas. The energies involved are on the order of a few electron volts per reaction.

Nuclear energy comes from rearranging the nucleus itself. When a uranium-235 atom absorbs a neutron and fissions into two lighter atoms, the energy released is roughly 200 million electron volts per reaction. Not a few. Two hundred million.

This is not a small difference. It is a difference of roughly eight orders of magnitude. It means that a single uranium fuel pellet, a little ceramic cylinder about the size of the tip of your pinky finger, weighing around 10 grams, contains as much energy as one ton of coal, or 149 gallons of oil, or 17,000 cubic feet of natural gas.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!p1x1!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fbbf31046-7d2b-40bd-8919-b0ebb272b8ab_1405x784.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!p1x1!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fbbf31046-7d2b-40bd-8919-b0ebb272b8ab_1405x784.png)

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!DuyO!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F1c1ae8ab-f0f4-4c8c-904e-134e6a534921_1405x781.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!DuyO!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F1c1ae8ab-f0f4-4c8c-904e-134e6a534921_1405x781.png)

Let me state that differently, because people tend to glaze over large numbers. Ten grams of uranium. That is lighter than two nickels stacked together. It replaces a ton of coal. A literal ton. Two thousand pounds. Enough coal to fill a large bathtub. You could hold the uranium pellet between your thumb and forefinger. You would need a pickup truck for the coal.

This energy density gap is not something that can be engineered away. It is baked into the fundamental physics of the strong nuclear force versus electromagnetic force. No improvement in solar panel efficiency, no breakthrough in battery chemistry, no advance in wind turbine design will ever close this gap, because they are all operating in the electromagnetic regime. Nuclear operates in a different regime entirely, and the energy densities are separated by a factor of millions.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!3QBD!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F9b9585dc-195c-4201-b36b-478ba28cad52_1405x794.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!3QBD!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F9b9585dc-195c-4201-b36b-478ba28cad52_1405x794.png)

This is why a single nuclear power plant can sit on a few hundred acres and produce as much electricity as a solar farm covering 50+ square miles, or a wind farm stretching across hundreds of square miles. This is why France, a country the size of Texas, generates about 65-70% of its electricity from nuclear with 56 operating reactors occupying a negligible fraction of its land area, while Germany, which chose renewables, has covered its landscape in turbines and panels and still burns more coal than any other country in Western Europe.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Jqve!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fa0ec94e8-7b0c-46bc-97a4-3e96e59ace32_1405x788.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Jqve!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fa0ec94e8-7b0c-46bc-97a4-3e96e59ace32_1405x788.png)

Land is not free. Land has opportunity costs. Land near cities, where the power is actually needed, is especially expensive. The energy density of nuclear is not just a fun physics fact. It is a hard economic advantage that compounds every year as land and permitting costs rise.

### **1.5 The Vibe Shift**

So we have a structural demand problem that is large, accelerating, and concentrated in the exact kind of loads (data centers, industrial electrification) that require 24/7 baseload power. We have a supply problem because the two dominant clean energy sources are intermittent and cannot economically provide storage at the scale needed. And we have a fuel source with energy density advantages that are literally millions of times better than the alternatives.

You might think the conclusion here is obvious. You would be right. And yet it took the world 15 years of dithering after Fukushima to arrive at it.

The turning point came not from environmentalists or engineers or policy wonks. It came from the tech industry. When you have Microsoft signing a 20-year power purchase agreement to restart Three Mile Island Unit 1 (yes, that Three Mile Island), when Amazon is buying a nuclear-powered data center campus from Talen Energy, when Google is signing deals with Kairos Power for small modular reactors, you know the vibe has shifted. These are not speculative bets by junior analysts. These are multi-billion dollar commitments by companies whose core business depends on having reliable, scalable, carbon-free power.

At the policy level, the shift has been equally dramatic. Twenty-two countries signed a pledge at COP28 in December 2023 to triple nuclear energy capacity by 2050, a number that has since grown to over 30 (by COP29 in November 2024) and 38 by the March 2025 Nuclear Energy Summit.

The IAEA has raised its nuclear capacity projections for five consecutive years. In the US, President Trump signed executive orders in May 2025 to accelerate nuclear adoption, targeting an expansion from approximately 100 GW today to 400 GW by 2050. In the high-case IAEA projection, global nuclear capacity could reach 992 GW by 2050, roughly 2.6 times the 2024 level.

Goldman Sachs projects that global nuclear generating capacity will rise from 378 GW to 575 GW by 2040, increasing nuclear’s share of the global electricity mix from roughly 9% to 12%. The total fleet of around 440 operable reactors today (approximately 416 actively generating) is set to expand to roughly 500 by 2030, with over 400 additional reactors in the planning or proposal stage.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!bEid!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F79b40817-9709-40e1-bba8-cb41266f92b5_1405x788.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!bEid!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F79b40817-9709-40e1-bba8-cb41266f92b5_1405x788.png)

The nuclear fleet set a generation record in 2024, producing 2,667 TWh of electricity, surpassing the previous record of 2,660 TWh set in 2006. But intellectual honesty demands a caveat: outside China, global nuclear generation in 2024 remained roughly 360 TWh below its 2006 level, a nearly 14% decline. The record is real, but it is substantially a Chinese story. The comeback is underway in the data, but it is geographically uneven, and the narrative is still catching up.

And this is where the story gets interesting for investors. Because while the demand case for nuclear is becoming consensus, the supply chain required to actually deliver on this demand is riddled with bottlenecks, geopolitical dependencies, and structural underinvestment. The uranium market. The enrichment bottleneck. The conversion duopoly. The construction workforce that evaporated after Fukushima. The regulatory apparatus that takes a decade to approve what China builds in five years.

This primer exists because the gap between “nuclear is coming back” (which is the easy part) and “here is exactly how the nuclear fuel cycle works, where the bottlenecks are, what the investable universe looks like, and what the bear cases are” (which is the hard part) is approximately the size of the Grand Canyon. And most of what has been written to fill that gap is either too technical for generalists, too shallow for professionals, or too ideological to be useful for either.

We are going to fix that.

What follows is a first-principles walkthrough of nuclear power, from the physics of fission to the structure of the uranium market to the investable equities, written for the kind of person who wants to actually understand what they own and why they own it. We assume you are smart but not yet an expert. By the time you finish, you will be dangerous.

## **Part 2: How Nuclear Power Actually Works**

### **2.1 Splitting the Unsplittable**

The ancient Greeks called the atom “atomos,” meaning “uncuttable.” They were wrong. Spectacularly, violently, world-changingly wrong.

In December 1938, two German chemists, Otto Hahn and Fritz Strassmann, fired neutrons at uranium and found barium in the debris. Barium has no business being near uranium. It is a completely different element, roughly half the atomic weight. Their colleague Lise Meitner, who had fled Nazi Germany months earlier because she was Jewish, figured out what had happened: the neutron had split the uranium nucleus in two. She and her nephew Otto Frisch calculated the energy released using Einstein’s E=mc², and the number they got was enormous. Orders of magnitude larger than any chemical reaction. They called it “fission,” borrowing the term from biology, where it describes a cell dividing in two.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!i28t!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F3e724b0a-18f4-4861-b26b-6aa8781377dc_1405x782.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!i28t!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F3e724b0a-18f4-4861-b26b-6aa8781377dc_1405x782.png)

Within seven years, fission had ended a world war. Within fifteen, it was generating electricity. And within seventy, it was powering roughly 10% of the world’s grid while most people still could not tell you what it actually is.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!B9-d!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F58b3d83a-cf08-434b-bc58-bf116bcab52c_1405x782.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!B9-d!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F58b3d83a-cf08-434b-bc58-bf116bcab52c_1405x782.png)

So let us fix that.

### **2.2 The Bowling Ball and the Billiard Table**

Here is the simplest possible explanation of nuclear fission, and I promise it is not dumbed down. It is just clear.

Imagine a bowling ball sitting on a billiard table. The bowling ball represents a uranium-235 nucleus. It is big. It is heavy. It has 92 protons and 143 neutrons crammed together in a space so small that if you scaled the atom up to the size of a football stadium, the nucleus would be a marble at the 50-yard line. All of the atom’s mass is in that marble. Everything else is empty space and electron clouds.

Now imagine you roll a cue ball at the bowling ball. The cue ball is a neutron. When it hits the bowling ball (gets absorbed by the U-235 nucleus), something extraordinary happens. The bowling ball does not just crack. It *vibrates*. The nucleus, which is held together by the strong nuclear force (the most powerful force in the universe at short range), becomes momentarily unstable. It elongates, like a water droplet about to split. And then it does split, violently, into two smaller nuclei (called “fission fragments”), plus two or three free neutrons, plus a burst of energy.

That burst of energy is the entire point. It is roughly 200 million electron volts per fission event, or about 200 MeV if you want to sound like you know what you are talking about at dinner. For comparison, the combustion of a single carbon atom with oxygen releases about 4 eV. Nuclear fission releases about 50 million times more energy per event than chemical combustion.

But here is the thing that makes fission uniquely powerful, and uniquely dangerous: those two or three free neutrons that fly out of the split nucleus? They can go hit other U-235 nuclei. And those nuclei split, releasing more neutrons. Which hit more nuclei. Which split, releasing more neutrons.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Mkt_!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe4d2b3d8-0d31-46b5-81a3-0a68cf24e3d9_1405x784.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Mkt_!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe4d2b3d8-0d31-46b5-81a3-0a68cf24e3d9_1405x784.png)

This is a chain reaction.

In a bomb, the chain reaction is uncontrolled. Each generation of fissions happens in nanoseconds, and the number of fissions doubles (or triples) with each generation. Within microseconds, you have a trillion trillion fissions happening simultaneously, and the resulting energy release is measured in kilotons of TNT equivalent. This is what happened over Hiroshima.

In a reactor, the chain reaction is controlled. The goal is to maintain what physicists call “criticality,” which means exactly one neutron from each fission event goes on to cause another fission event. Not two (which would cause the reaction to accelerate exponentially). Not zero (which would cause the reaction to die). Exactly one. This produces a steady, continuous release of heat, which is what we use to generate electricity.

The mechanism for control is beautifully simple: control rods. These are rods made of neutron-absorbing materials (boron, hafnium, cadmium, or silver-indium-cadmium alloys) that are inserted into the reactor core. Push them in further, they absorb more neutrons, the reaction slows down. Pull them out, more neutrons are available to cause fissions, the reaction speeds up. Want to shut the reactor down entirely? Drop all the rods in. Gravity does the work. In a modern pressurized water reactor, the control rods are held up by electromagnets. Lose electrical power, the magnets de-energize, and the rods fall into the core by their own weight. The reactor shuts itself down. This is called a “scram,” and it happens in seconds.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!dyw-!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F34c6a1ca-5ca4-42be-a4ed-f707da9b19d1_1405x757.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!dyw-!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F34c6a1ca-5ca4-42be-a4ed-f707da9b19d1_1405x757.png)

There is an elegant philosophical point buried in this engineering. A nuclear reactor is not a machine you “turn on.” It is a machine you prevent from shutting itself down. Left to its own devices, with the control rods dropped, it shuts down. Even without the rods, modern light water reactors are designed with negative temperature and void coefficients, meaning the physics actively resists power excursions: if the fuel heats up, the fission rate drops; if the coolant boils away, the chain reaction loses its moderator and dies.

You have to actively, deliberately maintain the precise conditions under which the chain reaction sustains itself. The default state of a well-designed reactor is “off.” The default state of a coal fire is “on until you run out of coal.” This distinction matters more than most people realize, and it is the reason why the catastrophic positive-feedback failure at Chernobyl was a design-specific pathology, not a feature of nuclear reactors in general.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Ita2!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe7a74654-f5f0-4928-9cdb-88f5fe34a677_1405x690.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Ita2!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe7a74654-f5f0-4928-9cdb-88f5fe34a677_1405x690.png)

### **2.3 Why U-235, and the 0.7% Problem**

If fission is so powerful, why aren’t we fissioning everything? Why uranium specifically?

The answer is that not all atomic nuclei are willing to be split. Most are not. The strong nuclear force holds the nucleus together with extraordinary tenacity, and for most elements, a free neutron bouncing into the nucleus just gets absorbed without causing fission. The nucleus gets a little heavier, maybe emits some gamma radiation, and carries on with its life.

But a handful of very heavy, very specific isotopes have nuclei that are right on the edge of stability. They are so large, so packed with protons and neutrons, that the electrostatic repulsion between all those protons (positive charges repel each other) nearly overwhelms the strong force holding the nucleus together. These nuclei are primed. One extra neutron is all it takes to push them over the edge.

The isotope that matters most for commercial nuclear power is uranium-235. Why this one specifically? Two reasons.

First, U-235 is “fissile,” meaning it can be split by a slow-moving (thermal) neutron. This is crucial. Fast neutrons are hard to work with. They zip through material and are difficult to capture. Slow neutrons, the ones that have been decelerated by bouncing around in water or graphite (a process called “moderation,” which we will get to shortly), are much more likely to be absorbed by a U-235 nucleus and cause fission. This makes U-235 the practical workhorse of nuclear energy.

Second, U-235 exists in nature. You can dig it out of the ground. This sounds obvious, but it is not trivial. Other fissile materials, like plutonium-239 or uranium-233, must be *manufactured* in a reactor. They do not occur naturally in usable quantities. U-235 is the only fissile material you can mine.

Now here is the catch, and it is a big one.

Natural uranium is 99.3% uranium-238 and only 0.7% uranium-235.

Let that sink in. Of all the uranium you dig out of the ground, process, mill, and refine, only 7 atoms out of every 1,000 are the ones you actually want. The other 993 are U-238, which is *not* fissile. U-238 is “fertile,” meaning it can absorb a fast neutron and eventually transmute into plutonium-239 (which is fissile), but it will not sustain a chain reaction on its own.

This 0.7% problem is the reason the entire enrichment industry exists. It is the reason centrifuges are classified technology. It is the reason Iran’s nuclear program makes geopolitical headlines. It is the reason the nuclear fuel cycle is orders of magnitude more complex than “dig up coal, burn coal.” Everything downstream in the nuclear supply chain, conversion, enrichment, fabrication, exists because nature gave us uranium that is 99.3% wrong and 0.7% right, and we have to fix that ratio before we can use it.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!aYHU!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fad28d3d4-dfd8-402a-a08c-e92f39e8a92b_1405x779.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!aYHU!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fad28d3d4-dfd8-402a-a08c-e92f39e8a92b_1405x779.png)

Most commercial reactors need uranium enriched to between 3% and 5% U-235. This sounds modest until you consider the physics involved in separating two isotopes that are chemically identical and differ in mass by only three neutrons. You cannot do it with chemistry. You have to exploit that tiny mass difference mechanically, and the technology for doing so (gas centrifuges, spinning uranium hexafluoride gas at tens of thousands of RPM to separate the slightly lighter U-235 from the slightly heavier U-238) is one of the most technically demanding and strategically sensitive industrial processes on Earth.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!6Co_!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4332ddf1-f51b-4ff9-a034-10ac0f9ba040_1405x657.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!6Co_!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4332ddf1-f51b-4ff9-a034-10ac0f9ba040_1405x657.png)

We will cover enrichment in exhaustive detail in Part 3. For now, just understand this: the 0.7% figure is the first constraint in the entire nuclear value chain. Everything else flows from it.

### **2.4 From Fission to Electricity: The World’s Most Complicated Way to Boil Water**

Here is the single most disappointing fact in all of energy technology:

A nuclear power plant generates electricity by boiling water.

That is it. That is the whole trick.

You use nuclear fission to generate heat. The heat boils water. The steam turns a turbine. The turbine drives a generator. The generator produces electricity. This is fundamentally identical to how a coal plant works, or a natural gas plant, or a concentrated solar thermal plant, or a geothermal plant. They all boil water. The only thing that changes is the heat source.

The reason this is disappointing is that fission releases energy at scales that could, in theory, do far more exotic things. Two hundred million electron volts per fission event, and we use it to make steam.

It is like using a Ferrari to deliver pizzas.

Technically, it works. Aesthetically, it is an affront to the engineering gods. A physicist’s footnote: this is a deliberate simplification. Some Gen IV designs abandon the steam cycle entirely. High-temperature gas-cooled reactors can drive a Brayton gas turbine cycle at higher efficiencies.

Molten salt designs can supply industrial process heat directly. And fission’s real untapped potential lies not in electricity generation but in high-temperature applications (hydrogen production, desalination, synthetic fuel synthesis) where the 600-900°C heat from advanced reactors becomes an industrial feedstock rather than a source of steam. The existing fleet boils water. The next generation may not.

But thermodynamics is thermodynamics. The Rankine cycle (heat water, make steam, spin turbine, condense steam, repeat) is the most proven, most efficient, most scalable method we have for converting heat to electricity.

Nuclear plants typically achieve thermal efficiencies of 32-34% for the existing PWR and BWR fleet, with newer Gen III+ designs like the EPR pushing toward 36-37%. Roughly a third of the heat energy from fission is converted to electricity.

The rest is waste heat, which is why nuclear plants need cooling, either from rivers, oceans, cooling towers, or (in some designs) air.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!tE7P!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F7a756d3f-68cd-4959-b3da-a6956e4725e8_1405x789.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!tE7P!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F7a756d3f-68cd-4959-b3da-a6956e4725e8_1405x789.png)

This is lower than a modern combined-cycle natural gas plant (which can hit 60%+ efficiency by using both a gas turbine and a steam turbine in sequence), but higher than most coal plants. The reason nuclear plants are less thermally efficient than gas plants has nothing to do with the fission itself and everything to do with the water temperature. Nuclear reactors operate at lower steam temperatures (roughly 290-330°C) than gas turbines (which operate at over 1,000°C), because the materials in the reactor have to withstand decades of neutron bombardment and cannot be pushed to extreme temperatures without degradation. Higher temperature = higher efficiency per the Carnot theorem, and nuclear plants just run cooler.

Some Gen IV designs (molten salt reactors, high-temperature gas-cooled reactors) aim to change this by operating at much higher temperatures, potentially pushing nuclear thermal efficiency into the 45-50% range. But those are still in development. The existing fleet, the roughly 440 reactors operable worldwide today (of which approximately 416 are actively generating, with the remainder in long-term outage or suspended operation, principally in Japan), essentially all boil water.

Now, *how* they boil water, that is where the engineering gets interesting.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!ohLI!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F7860eaf5-ca0d-4618-86a7-6969e919d6e8_1405x784.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!ohLI!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F7860eaf5-ca0d-4618-86a7-6969e919d6e8_1405x784.png)

### **2.5 The Reactor Zoo: PWR, BWR, and Why the Plumbing Matters**

There are many types of nuclear reactor in the world, but the vast majority, roughly 85% of the global fleet, fall into two categories, both of which use ordinary water (called “light water” to distinguish it from heavy water, which we will get to) as both the coolant and the “moderator” (the material that slows down the neutrons so they can cause fission).

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!tj7o!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe0267982-4eeb-4f97-87f8-360bc4109b88_1405x780.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!tj7o!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe0267982-4eeb-4f97-87f8-360bc4109b88_1405x780.png)

**The Pressurized Water Reactor (PWR): The World’s Reactor**

The PWR is the dominant reactor design on Earth. About 70% of all operating commercial reactors are PWRs. It was originally developed by the United States Navy in the 1950s for nuclear submarines (the USS Nautilus, the world’s first nuclear-powered submarine, used a PWR), and the design was then scaled up for commercial electricity generation by Westinghouse.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!RqIV!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fbb400c5c-a137-419f-baf9-39d63a904968_1405x780.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!RqIV!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fbb400c5c-a137-419f-baf9-39d63a904968_1405x780.png)

The defining feature of a PWR is that it uses *two* separate water loops, and the water in the reactor core never boils.

Loop one (the primary circuit): Water is pumped through the reactor core at very high pressure, around 155 bar (roughly 2,250 psi, for reference, your car tires are inflated to about 32 psi). This enormous pressure keeps the water liquid even though it is heated to over 320°C, well above water’s normal boiling point of 100°C. The superheated, pressurized water carries the heat from the fission reaction out of the core and into a steam generator.

Loop two (the secondary circuit): Inside the steam generator (essentially a giant heat exchanger), the hot water from the primary circuit transfers its heat to water in a separate, lower-pressure secondary circuit. This secondary water *does* boil, producing steam. The steam goes to the turbine, spins it, generates electricity, then gets condensed back into water and recycled.

The genius of this design is the separation. The water that touches the reactor core (and picks up some radioactivity from passing through a neutron field) never touches the turbine. The turbine sees only clean, non-radioactive steam from the secondary circuit. This means the turbine hall does not need heavy radiation shielding, maintenance workers can access it relatively freely, and if there is a leak in the turbine system, it does not release radioactive water.

The downside is complexity. Two full water circuits means more pumps, more piping, more things that can break, and the massive steam generators (which can be 20 meters tall and weigh 800 tons each, with thousands of tiny tubes inside) are among the most expensive and maintenance-intensive components in the plant.

France’s entire fleet of 56 operating reactors is PWR-based (from a historical build of 58, with Fessenheim 1 and 2 permanently shut in 2020). So are most of the US fleet. The AP1000, the EPR, China’s Hualong One, South Korea’s APR-1400, Russia’s VVER, these are all PWR variants. If you own a nuclear utility stock, you almost certainly own PWRs.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!b65v!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F8de1032e-8474-434a-8c45-d91eedbcd969_1405x783.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!b65v!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F8de1032e-8474-434a-8c45-d91eedbcd969_1405x783.png)

**The Boiling Water Reactor (BWR): The Simpler Cousin**

BWRs make up roughly 13-14% of the global fleet (down from 15% a few years ago, primarily due to permanent shutdowns of older Japanese BWR units post-Fukushima).

They were developed by General Electric (now GE Vernova Hitachi Nuclear Energy) at Argonne National Laboratory in the 1950s, and their design philosophy is: “Why bother with two loops when one will do?”

In a BWR, the water in the reactor core boils directly. The steam goes straight to the turbine. One loop. No steam generator. Simpler plumbing. Lower operating pressure (about 70 bar versus 155 bar for a PWR). In principle, cheaper and simpler to build.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!xg93!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc8058c2e-58d9-4ea5-98e6-737105fb16dc_1405x778.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!xg93!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc8058c2e-58d9-4ea5-98e6-737105fb16dc_1405x778.png)

The trade-off: the steam that drives the turbine has passed directly through the reactor core, picking up trace radioactivity (primarily nitrogen-16, half-life of about 7 seconds). The turbine hall needs radiation shielding, and maintenance workers need protocols for working around mildly activated components. In practice, this is manageable (radiation levels drop quickly once the reactor shuts down), but it adds operational complexity the PWR avoids.

BWRs are found primarily in the United States, Japan, Sweden, and Taiwan. Fukushima Daiichi was a BWR facility.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!gmDm!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F25770dc8-1a63-4ffc-a918-cd1ba8f39f54_1405x732.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!gmDm!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F25770dc8-1a63-4ffc-a918-cd1ba8f39f54_1405x732.png)

**A Quick Note on the Moderator, Because This Matters**

I mentioned “moderation” earlier, and it is one of those concepts that sounds simple but contains a key insight that most nuclear primers gloss over.

When a U-235 atom fissions, the neutrons that fly out are moving extremely fast, roughly 20,000 km/s, or about 7% of the speed of light. These are “fast neutrons.” And here is the counterintuitive part: fast neutrons are actually *bad* at causing fission in U-235. They zip right through other uranium nuclei without interacting. It is like throwing a bullet through a crowd; it goes too fast to shake hands.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!VsYl!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F888f8957-6c38-4528-9bd5-77096e33a380_1405x788.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!VsYl!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F888f8957-6c38-4528-9bd5-77096e33a380_1405x788.png)

To maximize the probability of a neutron being absorbed by another U-235 nucleus and causing fission, you need to slow it down. Way down. To “thermal” energies, where the neutron is moving at roughly the same speed as the molecules of the surrounding material (about 2 km/s, roughly the speed of a rifle bullet, versus the 20,000 km/s it started at).

You slow neutrons down by bouncing them off light nuclei. Think of it like pool: if a cue ball (the neutron) hits a bowling ball (a heavy nucleus), it bounces off with almost no loss of energy. But if it hits another cue ball (a nucleus of similar mass), it transfers a lot of energy in the collision. Hydrogen, the lightest element, is the best moderator. One good collision with a hydrogen nucleus transfers about half the neutron’s energy. A few dozen collisions, and the neutron is at thermal energies.

And what is full of hydrogen? Water. H₂O. Two hydrogen atoms per molecule. This is why light water reactors use water as both a coolant (to carry heat away from the core) and a moderator (to slow neutrons down so they can sustain the chain reaction). It is a beautifully efficient dual-purpose design.

It also contains a built-in safety feature that most people do not appreciate. If the water in a light water reactor boils away or leaks out, you lose your moderator. Without a moderator, the fast neutrons cannot sustain the chain reaction. The reactor shuts itself down. This is called a “negative void coefficient,” and it means that the most common failure mode (loss of coolant) inherently stops the nuclear reaction.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!-q9B!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd821eb41-49fe-49ea-bf8e-d2932f188dcb_1405x786.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!-q9B!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd821eb41-49fe-49ea-bf8e-d2932f188dcb_1405x786.png)

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!l2eB!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F074527eb-7af8-4dfe-a2a6-4da76c5dac1d_1405x782.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!l2eB!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F074527eb-7af8-4dfe-a2a6-4da76c5dac1d_1405x782.png)

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!sGjG!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd35a8a65-4d9e-4f58-be6c-27ea19ea6172_1405x782.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!sGjG!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd35a8a65-4d9e-4f58-be6c-27ea19ea6172_1405x782.png)

This is exactly the opposite of what happened at Chernobyl, and we need to talk about why.

### **2.6 The Outliers: CANDU and RBMK**

Not every reactor uses light water. Two important designs use different moderators, and understanding them matters both for completeness and for understanding why Chernobyl happened.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!cSgV!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F03f603f2-5de3-4594-a370-990d3575feb9_1405x759.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!cSgV!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F03f603f2-5de3-4594-a370-990d3575feb9_1405x759.png)

**The CANDU (Canada Deuterium Uranium)**

Canada went a different route. Rather than enriching uranium (which Canada did not have the industrial capacity for in the 1950s), Canadian engineers designed a reactor that could use *natural* uranium, the stuff that comes straight out of the ground at 0.7% U-235, with no enrichment required.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!v0S8!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff626194a-1657-4026-a4e3-27047ef879cd_1405x645.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!v0S8!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff626194a-1657-4026-a4e3-27047ef879cd_1405x645.png)

The trick is the moderator. Light water absorbs too many neutrons, which means if you use natural uranium fuel (with its low U-235 content), there are not enough neutrons left over to sustain the chain reaction. Heavy water (deuterium oxide, D₂O, where the hydrogen atoms are replaced by deuterium, hydrogen with an extra neutron) absorbs far fewer neutrons, leaving enough to sustain fission even in natural uranium.

The CANDU reactor uses heavy water as both moderator and coolant. The fuel bundles are arranged horizontally in pressure tubes running through a large tank (the “calandria”) filled with heavy water. This design allows the reactor to be refueled without shutting down (you push new fuel bundles in one end and the spent ones come out the other), which is a significant operational advantage.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!twPO!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F11ad63de-f0a1-43e0-8307-c6939259ad89_2117x1188.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!twPO!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F11ad63de-f0a1-43e0-8307-c6939259ad89_2117x1188.png)

The downside: heavy water is expensive to produce (roughly $300-600 per kilogram), and you need hundreds of tons of it. The CANDU reactor makes up about 11% of the global fleet, operating primarily in Canada, India, Argentina, Romania, Pakistan, and China.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!yX7G!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F0d2b1727-f282-465a-9b84-161a17138fbe_1405x654.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!yX7G!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F0d2b1727-f282-465a-9b84-161a17138fbe_1405x654.png)

For investors, the CANDU matters because it represents a different fuel demand profile. CANDU reactors do not need enriched uranium, which means they do not contribute to enrichment demand (the bottleneck we will discuss later). But they do consume natural uranium in larger quantities per unit of energy produced.

**The RBMK: Why Chernobyl Was a Design Problem, Not a Nuclear Problem**

The RBMK (Reaktor Bolshoy Moshchnosti Kanalny, “High-Power Channel-type Reactor”) was a Soviet design, and understanding it matters because it explains why Chernobyl happened, and why Chernobyl could *not* happen in a Western reactor.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!CIE_!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4cde7405-e257-4a47-a92f-afac8cff1412_1405x663.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!CIE_!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4cde7405-e257-4a47-a92f-afac8cff1412_1405x663.png)

The RBMK used graphite as a moderator and light water as a coolant. This is the critical distinction. In a light water reactor (PWR or BWR), the water serves as both moderator and coolant. Lose the water, you lose both cooling and moderation, and the chain reaction stops. In an RBMK, the graphite moderator is separate from the water coolant. Lose the water, and you lose the coolant, but the graphite moderator is still there, still slowing down neutrons, still sustaining the chain reaction.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!PCaE!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff937b126-7da4-4702-93d2-b27d2d12afc1_2124x1185.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!PCaE!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff937b126-7da4-4702-93d2-b27d2d12afc1_2124x1185.png)

Worse, the RBMK had a large positive void coefficient of reactivity (approximately +4.7 beta, where beta is the delayed neutron fraction, the threshold above which a reactor becomes “prompt critical” and power rises on a timescale of microseconds rather than seconds). This positive void coefficient was always present, not just at certain power levels. At normal operating power, a separate effect, the negative Doppler fuel temperature coefficient (which reduces reactivity as fuel heats up), was strong enough to dominate, making the overall power coefficient negative. The reactor was manageable, if not safe, in its normal operating envelope. But below roughly 20% of rated power, where the Doppler effect weakened, the positive void coefficient overwhelmed all negative feedbacks.

The reactor became inherently unstable: any increase in void formation (steam bubbles in the coolant channels) increased reactivity, which increased power, which increased boiling, which created more voids. When the water coolant boiled (forming steam voids), it *increased* the reactor’s reactivity further.

The reactor got hotter, which caused more boiling, which caused more voids, which increased reactivity further. This is a positive feedback loop. In engineering, positive feedback loops are how you get explosions.

On April 26, 1986, the operators at Chernobyl Unit 4 were conducting a safety test at low power (which is ironically when the positive void coefficient was most dangerous), and they had disabled several safety systems to conduct the test. When the reactor experienced a sudden, massive power surge, the positive void coefficient amplified it exponentially. the reactor went from near-shutdown to an estimated 300,000 MW or more of thermal power (roughly 100 times its rated maximum) in less than a second. The last instrument reading before the sensors were destroyed registered approximately 33,000 MW, but computational models of the excursion indicate the actual peak was far higher.

The resulting steam explosion blew the 1,000-ton reactor lid off like a bottle cap, and the exposed graphite moderator caught fire, burning for 10 days and sending radioactive material into the atmosphere.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!AQ0m!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F444f6907-0ac1-4045-94bd-78b204ad23cc_1405x797.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!AQ0m!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F444f6907-0ac1-4045-94bd-78b204ad23cc_1405x797.png)

The point of this story is not to scare you. The point is that the RBMK design had known, fundamental flaws that do not exist in any Western reactor design, and the remaining RBMK reactors (which operate solely in Russia and now number approximately 10 units, making up roughly 2.3% of the global fleet following recent retirements including Kursk 2 in January 2024) have since been modified to eliminate the positive void coefficient at low power.

No PWR, no BWR, no CANDU, no Gen III+ reactor has the physics that enabled Chernobyl. It is not a question of better operators or better procedures. It is a question of fundamental reactor physics. The accident mechanism does not exist in these designs. Comparing Chernobyl to modern nuclear is like comparing the Hindenburg to modern aviation. Same word (”air travel”), completely different engineering reality.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!oCbP!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fda338842-aae2-4ee1-80d6-fa3f7265cd3f_1405x535.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!oCbP!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fda338842-aae2-4ee1-80d6-fa3f7265cd3f_1405x535.png)

### **2.7 Gen III+: The New Builds**

The reactors operating today are mostly Gen II designs, built between the 1970s and 1990s. They work. They work extremely well, in fact (92%+ capacity factors, 40+ year lifetimes, declining maintenance costs). But when you hear about “new nuclear,” you are hearing about Gen III+ designs, which incorporate passive safety features and evolutionary improvements over the existing fleet.

There are two Gen III+ designs that dominate the current new-build conversation, and both have troubled histories.

**The AP1000 (Westinghouse/Toshiba)**

The AP1000 (”Advanced Passive 1000”) is a 1,100 MWe pressurized water reactor designed by Westinghouse. Its core innovation is passive safety: in an emergency, the reactor can cool itself for 72 hours without any operator action, without any AC power, and without any external water supply, using gravity-fed water tanks and natural convection. This is a direct response to Fukushima, where the failure was not the reactor physics but the loss of active cooling systems (the diesel backup generators that were flooded by the tsunami).

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!a_Bi!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F675044b6-f6de-41c7-94cb-61bd2247dc82_1405x771.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!a_Bi!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F675044b6-f6de-41c7-94cb-61bd2247dc82_1405x771.png)

The problem with the AP1000 is not the design. It is the construction. The US built two AP1000 units at Plant Vogtle in Georgia. They were 7 years late and roughly 2.6 times over budget. Westinghouse went bankrupt during construction. A parallel project at V.C. Summer in South Carolina was abandoned entirely with $9 billion in sunk costs. China, meanwhile, built four AP1000s and completed them years faster, because China had a continuous construction workforce, a streamlined regulatory process, and the institutional coherence to learn from one unit and apply the lessons to the next in real time.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!VM76!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fae628802-c48d-4032-99aa-ccf143adf137_1405x782.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!VM76!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fae628802-c48d-4032-99aa-ccf143adf137_1405x782.png)

The AP1000 at Vogtle is now operating beautifully. The reactor is excellent. The construction management was catastrophic. We will dissect exactly why in Part 6, because the gap between “this is a good reactor” and “this reactor got built on time and on budget” is where the entire nuclear renaissance thesis lives or dies.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!HCkF!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F621ba5cd-90a1-456c-a983-c23ec45f312c_1405x783.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!HCkF!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F621ba5cd-90a1-456c-a983-c23ec45f312c_1405x783.png)

**The EPR (European Pressurized Reactor, Framatome/EDF)**

The EPR is a 1,600 MWe beast, the most powerful reactor design in commercial operation. It was designed by France’s Framatome (a subsidiary of EDF and Mitsubishi Heavy Industries) with an emphasis on safety redundancy: four independent safety systems (compared to two in most older designs), a “core catcher” to contain melted fuel in the event of a meltdown, and a double containment structure (a reinforced concrete inner shell plus a steel-lined outer shell designed to withstand the impact of a large aircraft).

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!xO09!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Faefcac50-fc96-4d24-88d1-e61b8ba41b3a_1405x783.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!xO09!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Faefcac50-fc96-4d24-88d1-e61b8ba41b3a_1405x783.png)

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!msMO!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fb0f18606-fe26-48d8-86bb-2ec1ba14e5cc_1405x775.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!msMO!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fb0f18606-fe26-48d8-86bb-2ec1ba14e5cc_1405x775.png)

The construction record is even worse than the AP1000’s.

Olkiluoto 3 in Finland: construction started 2005, originally due 2009, finally entered commercial operation in May 2023. Cost rose from roughly €3 billion (the fixed-price turnkey contract) to an estimated €11 billion all-in (combining TVO’s disclosed investment of €5.5 billion and Areva’s accumulated losses of €5.5 billion, which bankrupted the company and necessitated French government intervention). Eighteen years from first concrete to commercial operation.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!kYgh!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F78017318-4343-4128-9b9d-cd07a32ffd1f_1405x783.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!kYgh!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F78017318-4343-4128-9b9d-cd07a32ffd1f_1405x783.png)

Flamanville 3 in France: construction started 2007, originally due 2012, reached full power in late 2025. Cost rose from €3.3 billion to €13.2 billion on EDF’s books, or €23.7 billion including interest during construction according to the French Court of Auditors. That is four to seven times the original budget.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!0CTJ!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F244f2958-f181-4c6d-be8e-de9ba414f1c0_1405x785.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!0CTJ!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F244f2958-f181-4c6d-be8e-de9ba414f1c0_1405x785.png)

Hinkley Point C in the UK: construction started 2017, originally due 2025, now estimated for 2030 (base case per EDF’s February 2026 financial statement) with a cost of £35 billion in 2015 sterling, which translates to approximately £48.7 billion in 2026 prices (roughly $60+ billion at current exchange rates), up from the original £18 billion estimate. If it slips to 2031, add another £1 billion, bringing the worst case to £36 billion in 2015 prices.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!XH9A!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4e0eb51c-b410-423e-9bad-a70e07080246_1405x780.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!XH9A!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4e0eb51c-b410-423e-9bad-a70e07080246_1405x780.png)

The pattern across every Western new-build project is the same: the reactor works, the construction management does not. The reasons, which are institutional and financial rather than technical, are the subject of Part 6, and they are the single largest risk factor in the “nuclear renaissance” thesis.

A nuclear project ties up $15 to $35 billion for 8 to 15 years before generating a single dollar of revenue. During that entire period, the capital is accruing interest. This is interest during construction (IDC), and it is the mechanism that turns schedule delays into financial catastrophe.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!WpW8!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F438980cb-cc2a-4af8-9a88-07c6147fa9a0_1405x782.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!WpW8!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F438980cb-cc2a-4af8-9a88-07c6147fa9a0_1405x782.png)

At a 7% weighted average cost of capital, a $20 billion project that takes 7 years to build accumulates roughly $5 to $6 billion in IDC before commissioning. If that project slips an additional 5 years, IDC alone adds another $8 to $10 billion, and that compounding interest is itself accruing interest on the new, higher capital base. Vogtle’s final cost of roughly $36.8 billion includes an estimated $8 to $12 billion in IDC. Hinkley Point C’s escalation from £18 billion to roughly £49 billion nominal is substantially an IDC story. Flamanville’s explosion from €3.3 billion to €23.7 billion is an IDC story.

This is why nuclear is structurally different from every other generation technology. A gas plant that overruns by a year adds modest IDC on a $1 billion project with a 3-year build. A nuclear plant that overruns by five years is compounding interest on tens of billions across a decade-plus timeline. The implication for investors: construction schedule is the single most important variable in nuclear project economics. Overnight cost, cost per kilowatt, and LCOE estimates are all downstream of one question: how long does it actually take to build?
