Every 100 basis points on a $10 billion project adds roughly $100 million per year in interest expense during a 5-to-7-year construction period. That compounds into billions over the project lifecycle.

This is also why government-facilitated financing (DOE Loan Programs Office, regulated asset base models, sovereign credit guarantees) is not a nice-to-have but a structural prerequisite for Western nuclear construction. The UK’s Sizewell C learned from Hinkley Point C’s mistakes: it is financed under a Regulated Asset Base (RAB) model that allows construction costs to be recovered through consumer bills during construction, spreading IDC across ratepayers rather than concentrating it on the developer’s balance sheet. This is why Sizewell’s projected cost of £38 billion, roughly 20% cheaper than Hinkley for an identical design, is credible: the RAB model reduces the developer’s cost of capital, which reduces IDC, which breaks the compounding spiral. If Western nuclear is going to be built, the financing will look like RAB or Contract for Difference structures, not merchant market pricing. Investors evaluating new-build economics should be modeling these structures, not LCOE.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!cIoy!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F13572ada-f3d3-4cd0-9de9-4419c8645af5_1405x779.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!cIoy!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F13572ada-f3d3-4cd0-9de9-4419c8645af5_1405x779.png)

China, again, is the exception. It built two EPRs at Taishan and completed them roughly on time. South Korea builds its APR-1400 design reliably and at competitive cost. The problem is not nuclear physics. The problem is Western construction management, workforce continuity, and regulatory predictability. We will return to this theme repeatedly throughout this primer, because it is the single largest risk factor in the “nuclear renaissance” thesis.

### **2.8 From Physics to Logistics**

We covered energy density in Part 1. Here is what it looks like when it hits the supply chain.

A uranium fuel pellet is a small ceramic cylinder of uranium dioxide (UO₂). It is about half an inch tall and half an inch in diameter. It weighs roughly 10 grams. It looks like a dark gray pencil eraser. We covered its energy content in Part 1, the “ten grams replaces a ton of coal” comparison. Here is what that energy density means when it hits the supply chain.

A single 1 GW nuclear reactor holds approximately 18 million of these pellets, arranged in fuel rods, bundled into fuel assemblies, loaded into the reactor core. The fuel stays in the reactor for about 4 to 5 years before being replaced. A typical refueling replaces about one-third of the fuel assemblies every 18 to 24 months.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!RXKP!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fede735cc-14ac-4852-a9a8-cd6ccf4fdbfb_1405x781.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!RXKP!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fede735cc-14ac-4852-a9a8-cd6ccf4fdbfb_1405x781.png)

Here is what that means for logistics. A 1 GW coal plant burns about 9,000 tons of coal per day. That is roughly 100 train cars, delivered every single day, year-round. The mine, the rail network, the unloading facility, the coal yards, the conveyor belts, the ash disposal: all of that infrastructure exists to feed the beast.

A 1 GW nuclear plant requires roughly 200 tonnes of natural uranium feed per year, which the enrichment process concentrates down to about 25 tonnes of enriched fuel actually loaded into the reactor. The annual fuel load fits in a handful of shipping containers.

There are no coal trains. No ash ponds. No conveyor belts. No daily deliveries. The fuel arrives in a truck a few times a year. That is it.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!fot1!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F511cd585-e9fe-4719-be51-3d0f0e9c0bcf_1405x703.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!fot1!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F511cd585-e9fe-4719-be51-3d0f0e9c0bcf_1405x703.png)

This is not a marginal difference. It is a categorical difference. It is the difference between a logistics nightmare and a logistics footnote. And it has a financial consequence that will matter enormously when we reach Part 8:because nuclear fuel is so energy-dense, fuel cost represents only about 10 to 15% of a nuclear plant’s total generation cost when measured on a levelized (new-build) basis, where capital costs dominate. For the existing operating fleet, where the capital is already sunk and depreciated, fuel’s share is somewhat higher, roughly 17% of total production cost per NEI’s 2023 data ($5.32/MWh out of $31.76/MWh total), because the denominator shrinks when you strip out capital recovery. Both figures are tiny relative to fossil fuels, where the fuel itself is 60-80% of the cost. The distinction matters for investors because the existing fleet thesis (Constellation, Kansai, CEZ) runs on operating cost economics, while the new-build thesis runs on levelized cost economics, and the fuel cost sensitivity is different in each case.

A $20/lb swing in the uranium price barely moves the needle for the utility that burns the fuel, even as it transforms the earnings of the company that mines it. That asymmetry between upstream and downstream sensitivity is one of the most important structural features of the nuclear investment landscape.

## **Part 3: The Nuclear Fuel Cycle**

### **3.1 The Supply Chain Nobody Understands**

Most people think nuclear fuel works like this: you dig up uranium, you put it in a reactor, electricity comes out. This is like saying oil works like this: you dig a hole, oil comes out, you put it in your car. Technically not wrong. Practically useless.

The nuclear fuel cycle is a five-stage industrial supply chain, and every single stage has its own economics, its own bottlenecks, its own geopolitics, and its own set of companies that control the choke points. Most nuclear primers treat this as a flat list of steps. That is why most nuclear primers are useless for investors. The fuel cycle is not a list. It is a sequential chain, and the tightest link in the chain sets the price for the entire system.

Here is the chain:

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!PAyy!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc8dfb679-611b-42e2-8fd1-90e6cc6ce436_1405x672.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!PAyy!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc8dfb679-611b-42e2-8fd1-90e6cc6ce436_1405x672.png)

**Mining & Milling → Conversion → Enrichment → Fuel Fabrication → Reactor → Back End (Storage/Reprocessing)**

We are going to walk through each stage the way a supply chain analyst would, not the way a physics textbook would. At each stage, we care about three things: who controls the capacity, where the bottleneck is, and what happens if it breaks.

### **3.2 Stage 1: Mining and Milling (Digging Up the Yellowcake)**

Uranium mining is, in principle, just mining. You find a deposit, you extract the ore, you process it into a concentrate called “yellowcake” (triuranium octoxide, U₃O₈), and you ship it to a converter. The yellowcake is a bright yellow powder, and yes, it looks exactly like it sounds. It is mildly radioactive, about as dangerous to handle as a bag of fertilizer (which, for the record, is not very dangerous if you are not eating it).

But uranium mining is not copper mining or gold mining. The economics are different, the geography is different, and the geopolitics are *much* different.

**The Geography**

In 2024, three countries produced 75% of the world’s uranium:

Kazakhstan: 39% Canada: 24% Namibia: 12%

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!t1OC!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F6bb2b362-441f-42ae-a1e7-1cefa66a434b_1405x732.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!t1OC!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F6bb2b362-441f-42ae-a1e7-1cefa66a434b_1405x732.png)

Read that again. Nearly 40% of global uranium supply comes from a single landlocked Central Asian country that shares borders with Russia and China. If you have not thought about what Kazakhstan’s political alignment means for nuclear fuel security, you should start now.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!lBPv!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F0f6efa14-577e-4b4b-9215-7339c248a025_1405x780.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!lBPv!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F0f6efa14-577e-4b4b-9215-7339c248a025_1405x780.png)

Kazakhstan’s dominance is not an accident of geology. It is an accident of economics. Kazakhstan has large, low-grade uranium deposits in sandstone formations that are perfectly suited to a mining technique called in-situ recovery (ISR), which has dramatically lower costs than conventional mining.

**ISR vs. Conventional Mining**

Conventional uranium mining works like any other hard rock mining: you dig a pit or sink a shaft, haul out the ore, crush it, and leach it with acid to extract the uranium. It is capital-intensive, slow to permit, and environmentally intrusive. The Athabasca Basin deposits in Saskatchewan, Canada, are mined this way, and they are spectacular, with ore grades 100 times the world average (Cigar Lake runs at roughly 14% U₃O₈, compared to a global average around 0.1-0.3%). But they require hundreds of millions in upfront capital, years of permitting, and complex underground operations in challenging geological conditions.

ISR is completely different. Instead of bringing the rock to the surface, you bring the chemistry to the rock. You drill injection wells into a uranium-bearing sandstone aquifer, pump in a solution laced with an oxidant (sulfuric acid in Kazakhstan, alkaline carbonate solutions in the US), the solution dissolves the uranium from the sandstone grains, and then you pump the uranium-bearing solution up through production wells to a surface processing plant where the uranium is extracted via ion exchange.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!TV4O!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F39d5a430-9786-4291-9721-b7090e2329df_1405x717.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!TV4O!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F39d5a430-9786-4291-9721-b7090e2329df_1405x717.png)

No open pit. No underground shaft. No ore trucks. No tailings dam. The surface footprint looks like an oilfield, a grid of wellheads and piping, nothing more. The capital costs are a fraction of conventional mining, and the operating costs are even more favorable. This is why Kazakhstan, with its vast ISR-amenable deposits, went from a minor producer to the world’s largest in less than two decades.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!SHp_!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fbaccdd69-022e-46ea-801e-4d1386918875_1405x732.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!SHp_!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fbaccdd69-022e-46ea-801e-4d1386918875_1405x732.png)

The downside of ISR: it only works in specific geological settings (porous sandstone aquifers with uranium mineralization in a roll-front geometry), and it requires reliable supply of sulfuric acid, which has been a persistent problem for Kazakhstan. Kazatomprom, the state-owned national producer, has repeatedly flagged sulfuric acid shortages as a constraint on production, and in late 2023 it announced it would miss its production targets for 2024 and 2025, which was one of the catalysts that pushed uranium prices through $100/lb.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!XNDG!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd524b362-5ef0-44aa-b5a9-a549702b0664_1405x576.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!XNDG!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd524b362-5ef0-44aa-b5a9-a549702b0664_1405x576.png)

**The Producers**

The uranium mining industry is surprisingly concentrated. The top producers in rough order: Kazatomprom (Kazakhstan, state-owned, approximately 40% of global production on a 100% attributable basis across its JVs, or roughly 21-24% on a company-share basis), Cameco (Canada, publicly traded, ~15-18%), Orano (France, state-owned, mining in Kazakhstan, Canada, Niger), CGN Mining (China, state-affiliated), Uranium One (Rosatom subsidiary, operations in Kazakhstan), and a long tail of smaller producers and developers including Paladin Energy, Boss Energy, NexGen Energy, Denison Mines, and others.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!wIRt!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fbaa4effe-3674-4ef0-acb7-d99dfb0283ac_1405x747.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!wIRt!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fbaa4effe-3674-4ef0-acb7-d99dfb0283ac_1405x747.png)

For investors, the key dynamics are:

First, over half of global uranium mine production comes from state-owned entities. Kazatomprom, CGN, Orano, Uranium One. These companies do not always optimize for profit. They optimize for security of supply, strategic positioning, and sometimes just national prestige. This means the uranium market does not clear like a normal commodity market. Supply decisions are political as much as economic.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!0VrE!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F03534829-94c9-4713-984a-4c29843dfa01_1405x691.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!0VrE!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F03534829-94c9-4713-984a-4c29843dfa01_1405x691.png)

Second, the post-Fukushima bear market (2011-2020) destroyed the exploration and development pipeline. Mines were shuttered, exploration budgets were slashed, and an entire generation of mining engineers went into other commodities. The projects that *were* advancing were mostly ISR operations in Kazakhstan, which reinforced the geographic concentration.

The result is that the uranium market in 2025 faces a structural supply deficit that has been years in the making and will take years to resolve, because you cannot restart a conventional mine in 6 months. You need 5-10 years from discovery to production in most jurisdictions, and that assumes permitting goes smoothly (it rarely does).

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!EiWv!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F518820eb-1c88-429e-9214-f1765eb97e1e_1405x778.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!EiWv!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F518820eb-1c88-429e-9214-f1765eb97e1e_1405x778.png)

A necessary counterpoint: the geographic concentration of uranium supply is frequently cited as a risk, and it is real, but it should not be confused with resource scarcity. Uranium is one of the most geologically abundant energy minerals on Earth. The concentration is economic and political, not geological. At sustained prices above $100/lb, deposits in Canada, Australia, the US, Namibia, and elsewhere become viable at scales that would meaningfully diversify supply within 5 to 10 years. The constraint is time, capital, and permitting, not the absence of uranium in the ground. The bull case does not depend on permanent scarcity. It depends on the lag between price signals and supply response, which in uranium is measured in years, not quarters.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!WfS-!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F944d5945-d432-4a2a-88f1-57627f75410c_1405x777.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!WfS-!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F944d5945-d432-4a2a-88f1-57627f75410c_1405x777.png)

Third, the Niger coup in July 2023 put a spotlight on jurisdictional risk. Orano’s large Somaïr and COMINAK operations in Niger were a meaningful share of global production. The military junta’s seizure of power introduced uncertainty about export approvals and operating permits that has not fully resolved. More broadly, the uranium mining map includes a number of politically unstable or strategically contested jurisdictions: Namibia, Uzbekistan, South Africa, Tanzania. The “friend-shoring” of uranium supply, redirecting procurement away from geopolitically risky sources toward Canada, Australia, and the US, is a live policy conversation that directly affects which mining companies benefit.

### **3.3 Stage 2: Conversion (The Bottleneck Nobody Talks About)**

Once you have yellowcake (U₃O₈), it needs to be converted into uranium hexafluoride (UF₆), a chemical form that can be fed into enrichment centrifuges. UF₆ is a nasty compound, highly corrosive, reactive with moisture, and it sublimes directly from solid to gas at relatively low temperatures, which is exactly the property that makes it useful for enrichment (more on that in the next section).

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Qsfe!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F2a903293-5e8c-4bdf-ae8f-5c3a6ce44f4a_1405x566.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Qsfe!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F2a903293-5e8c-4bdf-ae8f-5c3a6ce44f4a_1405x566.png)

Conversion sounds boring. It is not boring. It is one of the tightest bottlenecks in the entire nuclear fuel chain, and until recently, almost nobody outside the industry was paying attention to it.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!UBCQ!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4db88bc3-8928-40e5-b9d5-49bcdf1079c7_1405x778.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!UBCQ!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4db88bc3-8928-40e5-b9d5-49bcdf1079c7_1405x778.png)

**The Players**

There are effectively five conversion facilities in the world that matter:

Cameco’s Port Hope Conversion Facility (Ontario, Canada): approximately 12,500 tU/yr UF₆ nameplate capacity (with additional UO₂ and fuel bundle capacity bringing total facility throughput to roughly 14,000 tU/yr equivalent), the largest Western UF₆ converter. Cameco holds roughly 18% of world UF₆ conversion capacity and set a new production record in 2025 with 11.2 million kgU of UF₆ output.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!cbdu!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F47ac63d5-abd3-48e8-97ae-d890ba95a6a6_1405x789.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!cbdu!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F47ac63d5-abd3-48e8-97ae-d890ba95a6a6_1405x789.png)

Orano’s Malvési/Philippe Coste plants (Tricastin, France): approximately 15,000 tU/yr nameplate UF₆ capacity per Orano’s published figures. Orano’s new Philippe Coste plant was commissioned in 2018 and has been ramping up since, but has struggled with operational reliability. Production has hovered around 10,000-11,000 tU in recent years, well below nameplate.

ConverDyn/Honeywell Metropolis Works (Illinois, USA): approximately 15,000-17,600 tU/yr nameplate capacity (the higher figure reflecting a recent expansion). The only conversion facility in the United States.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!J6YH!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd4dcf015-bca3-4d79-a1a6-081e107882a5_1405x788.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!J6YH!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd4dcf015-bca3-4d79-a1a6-081e107882a5_1405x788.png)

It has had a troubled operational history, including labor disputes, extended shutdowns, NRC safety concerns, and a UF₆ release incident in 2023. ConverDyn resumed production in 2023 after a period offline, but its reliability remains a concern.

Rosatom/TVEL at Seversk (Russia): significant capacity, but largely serving Russia’s domestic market and politically inaccessible to Western utilities post-Ukraine.

CNNC (China): growing capacity, but reserved entirely for China’s domestic program.

That is it. For Western utilities, the accessible conversion supply comes from exactly three facilities: Port Hope, Philippe Coste/Malvési, and Metropolis. Three buildings. On two continents. Serving the entire non-Russian, non-Chinese nuclear fleet.

**The Bottleneck**

Conversion prices have increased roughly six-fold from end-2021 to mid-2024, reaching historic highs. This is not a speculative bubble. It is a supply-demand squeeze driven by the simple arithmetic of too few plants running at too-low utilization rates, with inventories drawn down and no new capacity coming online for years.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!wSxL!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fa08eb297-eaa8-418d-9f6a-6352db4f45ce_1405x786.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!wSxL!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fa08eb297-eaa8-418d-9f6a-6352db4f45ce_1405x786.png)

The Western conversion market operates at approximately 91% utilization. That leaves almost zero buffer for unplanned outages, and in an industry where “unplanned outages” are a regular occurrence (labor disputes at Metropolis, technical issues at Philippe Coste, maintenance requirements at Port Hope), a 91% utilization rate is essentially running on fumes.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!YEEQ!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F35cfa9aa-dd58-4d81-8c17-a562c2434233_1405x715.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!YEEQ!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F35cfa9aa-dd58-4d81-8c17-a562c2434233_1405x715.png)

For investors, conversion matters because it sits between two much more visible markets (uranium and enrichment) and amplifies price signals in both directions. A conversion shortage means utilities cannot feed their enrichment contracts, even if they have uranium. It is the coupling joint in the supply chain, and when it seizes up, everything downstream stops.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!CWn-!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F47b41907-c6fe-476b-a2d6-def568f0d64c_1405x684.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!CWn-!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F47b41907-c6fe-476b-a2d6-def568f0d64c_1405x684.png)

Cameco, as the operator of the world’s largest and most reliable Western conversion facility, captures disproportionate pricing power from this bottleneck. It is one of the underappreciated reasons Cameco trades at a premium to its uranium mining peers.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!7F8A!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F204e4d9e-59a2-4f1f-b5be-d857c8325f9c_1405x764.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!7F8A!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F204e4d9e-59a2-4f1f-b5be-d857c8325f9c_1405x764.png)

### **3.4 Stage 3: Enrichment (The Chokepoint That Keeps Generals Up at Night)**

If you remember nothing else from this section, remember this: enrichment is the single most strategically sensitive, geopolitically consequential, and supply-constrained link in the nuclear fuel chain.

This is not hyperbole. The technology for enriching uranium is dual-use: the same centrifuges that enrich uranium to 4% for reactor fuel can, with additional cascading, enrich it to 90% for a weapon. This is why Iran’s enrichment program triggers international crises. It is why the Treaty on the Non-Proliferation of Nuclear Weapons exists. And it is why, of all the steps in the nuclear fuel cycle, enrichment is the one where the geopolitics matter most.

**The Mixed Nuts Analogy**

Imagine you have a 50-kilogram bag of mixed nuts. 99.3% of the nuts are almonds (U-238). 0.7% are cashews (U-235). You need the cashews, and you need the cashew concentration to go from 0.7% to about 4% of the total mix. The rest, the almonds, you discard (or store, because depleted uranium has some uses).

The problem: the almonds and the cashews look almost identical. Same color, same shape, same texture. The only difference is that the cashews are *slightly* lighter, about 1.3% lighter to be precise (uranium-235 has an atomic mass of 235 versus 238 for U-238, a difference of 3 atomic mass units out of 235).

You need a sorting machine that can separate nuts by a 1.3% weight difference, and you need it to run billions of nuts through the process, because to get the cashew concentration from 0.7% to 4%, you need to process roughly 8 to 10 kilograms of natural uranium for every kilogram of enriched product. The other 7-9 kilograms come out the other end as “tails,” depleted in U-235, and get stored in steel cylinders at the enrichment facility.

The sorting machine is a gas centrifuge. It spins UF₆ gas at extremely high speeds (50,000-70,000 RPM, generating forces of over a million Gs) inside a rotor the size of a trash can. The slightly heavier U-238 molecules migrate fractionally toward the outside of the rotor. The slightly lighter U-235 molecules concentrate fractionally toward the center. The enriched fraction is scooped off and fed into the next centrifuge. This is cascading: hundreds or thousands of centrifuges connected in series, each one making a tiny incremental separation, the cumulative effect producing usably enriched uranium.

**SWU: The Currency of Enrichment**

The unit of “sorting work” in the enrichment industry is the Separative Work Unit, or SWU (pronounced “swoo” by people in the industry and “S-W-U” by everyone else). A SWU does not measure the amount of enriched uranium produced. It measures the *effort* required to achieve a given degree of separation. The more separation you need (higher target enrichment, or lower tails assay), the more SWUs you need.

For a standard light water reactor fuel enrichment (from 0.711% natural to about 4.5% enriched, with tails at 0.25-0.30%), you need roughly 7-8 SWUs per kilogram of enriched product. At lower enrichments (3.5%, which is the bottom of the commercial range), the requirement drops to roughly 4.3-4.8 SWU/kg. The full range across commercial reactor fuels is approximately 4-9 SWU/kg depending on target enrichment and tails assay, which matters for fuel cost modeling.

SWU is priced independently of uranium: you can buy natural uranium from a miner and buy enrichment services (SWUs) from an enricher separately. The price of a SWU as of late 2024 was approximately $185-193 on the spot market, up from $47.50 in June 2020. That is a roughly 4x increase in four years.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!cdsS!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4dfdb525-62e9-4915-aa8b-a391f12e98dc_1405x789.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!cdsS!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4dfdb525-62e9-4915-aa8b-a391f12e98dc_1405x789.png)

Why this matters: the cost of nuclear fuel to a utility is a function of natural uranium prices, conversion prices, SWU prices, and fabrication costs. Enrichment (SWU) typically represents 30-40% of the total fuel cost. It is the largest single component. So when SWU prices quadruple, the economics of nuclear fuel change meaningfully, even if uranium prices stay flat.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!W58P!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ffe295742-4e94-4c65-9103-d25c8a9a6986_1405x619.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!W58P!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ffe295742-4e94-4c65-9103-d25c8a9a6986_1405x619.png)

**The Market Structure: Russia’s Shadow**

The global enrichment market is the most concentrated chokepoint in the entire nuclear supply chain. There are effectively four players:

Rosatom/TENEX (Russia): approximately 27 million SWU/year capacity across four facilities. That is roughly 40-44% of global capacity. Russia has the largest, most advanced, and cheapest enrichment infrastructure on Earth, a legacy of Cold War weapons production that was repurposed for commercial use.

Urenco (UK/Germany/Netherlands/USA joint venture): approximately 18 million SWU/year across four facilities (Capenhurst, Gronau, Almelo, and Eunice, New Mexico). Urenco is the principal Western enricher and is currently expanding capacity by 1.8 million SWU across all four sites, with the first new US cascades coming online in 2025.

Orano (France): approximately 7.5 million SWU/year at the Georges Besse II facility at Tricastin. Orano is studying a 30% capacity expansion and has selected Oak Ridge, Tennessee as the site for a potential new US enrichment facility.

CNNC (China): approximately 8-11 million SWU/year (estimates vary as Chinese capacity data is not publicly reported with precision) and growing rapidly, with upper-bound projections from Ux Consulting suggesting capacity could reach as high as 23 million SWU/year by 2030 under a maximum-build scenario, though most independent estimates cluster around 13-17 million SWU. But Chinese capacity is reserved for China’s domestic fleet and is functionally inaccessible to Western utilities.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!K7f0!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F0986bb07-a558-40ba-ad0f-522e7efe3e62_1405x778.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!K7f0!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F0986bb07-a558-40ba-ad0f-522e7efe3e62_1405x778.png)

Do the arithmetic. Total global capacity is roughly 60-65 million SWU/year. Russia controls about 27 million of that. China controls about 9 million, growing to 17 million. Neither is available to Western utilities going forward (Russia due to sanctions, China due to domestic reservation). That leaves Urenco and Orano with roughly 25-26 million SWU/year of capacity accessible to the West, against Western demand that currently runs around 35-40 million SWU/year.

The gap was historically filled by Russian supply. In 2024, Russia still supplied approximately 20% of total US enrichment needs (roughly 25% of foreign-origin SWU purchases), and about 23% of EU enrichment needs, down sharply from 31% in 2021 and 38% in 2023 per the Euratom Supply Agency.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!AUHC!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff096c3ae-4fa6-46b4-8f5d-bfec436b1c57_1405x775.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!AUHC!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff096c3ae-4fa6-46b4-8f5d-bfec436b1c57_1405x775.png)

The US banned imports of Russian enriched uranium effective August 2024 (with narrow waivers through January 2028), and Europe is moving toward disengagement under the REPowerEU framework, though without a hard ban.

This is the enrichment squeeze. Western utilities need to replace roughly 3.5-4 million SWU/year of Russian supply for the US market alone, and Urenco’s expansion adds only 1.8 million SWU across several years. Orano’s expansion is years from completion. Centrus Energy, the only other US-based enricher, is producing tiny HALEU (high-assay low-enriched uranium) quantities for the DOE, not commercial-scale LEU.

The market is responding. Urenco’s order book grew 27% in 2024 to €18.7 billion, extending into the 2040s. Utilities are signing longer-term contracts at higher prices. Spot SWU prices continue to rise. The structural deficit in Western enrichment capacity is not a forecast. It is a present-tense reality that will take until the early 2030s to meaningfully resolve, and that assumes all planned expansions execute on schedule (a generous assumption given the nuclear industry’s track record on construction timelines).

There is a second-order dynamic that the enrichment squeeze creates, and it is one that most uranium analysis misses entirely: overfeeding.

When SWU is expensive relative to uranium (as it is now, with spot SWU at roughly $193 versus historical norms of $50 to $100), utilities can adjust the enrichment process to use fewer SWUs per kilogram of product by raising the tails assay, the U-235 concentration left in the depleted uranium waste stream. Think of it this way: at a low tails assay (say, 0.20%), the centrifuges extract nearly every last atom of U-235, which requires more spinning (more SWU) but less raw uranium feed. At a higher tails assay (say, 0.30%), the centrifuges are less thorough, leaving more U-235 in the waste stream, which requires less spinning but more uranium to compensate.

The arithmetic is instructive. At a tails assay of 0.20% (historically standard when SWU was cheap), producing 1 kg of 4.5% LEU requires about 7.7 SWU and roughly 8.4 kg of natural uranium feed. At 0.30% tails (increasingly rational at current SWU prices), the same kilogram of LEU requires only about 6.2 SWU but needs roughly 10.2 kg of uranium feed. That is approximately 21% more uranium per kilogram of product.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!1Fyg!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd2ee320e-250f-442c-b89a-bfd2d15eb725_1405x591.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!1Fyg!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd2ee320e-250f-442c-b89a-bfd2d15eb725_1405x591.png)

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!RwX9!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F8bc23e45-ab68-49f9-9818-a6c9d15282ff_1405x784.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!RwX9!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F8bc23e45-ab68-49f9-9818-a6c9d15282ff_1405x784.png)

Across a global fleet of 440 reactors, a widespread shift from 0.20% to 0.30% tails could add 10,000 to 15,000 tonnes of incremental annual uranium demand, roughly 15 to 20% above baseline consumption.

This is not theoretical. Utilities actively adjust tails assay in their enrichment contracts based on relative uranium/SWU pricing, and the current price relationship strongly incentivizes overfeeding. The investment implication is significant: the SWU shortage does not just tighten the enrichment market. It structurally inflates uranium demand, creating a second-order tailwind for uranium prices. This is one of the clearest examples of how pricing power flows backward through the fuel chain from the tightest bottleneck.

A note of self-interrogation is warranted here. Whenever an analytical finding perfectly supports the highest-conviction trade, the right instinct is suspicion. The counterargument is that utility inventories are larger and more resilient than public data suggests (utilities do not disclose fuel inventories in detail), that the shift to higher tails assays may be less uniform than the math implies, and that secondary supply from re-enrichment of existing tails could partially offset the incremental demand. The direction of the effect is robust. The magnitude is uncertain.

There is also a quiet, uncomfortable wrinkle that few analysts discuss publicly. Some evidence suggests that China is importing Russian enriched uranium to feed its domestic reactors, thereby freeing its own enrichment capacity for export to Western customers. If true, this means Western utilities are obtaining what is effectively Russian-origin enrichment through a Chinese intermediary. Western lawmakers are beginning to flag this as a potential backdoor channel, which could trigger future regulatory action and further tighten supply.

For investors, enrichment is the section of the fuel cycle with the strongest structural bull case and the fewest available equity proxies. Urenco is privately held (owned by the British, Dutch, and German governments). Orano is state-owned. Rosatom is sanctioned. That leaves Centrus Energy (NYSE: LEU) as essentially the only publicly traded pure-play enrichment company in the West, which goes a long way toward explaining why its stock has been one of the best performers in the nuclear sector.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!nF40!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F24021a87-fb69-4310-b746-74675c417909_1405x782.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!nF40!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F24021a87-fb69-4310-b746-74675c417909_1405x782.png)

### **3.5 Stage 4: Fuel Fabrication (The Relatively Boring One)**

After enrichment, the enriched UF₆ is shipped to a fuel fabrication facility, where it is converted (de-converted, technically) from UF₆ gas back into uranium dioxide (UO₂) powder, pressed into those small ceramic pellets we talked about in Part 2, sintered (heated to about 1,750°C to densify them), ground to precise dimensions, stacked into metal tubes (zirconium alloy cladding, typically Zircaloy), sealed, and assembled into fuel assemblies. Each assembly is a precise, engineered component containing hundreds of fuel rods arranged in a specific geometry optimized for the reactor it is going into.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!CJ29!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd7240006-56eb-4255-a618-20146f67e757_1405x723.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!CJ29!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd7240006-56eb-4255-a618-20146f67e757_1405x723.png)

Fuel fabrication is more commoditized than the upstream stages, but it is still concentrated among a handful of players: Westinghouse (US/Sweden), Framatome (France, Orano subsidiary), TVEL (Russia, Rosatom subsidiary), CNNC/CJNF (China), and KEPCO Nuclear Fuel (South Korea). In the West, Westinghouse and Framatome dominate.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!dAtj!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F908c5e22-7d57-4b43-a259-28a319a3a0ef_1405x647.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!dAtj!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F908c5e22-7d57-4b43-a259-28a319a3a0ef_1405x647.png)

The strategic significance of fuel fabrication is more about lock-in than scarcity. Fuel assemblies are designed for specific reactor types, and switching fabricators requires re-engineering and regulatory re-qualification, which takes years. This means that once a utility is locked into a fabrication supplier, switching costs are high. It also means that Rosatom’s fuel fabrication business, particularly for VVER reactors (Russia’s export reactor design), creates a dependency chain: countries that bought Russian reactors are often locked into Russian fuel assemblies, which locks them into Russian enrichment, which locks them into a long-term strategic relationship with Moscow.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!tc7a!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F35616a8f-2d2d-43a5-903b-11416b3fbcf6_1405x793.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!tc7a!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F35616a8f-2d2d-43a5-903b-11416b3fbcf6_1405x793.png)

This is precisely why the transition of Eastern European VVER operators (Bulgaria, Czech Republic, Slovakia, Finland) away from Russian fuel assemblies and toward Westinghouse alternatives is such a significant strategic and commercial development. It is breaking Rosatom’s lock-in, one fuel assembly at a time.

For investors, fuel fabrication is not where the action is in the current cycle. The margins are relatively thin, the barriers to entry are high but stable, and the supply-demand dynamics are less acute than in enrichment or conversion. But watch Westinghouse (owned by Cameco and Brookfield) and Framatome (Orano subsidiary) as strategic assets in the broader nuclear renaissance.

### **3.6 Stage 5: The Back End (What Do You Do With the Waste?)**

This is the section that anti-nuclear advocates want you to read first and pro-nuclear advocates want you to skip. We are going to do neither. We are going to lay out the facts, explain the two competing philosophies, and let you decide how much of a problem this actually is.

After about 4-5 years in the reactor, a fuel assembly is “spent.” It has used up enough of its U-235 to no longer sustain an efficient chain reaction (though it still contains about 95% of its original uranium, plus small amounts of plutonium and other transuranic elements created during irradiation). The spent fuel is intensely radioactive and thermally hot. It needs to be managed. How it gets managed depends entirely on which of two philosophical camps your country falls into.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!ut2c!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F8dbedf44-e3fe-4109-a159-def293a9e4d7_1405x489.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!ut2c!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F8dbedf44-e3fe-4109-a159-def293a9e4d7_1405x489.png)

**Option A: Direct Disposal (The American Model)**

In the United States, spent fuel is classified as high-level radioactive waste and is intended for permanent geological disposal, burial in a deep underground repository designed to isolate the waste for hundreds of thousands of years.

The keyword in that sentence is “intended.” The US has never built a permanent repository.

The saga of Yucca Mountain is a case study in how democracy, geology, and nuclear politics collide. In 1987, Congress designated Yucca Mountain in Nevada as the site for the nation’s permanent spent fuel repository. The DOE spent $15 billion studying the site. In 2008, it submitted a license application to the NRC. In 2010, the Obama administration defunded the project, largely due to opposition from Nevada senator Harry Reid. The license application was withdrawn. No alternative was designated.

As a result, spent fuel in the United States is stored “temporarily” at the reactor sites where it was generated. First in spent fuel pools (large pools of water that provide cooling and radiation shielding), then, once cooled sufficiently (5-10 years), transferred to dry cask storage, massive concrete and steel containers that sit on concrete pads at the reactor site.

There are currently over 90,000 metric tons of spent nuclear fuel stored at roughly 75 reactor sites across the United States. It is perfectly safe in its current configuration, dry casks are designed to withstand earthquakes, tornadoes, floods, and aircraft impacts, but it is not a permanent solution, and the legal and political status of the waste remains unresolved.

The cost is real but manageable. US utilities pay a fee of 1 mill (one-tenth of a cent) per kilowatt-hour of nuclear electricity into the Nuclear Waste Fund, which has accumulated over $50 billion (including interest), most of which the government has not spent because there is no repository to spend it on. The Department of Energy is legally liable for failing to take possession of the waste as required by the Nuclear Waste Policy Act, and has paid billions in damages to utilities for breach of contract.

**Option B: Reprocessing (The French Model)**

France took a fundamentally different approach. Rather than treating spent fuel as waste, France treats it as a resource to be recycled.

At the La Hague reprocessing plant in Normandy, operated by Orano, spent fuel assemblies are dissolved in nitric acid. The solution is then put through a chemical separation process (PUREX, for Plutonium Uranium Extraction) that separates three streams: recovered uranium (~95% of the original), plutonium (~1%), and high-level fission products (~4%).

The recovered uranium can be re-enriched and made into new fuel. The plutonium is mixed with depleted uranium to create MOX (Mixed Oxide) fuel, which can be used in standard PWRs. The high-level fission products are vitrified (mixed into glass) and stored in steel canisters for eventual deep geological disposal.

The result is dramatic waste volume reduction. France generates about 75% of its electricity from nuclear and has been doing so for decades, yet the total volume of high-level vitrified waste produced by the entire French nuclear program, all of it, fits into a single room beneath La Hague. That is not a metaphor. It is a literal room.

The trade-off is that reprocessing is expensive, it involves separating plutonium (a proliferation concern), and it does not eliminate the need for a geological repository (you still need to store the vitrified fission products somewhere permanent). France is building its deep geological repository, Cigéo, in clay formations in northeastern France, scheduled for operations starting in the 2030s.

The United Kingdom, Japan, and Russia also reprocess spent fuel. The US abandoned reprocessing in the 1970s under President Carter due to proliferation concerns, and has never revisited the policy in a serious way.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!48nl!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe23d3718-e7c0-4bd8-a52a-896ad5ab85af_1405x785.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!48nl!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe23d3718-e7c0-4bd8-a52a-896ad5ab85af_1405x785.png)

**Putting the Waste “Problem” in Context**

Here is the number that reframes the entire debate: all of the spent nuclear fuel ever generated by the entire US commercial nuclear fleet, more than 60 years of electricity generation providing roughly 20% of the country’s power, fits in a volume equivalent to a football field stacked about 10 yards high (though decay heat from spent assemblies requires wide spacing in any actual repository, making the facility footprint far larger than the raw volume implies). That is it

The total physical mass of the “waste problem” is roughly 90,000 metric tons, sitting on concrete pads at reactor sites, doing nothing harmful to anyone.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!pMbH!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F94447c51-fcf4-4f06-be4d-cb504d65fee0_1405x782.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!pMbH!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F94447c51-fcf4-4f06-be4d-cb504d65fee0_1405x782.png)

The contrast with coal ash, which is toxic, voluminous, and routinely leaches into groundwater from unlined disposal ponds, is categorical rather than incremental. Nuclear waste is compact, contained, solid, and has never harmed a single member of the public in the history of commercial nuclear power. The “waste problem” is a political problem, not a technical one, and it has been solved, technically, by multiple countries. The US just has not chosen to implement any of the solutions.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!j013!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F9c17412b-abb5-4ffc-b0d4-7f549afb0a99_1405x671.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!j013!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F9c17412b-abb5-4ffc-b0d4-7f549afb0a99_1405x671.png)

#### **3.6.1 Decommissioning: The Back-End Cost Nobody Budgets For**

There is a cost at the end of a reactor’s life that most nuclear investment analyses treat as a footnote: decommissioning. The process of safely dismantling a nuclear power plant, decontaminating the site, and managing the resulting waste takes 15-30 years and costs between $500 million and $1.5 billion per reactor, depending on size, design, and regulatory requirements. The NRC’s 2024 estimates range from $280 million to $612 million per unit in the most favorable cases, but actual completed decommissioning projects in the US (Yankee Rowe, Connecticut Yankee, Maine Yankee) have run $400 million to $1 billion per unit, and the UK’s Nuclear Decommissioning Authority estimates total lifetime decommissioning costs across the British nuclear legacy at £99-232 billion (though this includes military and early research facilities, not just commercial power stations).

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!x55w!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F72c53d36-2d4b-4804-aa30-ef6480137f84_1405x789.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!x55w!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F72c53d36-2d4b-4804-aa30-ef6480137f84_1405x789.png)

US nuclear utilities are required to maintain decommissioning trust funds, funded by ratepayer contributions during the reactor’s operating life. As of 2024, the industry held approximately $95 billion in decommissioning trust assets, which the NRC deems adequate for current obligations. The good news: for operating reactors with decades of remaining life, the trust funds will continue to compound. The concern: utilities that shut reactors prematurely (like Entergy’s Vermont Yankee, Indian Point, and Pilgrim) sometimes found their trusts underfunded relative to actual costs, requiring corporate backstops.

For investors, decommissioning is a manageable but real liability that should be factored into any honest lifecycle cost comparison between nuclear and alternatives. It is also a growing business opportunity: companies like Holtec International and EnergySolutions are building decommissioning-as-a-service businesses, purchasing retired reactors from utilities and managing the dismantlement process. Holtec, which also owns the Palisades restart project, is effectively a decommissioning company that is pivoting to become a nuclear operator, an unusual but strategically logical evolution.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!baa2!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5478b5f8-ff65-4851-b2cd-d93a8af5d41d_1405x789.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!baa2!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5478b5f8-ff65-4851-b2cd-d93a8af5d41d_1405x789.png)

The analytical point: any LCOE comparison between nuclear and alternatives should include decommissioning costs (typically adding $1-3/MWh to nuclear’s levelized cost, depending on assumptions). Most published LCOE figures do include this, but investors should verify, because the ones that look suspiciously cheap sometimes do not.

### **3.7 The Supply Chain as an Investment Framework**

Now that you understand the five stages, here is the framework that matters for investors:

The value chain flows left to right: Mining → Conversion → Enrichment → Fabrication → Reactor. But the *pricing power* flows right to left. The tightest bottleneck in the chain sets the clearing price for everything upstream.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!jylS!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F002767e1-5a28-44d5-abb5-aca616192ba9_1405x584.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!jylS!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F002767e1-5a28-44d5-abb5-aca616192ba9_1405x584.png)

Right now, enrichment is the tightest bottleneck. Western SWU capacity is structurally insufficient to replace Russian supply within this decade. Conversion is the second-tightest, with only three Western facilities operating at over 90% utilization. Mining is tight but responsive; higher prices are bringing mothballed capacity back online and incentivizing new development.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!9TK_!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5f0a2bec-df8e-49da-9afd-2c1e9d7a02b3_1405x679.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!9TK_!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5f0a2bec-df8e-49da-9afd-2c1e9d7a02b3_1405x679.png)

This means:

Enrichment companies (Centrus, and eventually Urenco/Orano if they ever go public or take investments) have the strongest structural pricing power. SWU prices have quadrupled and are not coming back down.

Conversion-advantaged producers (Cameco, which owns the world’s most reliable conversion facility) capture dual premiums: once on the uranium they mine, and again on the conversion services they provide. This is why Cameco is not “just a miner.”

Miners with near-term production in friendly jurisdictions (NexGen in Canada, Boss Energy in Australia, Paladin in Namibia, Denison in Canada) benefit from the overall uranium price environment, but their leverage to the enrichment/conversion bottleneck is indirect.

Physical uranium vehicles (Sprott Physical Uranium Trust, Yellow Cake plc) give exposure to the uranium price without mining risk, but they do not capture the conversion or enrichment premiums.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!D26N!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F49c55599-a7cd-4a8d-b623-b58ac9aaf8f5_1405x782.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!D26N!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F49c55599-a7cd-4a8d-b623-b58ac9aaf8f5_1405x782.png)

Here is the fuel cycle in one table:

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!_BIs!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F6bcfefb0-6c6e-4cf0-a832-b7aaf32de85c_1405x782.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!_BIs!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F6bcfefb0-6c6e-4cf0-a832-b7aaf32de85c_1405x782.png)

Understanding this hierarchy, and where each investment sits within it, is the difference between a portfolio that captures the nuclear thesis and a portfolio that merely owns some uranium miners and hopes for the best.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!sOc3!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F09eaa467-2461-4fe4-af00-138baf1491a5_1405x786.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!sOc3!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F09eaa467-2461-4fe4-af00-138baf1491a5_1405x786.png)

## **Part 4: The Uranium Market**

### **4.1 This Is Not a Normal Commodity Market**

Uranium does not trade like copper. It does not trade like oil. It does not trade like gold. If you try to analyze the uranium market using the mental models you developed trading literally any other commodity, you will get it wrong. Not a little wrong. Fundamentally wrong.

Here is why.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!blo0!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4092f714-1e35-4e3d-9159-1e98831b8148_1405x722.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!blo0!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4092f714-1e35-4e3d-9159-1e98831b8148_1405x722.png)

There is no meaningful futures market for uranium. No NYMEX contract. No ICE benchmark. No pit traders in colored jackets screaming at each other. Uranium is not traded in meaningful quantities on any commodity exchange. The market is principally based on private, bilateral negotiations between a small number of producers and an even smaller number of utility buyers, conducted through intermediaries and consultants, with prices reported by two specialist firms (UxC and TradeTech) that survey the market weekly and monthly.

This means several things that matter enormously for investors:

Price discovery is opaque. You cannot pull up a real-time uranium chart on your Bloomberg Terminal the way you can for crude oil. The “spot price” you see reported is a survey-based indicator derived from a handful of transactions, sometimes just one or two per week, each involving a few hundred thousand pounds. The “market” in any given week might be three to five trades. In a thin week, it might be one.

The market is dominated by long-term contracts, not spot trading. Approximately 85-90% of all uranium is purchased under multi-year term contracts between producers and utilities, typically with delivery windows stretching 5-10 years. The spot market accounts for only 10-15% of total volumes. This means the spot price, the number that shows up on financial websites and in analyst reports, is a residual market, a sideshow. The term price is where the real business happens.

And here is the dynamic that most analysts, especially those who come from equity or macro backgrounds, consistently underestimate: utilities are the slowest buyers in any commodity market on Earth. A nuclear utility’s fuel procurement cycle operates on a timeline measured in years, not months. They plan fuel loads 2-3 years in advance. They negotiate contracts over quarters, sometimes years. They have internal committees, board approvals, regulatory filings. They are constitutionally incapable of acting quickly, and they are rewarded for delay because uranium fuel represents only about 5-10% of a nuclear plant’s total operating cost. If they pay 30% more for uranium, the impact on the cost of electricity to the consumer is measured in fractions of a cent per kilowatt-hour. They can afford to wait.

This means the uranium market has a persistent asymmetry: when prices fall, production gets curtailed quickly (miners shut mines at cash cost), but when prices rise, demand responds with a lag measured in years as utilities slowly, grudgingly, eventually sign contracts. The bull case in uranium is always a story of inevitability delayed. The bears always sound right for longer than they should, because they are observing the spot market while the real action happens in conversations between procurement officers and mining executives that will not become public for months.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!EgBx!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe07e91d6-4471-4fd5-9fa6-649f922b23c1_1405x786.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!EgBx!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe07e91d6-4471-4fd5-9fa6-649f922b23c1_1405x786.png)

### **4.2 A Brief History of Uranium Cycles (Or: How to Lose Money Being Right)**

Understanding where we are requires understanding where we have been.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!q5jF!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F1ab70c29-f477-41dc-a183-964ba1cf811a_1405x782.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!q5jF!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F1ab70c29-f477-41dc-a183-964ba1cf811a_1405x782.png)

**The First Cycle (2003-2007): Cigar Lake Breaks and the Market Goes Insane**

In 2003, uranium was trading at roughly $10/lb. Nobody cared about it. The post-Cold War era had created a massive overhang of secondary supply: the US-Russia Megatons to Megawatts program was converting Russian weapons-grade highly enriched uranium (HEU) into commercial reactor fuel, flooding the market with roughly 24 million pounds of uranium equivalent per year. Mines were shuttering. Exploration was dead. The word “uranium” at an investment conference would empty the room.

Then two things happened. First, the Megatons to Megawatts program had a fixed end date (2013), and the market slowly began pricing in the eventual loss of that supply. Second, in October 2006, Cameco’s Cigar Lake mine, the largest undeveloped high-grade uranium deposit in the world, flooded during construction. The mine that was supposed to supply 18 million pounds per year, roughly 13% of global demand, was suddenly gone. The market panicked. Speculative capital rushed in. Uranium prices spiked from $40/lb in early 2006 to a peak of $136/lb in June 2007.

Then they crashed. The price fell back below $50 by late 2008 as the global financial crisis destroyed risk appetite, and speculators who had driven the price up vomited their positions. Cigar Lake eventually came online in 2014, eight years late.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!LGzb!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe200adaf-a59e-465b-adaf-46b6b1d5fc5e_1405x669.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!LGzb!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe200adaf-a59e-465b-adaf-46b6b1d5fc5e_1405x669.png)

The lesson: uranium price spikes driven by speculative capital and supply shocks can be violent but are not sustainable without a change in underlying demand fundamentals. The 2007 spike was real in its supply-side catalyst but synthetic in its magnitude.

**The Second Cycle (2011-2020): Fukushima and the Lost Decade**

On March 11, 2011, the Fukushima disaster destroyed not just three reactor cores but the entire demand thesis for uranium. Japan shut down 54 reactors. Germany accelerated its nuclear exit. Global uranium demand dropped roughly 10-15% almost overnight. Meanwhile, supply from Kazakhstan continued to ramp (Kazatomprom was aggressively growing ISR production regardless of price signals, because it was a state entity optimizing for market share, not profitability).

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!jqsq!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd048ba28-4c21-4b64-9b60-280fa85f35a5_1405x705.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!jqsq!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd048ba28-4c21-4b64-9b60-280fa85f35a5_1405x705.png)

The result was a decade of oversupply. The spot price ground lower, from $70/lb pre-Fukushima to a bottom of roughly $18/lb in November 2016. At $18, uranium was below the all-in cost of production for virtually every mine on Earth except the lowest-cost ISR operations in Kazakhstan. Mines closed. Cameco idled McArthur River and Cigar Lake. Paladin Energy put Langer Heinrich into care and maintenance. Exploration budgets were annihilated. An entire generation of uranium projects was shelved.

This is the decade that created the conditions for the current bull market. A decade of underinvestment in supply, combined with the drawdown of above-ground inventories, the depletion of secondary supply sources, and the beginning of a demand recovery (Japanese restarts, Chinese construction, life extensions in the US and Europe), set up the structural deficit that we are now living through.

**The Current Cycle (2020-Present): Structural Deficit Meets Geopolitical Disruption**

The current uranium market is defined by a convergence of demand growth, supply constraints, and geopolitical disruption that is qualitatively different from prior cycles.

On the demand side: the global reactor fleet set a generation record in 2024 (2,667 TWh). China is building reactors at a pace of 6-10 per year. Over 30 countries pledged to triple nuclear capacity by 2050. Life extensions for existing reactors are adding decades of fuel demand. Data center PPAs from tech companies are creating a new category of demand that did not exist in prior cycles.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!s6wo!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F72d24de5-6b73-4712-b8a5-431583a4d1b0_1405x622.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!s6wo!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F72d24de5-6b73-4712-b8a5-431583a4d1b0_1405x622.png)

On the supply side: global primary uranium production met only about 90% of reactor demand in 2024, with the remaining 10% covered by drawdowns of inventory and secondary supplies. Kazatomprom missed its production targets in 2024 and cut nominal 2026 production guidance. The Niger coup disrupted Orano’s operations (zero production at SOMAÏR in 2025 under junta control). US ISR restarts have ramped more slowly than planned. Cigar Lake and McArthur River are producing but at lower rates than peak capacity.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!vLSt!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fbf86aa00-8061-4ba5-a6c9-866f9e9ec160_1405x708.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!vLSt!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fbf86aa00-8061-4ba5-a6c9-866f9e9ec160_1405x708.png)

On the geopolitical side: the US ban on Russian enriched uranium (effective August 2024) and the broader Western effort to disentangle from Russian fuel supply has disrupted the market’s historical logistics. Kazakhstan’s uranium, which accounts for 39% of global supply, must transit either through Russia or via the Trans-Caspian route through the Caspian Sea, Azerbaijan, Georgia, and Turkey, a long and geopolitically fraught supply chain that has already experienced delays.

The spot price in 2025 traded in a range of roughly $63 to $83/lb, frustratingly flat for investors who expected a runaway bull market, before surging above $100/lb in January 2026 on aggressive buying from Sprott and tightening physical availability.

But the spot price is, as we discussed, a sideshow. The term price, the one that actually matters, climbed steadily from $80 to $86 over the course of the year, and utilities began accepting higher base prices in new contracts. Conversion term pricing hit historic highs with a 27% yearly increase. SWU spot prices sat at roughly $193/SWU, up from $47.50 five years ago.

The market is in a stalemate. Producers are reluctant to sell forward at current prices, believing they will go higher. Utilities are reluctant to contract at current prices, hoping they will go lower. This standoff is familiar to anyone who has traded commodity cycles. It always breaks in the same direction: the buyer blinks, because the buyer is the one who has to fuel a reactor. You can choose not to mine uranium. You cannot choose not to fuel a reactor that 600,000 homes depend on for electricity.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!4atr!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F840e8c4d-3b16-43d4-bb25-0d6375668690_1405x778.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!4atr!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F840e8c4d-3b16-43d4-bb25-0d6375668690_1405x778.png)

In 2025, approximately 116 million pounds of uranium was placed under long-term contracts, below the industry’s estimated replacement rate of approximately 150 million pounds (and well below the roughly 185 million pounds of annual reactor demand). This means uncovered utility requirements are growing larger each year the stalemate persists. The pent-up demand is building. When it releases, whether in 2026 or 2027, the contracting wave will be substantial, and it will be at higher prices than today.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!GgHW!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc591af86-9fae-4893-bdc9-024299fe5439_1405x777.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!GgHW!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc591af86-9fae-4893-bdc9-024299fe5439_1405x777.png)

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Kw1M!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F04d25fac-f3a3-46dd-856b-66278b0ca591_1405x781.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Kw1M!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F04d25fac-f3a3-46dd-856b-66278b0ca591_1405x781.png)

### **4.3 Secondary Supplies: The Buffer That’s Running Out**

One of the most important and least understood dynamics in the uranium market is the role of secondary supplies, material that reaches the market through routes other than primary mine production.

Historically, secondary supplies have been enormous. During the 1990s and 2000s, they included:

Government stockpile releases (US DOE, Russian HEU downblending under Megatons to Megawatts). Utility inventory drawdowns (utilities ran down their fuel reserves, buying less new uranium than they consumed). Enricher underfeeding (enrichment plants can operate their centrifuges to extract more U-235 from the tails than the contract specifies, effectively creating “extra” uranium that can be sold back into the market). Reprocessed and re-enriched uranium (MOX fuel, RepU). Military surplus.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!NCGr!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F60631e8f-9224-41de-815e-bac40f37e9ac_1405x786.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!NCGr!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F60631e8f-9224-41de-815e-bac40f37e9ac_1405x786.png)

At their peak, these secondary supplies covered roughly one-third of global reactor demand. This was the elephant in the room that kept uranium prices suppressed for decades. Even as primary mine production was insufficient to meet demand, the market balanced because there was a massive buffer of above-ground material being drawn down.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!d5kZ!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fec39c304-fc70-4c3d-9bb9-f27fe6c9c615_1405x771.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!d5kZ!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fec39c304-fc70-4c3d-9bb9-f27fe6c9c615_1405x771.png)

That buffer is now largely depleted.

The Megatons to Megawatts program ended in 2013. US DOE stockpile sales have been curtailed by legislation. Utility inventories, after a decade of drawdowns following Fukushima, are at their lowest levels in years. Enricher underfeeding, which was substantial when SWU prices were low and uranium prices were low (making it cheap to “overfeed” the centrifuges and sell the excess natural uranium equivalent), has reversed as SWU prices have quadrupled, the economics now favor overfeeding (using more SWUs and less natural uranium), which *increases* uranium demand rather than substituting for it.

The transition from an inventory-driven market to a production-driven market is one of the most important structural shifts in uranium in two decades. It means that incremental demand must be met by incremental mine production, not by drawing down a stockpile. And incremental mine production takes 5-10 years to bring online.

This is why the “structural deficit” thesis has legs. The bears will point out (correctly) that the deficit has been smaller than the bulls predicted in some years, and that above-ground inventory acts as a shock absorber. But the shock absorber is getting thinner each year, and the demand trajectory is getting steeper. The math eventually overwhelms the buffer.

### **4.4 The Structural Deficit: Real, but More Complicated Than the Bulls Admit**

Let me give you the bull case and the bear case honestly, because TSCS does not do advocacy.

**The Bull Case**

Global reactor demand is roughly 180-185 million pounds per year and growing. Primary mine production covers roughly 155-165 million pounds (UxC estimates approximately 164 million pounds for 2025). The gap of 25-35 million pounds per year is being filled by inventory drawdowns and secondary supplies that are depleting. New mine supply takes 5-10 years to bring online. Therefore, prices must rise to incentivize enough new production to close the gap, and in the interim, inventory drawdowns will tighten the market further.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Wpco!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff8223576-186e-490b-aac2-3966e0ae4a71_1405x732.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Wpco!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff8223576-186e-490b-aac2-3966e0ae4a71_1405x732.png)

This case is straightforward, well-supported by data, and directionally correct.

**The Bear Case (or at least, the nuances the bulls skip)**

First, the deficit is measured against *requirements*, not actual purchases. Utilities can and do undercontract, running down inventories, delaying procurement, and managing their fuel cycles to defer purchases. The “deficit” in any given year is partly real and partly a reflection of utility timing decisions. Utilities are not going to run out of fuel. They are going to run out of *optionality*, which is a different thing.

Second, supply elasticity is not zero. At $80+/lb, projects that were uneconomic at $40 are now viable. ISR operations in Wyoming and Texas are restarting. Boss Energy’s Honeymoon in Australia is ramping. Paladin’s Langer Heinrich in Namibia achieved commercial production in 2024. NexGen’s Arrow project in Canada is the most significant new high-grade deposit in a generation. The question is not whether new supply will come, it is whether it will come fast enough to match demand growth, given permitting timelines and construction schedules.

Third, China’s domestic uranium mining and enrichment buildout is wild card. If China successfully scales domestic capacity to self-sufficiency, it releases Kazakh and other supply back into the global market. China currently aims to source one-third of its fuel domestically, one-third from equity stakes in foreign mines, and one-third on the open market. If the domestic third grows faster than expected, the supply picture loosens.

Fourth, the demand projections assume that all announced reactor construction, life extensions, and restarts actually happen, on time. History suggests at least some of them will be delayed or cancelled. The demand side has optionality that the bulls sometimes assume away.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!4Ifq!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F03164598-6bd6-4447-8551-5449cc17d400_1405x776.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!4Ifq!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F03164598-6bd6-4447-8551-5449cc17d400_1405x776.png)

**The Synthesis**

The structural deficit is real in direction. Primary supply growth cannot match the trajectory of demand growth within this decade without significantly higher prices. The secondary supply buffer is thinning. The geopolitical disruption to Russian and Kazakh supply adds a risk premium that was not in the market five years ago.

But the magnitude and timing of the deficit are uncertain.
