It could manifest as a sharp price spike (if a major supply disruption coincides with a contracting wave), or as a slow grind higher (if supply restarts and new projects gradually close the gap). The base case for uranium is higher for longer, not a vertical spike. If you are positioning for $200/lb uranium on a specific timeline, you are making a timing bet, not a fundamental bet. The fundamentals say prices need to be higher. They do not say when.

### **4.5 What This Means for Investors**

The uranium market’s unusual structure creates specific investment implications:

**Physical uranium vehicles** (Sprott Physical Uranium Trust, Yellow Cake plc) give you direct commodity exposure without mining risk. SPUT added 7.8 million pounds in 2025, growing its holdings to over 74 million pounds, making it one of the largest holders of physical uranium on Earth. These vehicles effectively remove supply from the market, tightening the spot balance. They are a way to own the “higher for longer” thesis without betting on any individual miner’s execution.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!4OBi!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F476e9ade-5d19-443a-ad43-e9aa5d1a808c_1405x492.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!4OBi!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F476e9ade-5d19-443a-ad43-e9aa5d1a808c_1405x492.png)

**Producers with near-term cash flow** (Cameco, Kazatomprom, Paladin, Boss Energy) benefit from the term price moving higher, as they roll legacy contracts signed at lower prices into new contracts at current market levels. Cameco is the best-positioned: it is a miner, a converter, and (through its Westinghouse JV) a fuel fabricator, capturing value at three stages of the fuel chain. The premium multiple Cameco trades at relative to its peers reflects this integration.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!KzS9!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fb0a1cb9f-4b5f-4a6a-9ccd-bb444332f096_1405x528.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!KzS9!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fb0a1cb9f-4b5f-4a6a-9ccd-bb444332f096_1405x528.png)

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!lmGs!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd7938297-03e6-49b5-94e4-1628ef96c959_1405x471.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!lmGs!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd7938297-03e6-49b5-94e4-1628ef96c959_1405x471.png)

**Developers** (NexGen, Denison, Fission Uranium, Bannerman, Global Atomic, IsoEnergy, enCore) are leveraged bets on higher uranium prices and successful project execution. NexGen’s Arrow deposit in the Athabasca Basin is arguably the most important new uranium mine globally, with grades and scale that rival Cigar Lake. But it is still in permitting, and the road from discovery to production is long and full of risk. Developers are where you get the most upside if the cycle plays out, and the most downside if it doesn’t.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!y8GR!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F10fb1a58-1c51-44c9-ac33-6941ce96d65f_1405x516.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!y8GR!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F10fb1a58-1c51-44c9-ac33-6941ce96d65f_1405x516.png)

**Enrichment proxies** (Centrus Energy) capture the SWU pricing dynamic, which has a stronger structural case than uranium itself. As discussed in Part 3, Western enrichment capacity is the tightest bottleneck in the fuel chain, and Centrus is the only publicly traded Western enricher.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Yavd!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F0942b90e-e0ac-45c6-9141-1ab56e732a8b_1405x462.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Yavd!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F0942b90e-e0ac-45c6-9141-1ab56e732a8b_1405x462.png)

The key mental model for uranium investing is patience. Uranium cycles are long. The bull case took three years longer to play out than most analysts expected. Utilities will be the last actors to confirm the thesis through their contracting behavior. If you need to be right quickly, this is the wrong market. If you can be right slowly, the asymmetry is compelling.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!hwj3!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F9220a63c-d1af-4fa8-b51c-00457847b983_1405x781.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!hwj3!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F9220a63c-d1af-4fa8-b51c-00457847b983_1405x781.png)

## **Part 5: The Geopolitics of Nuclear Fuel**

### **5.1 The Map Is the Trade**

Every commodity has a geography. Oil has the Strait of Hormuz. Natural gas has the Yamal Peninsula. Copper has Chile. These are not footnotes in an investor presentation. They are the load-bearing walls of the global economy, and when they crack, everything built on top of them shifts.

Nuclear fuel has a geography too. And it is, to put it diplomatically, a disaster.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!51B1!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4d751c06-cc6c-4634-afe3-ec44ebf5941e_1405x775.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!51B1!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4d751c06-cc6c-4634-afe3-ec44ebf5941e_1405x775.png)

Forty percent of the world’s uranium comes from a landlocked Central Asian country sandwiched between Russia and China. Forty-four percent of the world’s enrichment capacity sits inside Russia. The only commercially operating conversion facility in the United States has a history of labor disputes, safety incidents, and extended shutdowns. The West’s newest major uranium mine, in Niger, was nationalized by a military junta that promptly invited Rosatom to the table.

If you designed a supply chain specifically to maximize geopolitical vulnerability, you could not do much better than the nuclear fuel cycle as it exists today. The question for investors is not whether this geography creates risk. It does. The question is whether the Western response to that risk, which is now funded, legislated, and accelerating, will succeed in time to matter. And who benefits while the transition unfolds.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!rsYb!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff66b3a78-8b87-4a52-a55b-d8a7d8ad1c33_1405x754.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!rsYb!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff66b3a78-8b87-4a52-a55b-d8a7d8ad1c33_1405x754.png)

### **5.2 Kazakhstan: The Saudi Arabia of Uranium, with a Russia Problem**

Kazakhstan produces 39-43% of the world’s uranium. This is not a typo. A single country, with a GDP smaller than Norway’s, supplies nearly half of the fuel that powers 10% of global electricity. The Saudi Arabia comparison is not hyperbole. It is structural. And like Saudi Arabia’s relationship with oil, Kazakhstan’s relationship with uranium is complicated by the fact that its two largest neighbors are the two countries the West is most actively trying to decouple from.

Kazatomprom, the state-owned producer, is the world’s largest uranium mining company. It produced 23,270 tonnes in 2024, a 10% increase year-on-year but still 7-9% below its own target.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!mAxy!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe9a367ef-d3f8-41b9-87dc-91da5edc6aaa_1405x780.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!mAxy!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe9a367ef-d3f8-41b9-87dc-91da5edc6aaa_1405x780.png)

In August 2024, Kazatomprom slashed its initial 2025 production intention of 30,500-31,500 tonnes (100% basis) down to a formal guidance range of 25,000-26,500 tonnes, an approximately 17% reduction driven by sulfuric acid supply constraints and delayed infrastructure construction at JV Budenovskoye (which missed its subsoil use agreement target by roughly 65-80%).

Actual 2025 output of approximately 25,839 tonnes fell within this revised guidance, so the company technically delivered on its downgraded plans. But the key point for investors is that Kazatomprom voluntarily lowered the bar, not that it missed it. The original intention of 30,500-31,500 tonnes was a subsoil use agreement entitlement, not a production forecast, and the gap between entitlement and actual output reveals structural constraints (acid supply, infrastructure delays) that are not resolving quickly.

For 2026, Kazatomprom reduced its subsoil use agreement production level by roughly 10% to 29,697 tonnes and signalled it would exercise its 20% downflex option, meaning actual 2026 production could come in as low as approximately 23,750 tonnes. The company explicitly cited “insufficient uncovered demand” to justify restraint, a polite way of saying “we are not going to pump into a market where our biggest customers are not signing contracts.”

The persistent shortfalls trace to a mundane but critical bottleneck: sulfuric acid. Kazakhstan’s ISR mining method requires enormous quantities of the stuff, roughly 1.85 million tonnes per year, and supply has been disrupted by agricultural demand competition and regional logistics problems. Acid costs rose approximately 33.6% over the course of 2023-2024, per Kazatomprom’s financial disclosures.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!QmXR!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4ed1213f-73fe-45e3-a77b-03db441556a1_1405x784.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!QmXR!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4ed1213f-73fe-45e3-a77b-03db441556a1_1405x784.png)

Kazatomprom is building a new 800,000-tonne-per-year acid plant, but commissioning has slipped to late 2026 at the earliest. Until then, production will remain constrained by chemistry, not geology.

**The Transit Problem**

Here is a detail that should keep Western fuel procurement officers awake at night. Roughly 90% of Kazakh uranium exports have historically traveled by rail through Russia to the port of St. Petersburg, and then by ship to Western buyers. In a world where the West is actively sanctioning Russian enrichment and trying to decouple from Russian supply chains, the inconvenient fact is that most of the “non-Russian” uranium the West buys from Kazakhstan *transits through Russia*.

The alternative is the Trans-Caspian International Transport Route, the so-called “Middle Corridor,” which ships uranium from the Kazakh port of Aktau across the Caspian Sea to Azerbaijan, then by rail through Georgia and Turkey to European ports. Cameco, which operates the Inkai JV in Kazakhstan, has publicly called this route “neither reliable nor predictable.” A test shipment in 2022 took months. Total Middle Corridor trade volumes actually dropped 37% from 2022 to 2023 due to congestion, pushing traffic back to the Russian route.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!aaxu!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F6db9f1c6-dbdc-4839-b2a2-11c8ff8a837f_1405x781.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!aaxu!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F6db9f1c6-dbdc-4839-b2a2-11c8ff8a837f_1405x781.png)

This is the kind of detail that never appears in a sell-side uranium thesis but matters enormously for risk pricing. Kazakhstan’s uranium is only as “Western-friendly” as the route it travels. And right now, the dominant route goes through the country the West is trying to cut off.

**China’s Quiet Takeover**

While the West debates transit routes, China is solving the problem differently: it is buying Kazakhstan outright.

More than 50-60% of Kazatomprom’s output now flows to Chinese buyers. CGN holds 49% stakes in multiple mining JVs. CNNC has invested in additional deposits. And in December 2024, in a transaction that received remarkably little Western media attention, Uranium One (Rosatom’s mining subsidiary) sold its 49.98% stake in JV Zarechnoye and 30% stakes in JVs Khorasan-U and Kyzylkum to Chinese state entities. That single transaction transferred roughly 4,831 tonnes of annual production capacity from Russian to Chinese control.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!KoRI!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F844967e2-79cf-4cd3-80de-9b8192452841_1405x742.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!KoRI!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F844967e2-79cf-4cd3-80de-9b8192452841_1405x742.png)

In 2024, Kazatomprom signed a series of major long-term supply agreements with Chinese state entities, reportedly valued at a substantial fraction of the company’s total book assets. This is not a one-off supply contract. It is a strategic alignment, reinforced by CGN’s 49% stakes in multiple mining JVs and the December 2024 transfer of Uranium One equity to Chinese buyers.

Kazakhstan is doing what small countries between empires have always done: playing both sides. President Tokayev maintains a “permanent comprehensive strategic partnership” with Beijing, bilateral trade hit a record $44 billion in 2024, and China was selected to lead Kazakhstan’s second and third nuclear power plants. Simultaneously, Kazakhstan refused to join BRICS+, declined to recognize Russian-occupied Ukrainian territories, and welcomed Macron for the first French presidential visit since 1994. The country held a nuclear power referendum in October 2024, which passed with 71% approval, but awarded the first reactor contract to Rosatom anyway.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!-EAL!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff69cca52-08d9-4c48-b9f8-b83c985b8fb1_1405x789.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!-EAL!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff69cca52-08d9-4c48-b9f8-b83c985b8fb1_1405x789.png)

For Western utilities, the message is uncomfortable: Kazakhstan’s uranium is increasingly Chinese uranium. Not because Beijing owns the deposits, but because it owns the offtake contracts, the JV equity stakes, and the political relationship. The assumption that Kazakh supply is “available” to the West needs to be stress-tested against the reality that China is locking it up.

### **5.3 Russia: The Enrichment Chokehold**

We covered the enrichment market structure in Part 3. Here is the geopolitical overlay.

Rosatom is not a company. It is a state within a state. It controls approximately 27 million SWU of enrichment capacity (40-44% of global total), supplies enrichment services to over 30 utilities across 16 countries, operates nuclear fuel fabrication for dozens of VVER reactors worldwide, builds reactors in Turkey, Egypt, Bangladesh, and India, and generates up to half of Russia’s foreign currency revenue outside hydrocarbons. It is, by any reasonable measure, Russia’s single most important strategic industrial asset after Gazprom.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!P5ws!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd61d6326-f35b-4c65-9e72-1dc1f7711d00_1405x764.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!P5ws!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd61d6326-f35b-4c65-9e72-1dc1f7711d00_1405x764.png)

The US Prohibiting Russian Uranium Imports Act, signed May 13, 2024, banned imports of Russian-origin low-enriched uranium effective August 11, 2024, with waiver provisions through January 1, 2028 and declining annual caps. The ban runs through 2040. Russia retaliated on November 15, 2024, imposing its own export ban on enriched uranium shipments to the US.

The European picture is messier. The EU has no hard ban on Russian nuclear fuel. Under the REPowerEU framework, Europe is “working toward” reducing dependence, and progress has been faster than expected: the share of EU enrichment sourced from Russia dropped from 38% in 2023 to 23% in 2024. But five EU member states still operate 19 Russian-designed VVER reactors that historically depended on specialized fuel assemblies made exclusively by Rosatom’s TVEL subsidiary. Bulgaria, Czech Republic, Finland, Slovakia, and Hungary all fall into this category.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!fkv_!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F2d71e70e-8b02-4a41-aa74-4d3adf595453_1405x670.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!fkv_!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F2d71e70e-8b02-4a41-aa74-4d3adf595453_1405x670.png)

The lock-in works like this. Rosatom does not just sell fuel. It sells an ecosystem. A country that buys a VVER reactor also buys VVER fuel assemblies (hexagonal geometry, incompatible with Western PWR fuel), which requires Russian enrichment, which is bundled with Russian conversion services, which creates a dependency chain that lasts the reactor’s entire operating lifetime (40-60 years). This is the nuclear equivalent of the razor-and-blade model, except the blades are enriched uranium and the switching cost is measured in billions.

Breaking that lock-in has been one of the most significant and underreported industrial achievements of the past two years. Westinghouse now manufactures VVER-1000 fuel at facilities in Sweden and the UK, and has been supplying Ukraine’s VVER fleet for over a decade. Bulgaria’s Kozloduy received its first Westinghouse VVER-1000 reload in April 2024. Czech Republic’s Temelín completed first full reload deliveries in June 2025. For the more challenging VVER-440 design, Westinghouse loaded first fuel at Finland’s Loviisa in late 2024, and Hungary signed a $114 million contract for VVER-440 fuel supply starting 2028. As of late 2024, 21 of 34 VVER reactors globally had fuel supply contracts outside Russia.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!7TJX!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fa383f79d-a82d-4703-9798-668d85947d8a_1405x779.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!7TJX!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fa383f79d-a82d-4703-9798-668d85947d8a_1405x779.png)

This is a quiet revolution. It does not make headlines like sanctions or tariffs. But it is systematically dismantling Rosatom’s most durable competitive moat, one fuel assembly at a time.

**The China Backdoor**

Here is the part that keeps compliance lawyers up at night.

Chinese shipments of enriched uranium to the United States surged from essentially zero in 2020-2022 to 242,990 kg in December 2023 alone (the same month the House passed the ban), with cumulative 2024 imports reaching approximately 123,894 kg by October 2024 per S&P Global reporting. The timing is difficult to dismiss as coincidental. Simultaneously, China’s imports of Russian enriched uranium tripled in the first ten months of 2024, reaching $849 million.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!4xQm!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F3764efe2-cc07-49d0-81a7-ad4c84951fd8_1405x720.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!4xQm!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F3764efe2-cc07-49d0-81a7-ad4c84951fd8_1405x720.png)

The pattern is obvious to anyone with a pulse. China imports Russian enriched uranium, feeds it to its own reactors, and frees up Chinese-enriched material for export to Western utilities at non-Russian prices. The DOE, Commerce Department, and National Security Council are investigating. The US ban includes anti-circumvention provisions that make such swaps illegal if designed to evade the law, but proving intent requires access to confidential fuel contracts, and good luck with that.

For investors, this has two implications. First, it means the effective Russia ban is leakier than it appears on paper, which partially explains why SWU prices have not spiked even more violently. Second, if the US government closes the backdoor (which it is actively exploring), the supply squeeze on Western enrichment tightens further, and SWU prices go higher.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!rydx!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Febf03d5b-0736-43df-89f8-76726564ce84_1405x513.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!rydx!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Febf03d5b-0736-43df-89f8-76726564ce84_1405x513.png)

**What Russia Could Do Next**

Russia’s November 2024 export ban on enriched uranium shipments to the US was a retaliatory gesture, not a strategic escalation. The US had already banned Russian imports; Russia simply formalized the reciprocal. But the question investors should be asking is not what Russia has done. It is what Russia could do if it chose to weaponize its enrichment dominance more aggressively.

Scenario one: VVER fuel embargo on non-aligned states. Russia continues supplying VVER fuel to Hungary (a political ally) but restricts or prices up fuel deliveries to Bulgaria, Czech Republic, and Slovakia, all of which have been actively transitioning to Westinghouse alternatives. The goal would be to punish defection and slow the fuel diversification campaign. The practical impact would be modest (Westinghouse VVER fuel supply is already ramping), but the signaling effect could accelerate European enrichment and conversion procurement, tightening those markets further.

Scenario two: Kazakhstan transit disruption. Russia does not need to formally block Kazakh uranium shipments transiting its territory. It simply needs to create “administrative delays,” customs complications, rail scheduling “challenges,” or inspection requirements that slow shipments by weeks or months. Given that roughly 90% of Kazakh uranium exports historically travel through Russia, even a moderate increase in transit friction would tighten Western spot availability and force utilities to draw down inventories faster. This is the scenario that is hardest to attribute, hardest to sanction, and most likely to actually happen. It may, in fact, already be happening; Cameco has reported transit delays from Inkai that are difficult to fully explain by logistics alone.

Scenario three: dumping enriched uranium into the Chinese market at below-cost pricing, effectively subsidizing China’s nuclear program while simultaneously flooding the channel through which Chinese-origin SWU reaches Western utilities. If Russia sells SWU to China at $50/SWU while Western spot is $193, China can re-export at $150 and still make a handsome margin, while Western enrichers face price competition from material that is, at root, Russian-subsidized. This would compress Western SWU pricing without resolving the underlying capacity deficit, squeezing Centrus and Urenco margins while leaving the strategic vulnerability intact.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!vmlg!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc9c902d0-7806-4322-be14-6275c1ec3584_1405x786.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!vmlg!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc9c902d0-7806-4322-be14-6275c1ec3584_1405x786.png)

None of these scenarios is certain. All are within Russia’s operational capability. The point is not to predict which one materializes but to stress-test the thesis against a world where Russia does not passively accept the loss of its most valuable non-hydrocarbon export franchise. Rosatom generated substantial foreign currency revenue from enrichment services. Losing that revenue stream to Western sanctions and EU disengagement gives Moscow an incentive to disrupt the transition, not facilitate it.

The investment implication: the geopolitical risk premium in Western enrichment and conversion pricing is not a temporary dislocation. It is a structural feature of a market where the largest supplier has both the capability and the incentive to create friction. Pricing that premium correctly is the difference between a good trade and a great one.

An honest note: the enrichment thesis is the section of this primer where my own analytical priors most perfectly align with the bullish conclusion, and that is precisely when rigorous self-interrogation is most necessary. The counterarguments deserve explicit airing. Utility inventories may be larger and more resilient than public data suggests; utilities do not disclose fuel inventories in detail, and most US utilities hold 2 to 3 years of fuel supply, providing more runway than the headline deficit implies. Third-country processing of Russian material through intermediaries could prove more substantial and more persistent than currently assumed. And demand growth projections for new reactors may slip right alongside their construction timelines, reducing the future SWU requirement. The enrichment gap is real. Whether it is as severe as the pure arithmetic suggests depends on assumptions about inventory levels that are, by their nature, opaque.

### **5.4 Niger: A Supply Source That Disappeared Overnight**

Niger was never the largest uranium producer, but it was a meaningful one, roughly 4-5% of global supply, and critically important to France, which sourced roughly a quarter of its EU uranium imports from Nigerien mines.

Then the military coup happened on July 26, 2023. And the uranium supply went to zero.

The timeline is bleak. Orano halted production at its SOMAÏR mine on October 31, 2024, citing inability to export and losses exceeding $200 million. By December 2024, Orano confirmed it had lost all operational control. The junta formally nationalized SOMAÏR on June 20, 2025. An unauthorized shipment of roughly 1,000 tonnes of yellowcake left the mine without Orano’s knowledge in November 2025 and got stranded at Niamey’s international airport, unable to transit through hostile neighboring countries, where it became a sitting target for an IS Sahel Province attack in January 2026.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!nPJF!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5cabb293-0a9b-4129-a671-09edddaa90aa_1405x786.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!nPJF!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5cabb293-0a9b-4129-a671-09edddaa90aa_1405x786.png)

France’s relationship with Niger is effectively severed. All French troops withdrew by December 2023. The embassy is closed. Niger has joined the Alliance of Sahel States, withdrawn from ECOWAS, and signed an MoU with Rosatom and a mining cooperation agreement with Uranium One. CNNC has been studying the Azelik mine restart.

The Imouraren deposit, one of the world’s largest undeveloped uranium resources at roughly 174,000-200,000 tonnes, had its operating permit revoked. Canada’s GoviEx lost its Madaouela permit.

For the uranium market, Niger is a write-off until the political situation changes, and there is no indication that it will anytime soon. The practical impact has been manageable because Niger’s production was already declining and utility contracts provided buffer. But it removes optionality from the supply side at exactly the wrong time.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!X1yg!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4466474c-242a-4167-be6c-533cedd9b570_1405x618.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!X1yg!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4466474c-242a-4167-be6c-533cedd9b570_1405x618.png)

### **5.5 Australia: The Sleeping Giant**

Australia holds 28-35% of the world’s recoverable uranium resources. It is, by a wide margin, the most resource-rich uranium jurisdiction on Earth. BHP’s Olympic Dam alone contains over 2.5 million tonnes, the single largest deposit anywhere.

Australia produced roughly 4,500-5,100 tonnes in 2024. That is about 8-9% of global output. From a country with a third of the world’s reserves.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!avdr!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc3ef6818-3840-4553-b7ec-066f4828239d_1405x785.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!avdr!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc3ef6818-3840-4553-b7ec-066f4828239d_1405x785.png)

The gap between reserves and production is entirely political. The May 2025 federal election was a landslide for Labor, which won 94 seats (a record for any single party) and comprehensively defeated Opposition Leader Peter Dutton, who lost his own seat partly on the back of his deeply unpopular proposal to build seven nuclear power plants. Nuclear power remains banned in Australia under the EPBC Act. Western Australia bans new uranium mines under state Labor policy.

Boss Energy’s Honeymoon ISR mine is the bright spot, having restarted in April 2024 and declared commercial production in January 2025 with cash costs of just $23/lb. But Australia will not be the swing producer that saves the Western supply chain. The political constraints are structural, not cyclical, and no amount of uranium bull market will change them within the investment horizon that matters.

For investors, the Australian resource base is a floor under the long-term uranium thesis (the material exists and can eventually be accessed), but it is not a near-term supply solution.

### **5.6 Canada: The West’s Last Best Hope**

If you are looking for a jurisdiction that combines world-class geology, stable rule of law, friendly relations with every major Western ally, an experienced regulatory apparatus, and active political support for uranium mining, your list has one entry: Saskatchewan, Canada.

The Athabasca Basin hosts uranium grades 100 times the world average. Cameco’s McArthur River/Key Lake mill set a world record in 2024, packaging 20.3 million pounds of U₃O₈ on a 100% basis, the highest annual production from any uranium mill in history. Cigar Lake continues producing at planned levels. Cameco holds roughly 230 million pounds in committed long-term contracts and operates at an average realized sale price north of $79/lb.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!G686!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fb8ce425a-25ef-4354-a8f0-5452769718f7_1405x666.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!G686!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fb8ce425a-25ef-4354-a8f0-5452769718f7_1405x666.png)

Two transformative new projects are clearing final permitting hurdles:

NexGen Energy’s Arrow deposit contains 256.7 million pounds in measured and indicated resources. At projected average production of 28.8 million pounds per year in years 1-5, it would represent roughly 20% of current global fuel supply from a single mine. The Canadian Nuclear Safety Commission approved the construction licence in early 2026. NexGen holds over $800 million in cash and targets construction start in summer 2026 with a four-year build timeline.

Denison Mines’ Wheeler River (Phoenix ISR) received its construction licence in late 2025/early 2026, the first new uranium mine approved in Canada in approximately 20 years. The feasibility study shows an after-tax IRR of 90%, a 10-month payback, and operating costs of $6.28/lb. First production is targeted for mid-2028.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!mhnK!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F13d24eed-a3e1-4125-aedd-37049bd67383_1405x785.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!mhnK!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F13d24eed-a3e1-4125-aedd-37049bd67383_1405x785.png)

Canada is where the “friend-shoring” thesis becomes investable. The country is expanding both production (Cameco, NexGen, Denison) and fuel cycle services (Port Hope conversion, Cameco/Brookfield’s Westinghouse JV for fuel fabrication), and it is doing so in a regulatory environment that is actively scaling to accommodate the pipeline. Saskatchewan proclaimed its first Nuclear Science Week in October 2025 and released a nuclear-powered electricity strategy. The province wants to be the nuclear fuel capital of the West, and it is making the investments to back that ambition.

### **5.7 The Friend-Shoring Architecture: $5.6 Billion and Counting**

The Western response to the Russia/China fuel supply challenge has moved from rhetoric to funded programs at a speed that is, by the standards of nuclear policy, genuinely impressive.

The core alliance is the Sapporo 5 (US, Canada, France, Japan, UK), formed at the April 2023 G7 Ministers’ Meeting, which has mobilized $5.6 billion as of September 2024 to establish a Russia-free commercial fuel supply chain. These five nations collectively control roughly half of world uranium conversion and enrichment capacity.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!97kC!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F59020a4e-a182-4594-985b-808731205334_1405x779.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!97kC!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F59020a4e-a182-4594-985b-808731205334_1405x779.png)

The US alone has committed $2.72 billion in appropriated funding for domestic enrichment. In January 2026, DOE awarded three major task orders: Centrus Energy ($900 million for HALEU enrichment at Piketon, Ohio, first capacity by 2029), General Matter ($900 million for a novel-technology facility at the former Paducah plant in Kentucky, targeting 2034), and Orano Federal Services ($900 million for “Project IKE,” a $5 billion LEU enrichment facility in Oak Ridge, Tennessee, targeting initial production by 2031).

Uranium was added to the USGS Final 2025 List of Critical Minerals, reversing its removal by the Biden administration. A Section 232 national security investigation on uranium and processed critical minerals was initiated in April 2025.

On the enrichment side, Orano inaugurated a €1.7 billion expansion of Georges Besse II in October 2024, boosting capacity by 30% to 10 million SWU/year. Urenco is adding 1.8 million SWU across its four plants, with new cascades online in 2025 and full completion by 2027. Urenco’s order book reached €20.1 billion as of mid-2025, extending through 2040.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!kFEX!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fcab49365-9620-4267-8ad7-826d4029f617_1405x793.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!kFEX!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fcab49365-9620-4267-8ad7-826d4029f617_1405x793.png)

The numbers are real. The funding is appropriated. The construction is underway. But there is a transition gap of 5-8 years before new Western enrichment and mining capacity can materially replace Russian SWU. During that gap, the market remains tight, prices remain elevated, and the companies positioned on the right side of the geopolitical divide capture disproportionate value.

### **5.8 China: The 800-Pound Gorilla**

We have danced around China in every section of this primer. It is time to confront it directly.

China operates approximately 59-60 reactors with roughly 62 GW of installed capacity. Another 28-33 reactors are under construction, the largest pipeline on Earth. The country has approved 10 or more new reactor units annually for four consecutive years. The 15th Five-Year Plan targets 110 GW by 2030 and 150 GW by 2035, requiring a sustained build rate of roughly 10 GW per year.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!I0FS!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fb9a6acd0-e431-4319-b313-0d0e0cbd9b7d_1405x770.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!I0FS!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fb9a6acd0-e431-4319-b313-0d0e0cbd9b7d_1405x770.png)

China’s uranium demand, currently about 13,130 tonnes per year, is projected to reach roughly 21,000 tonnes by 2030 and 26,800 tonnes by 2035. Domestic mining covers only 13-15% of needs. The rest comes from imports (Kazakhstan supplies 66%), overseas equity mines (Namibia’s Husab and Rössing), and open market purchases. Beijing holds a strategic uranium stockpile estimated at 132,000 tonnes, roughly triple the US stockpile and sufficient for approximately 10 years of current fleet operations.

On enrichment, CNNC operates an estimated 8-11 million SWU of capacity with two new plants started in 2023. The upper-bound projection from Ux Consulting suggests capacity could reach 23 million SWU by 2030, though most independent analysts (IPFM, various consultancies) project 13-17 million SWU as more realistic. Even the lower range would make China largely self-sufficient; the higher range could position it as a net exporter. The uncertainty in this figure is material for the Western enrichment thesis, since it determines whether Chinese overcapacity eventually spills into global markets.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!7_4t!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F43383f6a-8e1c-4d4b-a9ff-65e291ad4408_1405x708.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!7_4t!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F43383f6a-8e1c-4d4b-a9ff-65e291ad4408_1405x708.png)

CNNC’s fuel cycle ambitions extend to reprocessing, MOX fabrication, and fast reactors. The world’s first thorium molten-salt reactor (TMSR-LF1) achieved first criticality in October 2023 and confirmed in-reactor breeding of U-233 from thorium in November 2025.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!MtYL!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F845db0d7-b51e-45d4-aa46-234e078b6f13_1405x780.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!MtYL!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F845db0d7-b51e-45d4-aa46-234e078b6f13_1405x780.png)

China’s reactor export program has underperformed relative to ambitions (actual completed exports are limited to Pakistan’s seven units, and the UK’s Bradwell B Hualong One project was cancelled). But where China’s nuclear program most affects global markets is not through exports. It is through sheer domestic demand. Chinese procurement is redirecting uranium flows eastward, Chinese enrichment expansion is absorbing SWU that might otherwise reach Western markets, and Chinese intermediation of Russian enriched material raises the specter of sanctions circumvention.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Q1WJ!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5742a21b-3879-44a8-95de-9af1c03fc651_1405x789.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Q1WJ!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5742a21b-3879-44a8-95de-9af1c03fc651_1405x789.png)

The 132,000-tonne strategic stockpile is the number that should give every Western fuel procurement officer pause. It means China can weather any supply disruption for a decade. It means Chinese utilities can afford to be patient in contract negotiations, waiting for Western desperation to drive prices up before releasing material. It is the uranium equivalent of the Strategic Petroleum Reserve, except China actually built one and the West did not.

#### **5.8.1 India: The Thorium Wild Card**

India is the analytical gap in most Western nuclear primers, and the reason is uncomfortable: India’s nuclear program does not fit neatly into either the Western friend-shoring narrative or the Chinese/Russian parallel ecosystem. It sits in between, strategically non-aligned, operationally constrained, and sitting on a geological asset that could, in theory, rewrite the nuclear fuel equation entirely.

India operates 23 reactors with roughly 8 GW of installed capacity. Another 8 reactors are under construction, and the government targets 22 GW by 2031-32 and 100 GW by 2047. These are ambitious numbers against a track record of persistent delays and chronic underperformance relative to plan. India has been “about to scale up nuclear” for roughly three decades, and the gap between announcement and execution has been consistently wide. The Kudankulam VVER-1000 units (built with Russian cooperation) took over a decade each. Indigenous PHWR (Pressurized Heavy Water Reactor, essentially a CANDU derivative) construction has improved but still averages 7-10 years per unit.

The strategic wrinkle is that India’s nuclear program was built outside the Non-Proliferation Treaty. India tested nuclear weapons in 1974 and 1998, and was excluded from the Nuclear Suppliers Group until the landmark US-India Civil Nuclear Agreement of 2008, which granted India access to international nuclear commerce in exchange for placing its civilian (but not military) reactors under IAEA safeguards. This means India’s fuel supply chain is bifurcated: civilian reactors can import uranium (from Canada, Kazakhstan, Australia, and others), while military reactors rely on domestic production. The country’s domestic uranium mining output covers only a fraction of civilian needs, roughly 25-30% by most estimates, and India imports the rest.

For the global uranium market, India’s expansion adds roughly 2,000-4,000 tonnes of incremental annual demand by 2035 under realistic (not aspirational) build-out scenarios. This is not transformative at the scale of China’s 26,800-tonne projected demand, but it is additive at exactly the wrong time for a supply-constrained market.

Now, the thorium question.

India holds a significant share of the world’s known thorium resources, commonly cited at 25-30% in Indian government and older IAEA estimates, though more recent compilations using stricter reserve-category definitions place the figure closer to the low-to-mid teens. Total Indian resources are estimated at roughly 1.1 million tonnes in the Keral, Jharkhand, and Rajasthan coastal sands.

Thorium is three to four times more abundant than uranium in the Earth’s crust. It is not, however, fissile. You cannot put thorium in a reactor and get a chain reaction. Thorium-232 must first absorb a neutron and transmute into uranium-233, which is fissile. This means thorium is a “fertile” material (like U-238), not a fuel in itself. To use it, you need a reactor that can breed U-233 from thorium, which requires either a fast neutron spectrum or a specialized thermal breeder design.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!4u5Q!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fdef3416a-2bbf-4e41-bfd2-1481e422a9c5_1405x782.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!4u5Q!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fdef3416a-2bbf-4e41-bfd2-1481e422a9c5_1405x782.png)

India designed its nuclear program around a three-stage strategy to exploit this thorium endowment. Stage one: pressurized heavy water reactors fueled by natural uranium, producing plutonium in the spent fuel. Stage two: fast breeder reactors fueled by plutonium, breeding U-233 from a thorium blanket. Stage three: advanced heavy water reactors fueled by thorium-U-233, achieving a self-sustaining thorium fuel cycle.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!zXqK!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F534acd43-89dc-4e8c-ac9e-3db766e9ee3c_1405x782.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!zXqK!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F534acd43-89dc-4e8c-ac9e-3db766e9ee3c_1405x782.png)

The program is decades behind schedule. The Prototype Fast Breeder Reactor (PFBR) at Kalpakkam, the linchpin of Stage two, has been under construction since 2004 and has been “nearing completion” for roughly a decade. First criticality is now targeted for 2025-2026, roughly 15 years late. Stage three remains theoretical.

China’s TMSR-LF1, the world’s first thorium molten-salt reactor, achieved first criticality in October 2023 and reported successful in-reactor conversion of thorium to U-233 in November 2025, though the conversion ratio and precise experimental parameters have not been independently published.

This is significant not because it makes thorium commercially viable tomorrow (it does not; TMSR-LF1 is a 2 MW thermal test reactor, not a power plant), but because it demonstrates the physics at reactor scale for the first time, and China, not India, achieved it first despite India having the larger thorium endowment and the longer head start.

For investors, the thorium question resolves simply: thorium is a 2040s-and-beyond technology. No commercial thorium power reactor exists or is under construction anywhere on Earth. India’s three-stage program has been running for 70 years and has not reached Stage two. China’s molten-salt program is a decade from commercial demonstration. Thorium does not affect the uranium supply-demand balance within any investment horizon that matters for this cycle.

What does matter is India’s conventional uranium demand, which is growing, and India’s geopolitical positioning, which is uniquely flexible. India buys Russian reactors (Kudankulam) and Russian enrichment. It also buys Canadian uranium (Cameco), Australian uranium, and French reactor technology (EDF has proposed EPR units at Jaitapur, which would be the world’s largest nuclear complex if built). India signed the COP28 nuclear tripling pledge. It is a member of the Quad (with the US, Japan, and Australia) but maintains deep economic ties with Russia.

For the friend-shoring thesis, India is neither friend nor adversary. It is an independent demand centre that will source fuel from whoever offers the best deal, which means it competes with Western utilities for the same Kazakh and Canadian supply while simultaneously absorbing Russian fuel that might otherwise need to find alternative markets. India’s nuclear expansion, if it actually executes at anything close to planned pace, tightens the global uranium and enrichment balance without contributing to Western supply chain diversification. It is additive to demand and neutral-to-negative for Western supply security.

### **5.9 The Parallel Ecosystem Risk: China as Nuclear Exporter**

The primer has so far treated China as a demand centre and a supply chain variable. But there is a scenario that could reshape the geopolitical map of nuclear energy within the next decade: China becoming a full-spectrum nuclear exporter to the Global South, effectively creating a parallel nuclear ecosystem that competes with the Western friend-shoring architecture.

Russia perfected this model over two decades. Rosatom does not just sell reactors. It sells an integrated package: sovereign financing (Russian state loans covering up to 90% of construction costs), lifetime fuel supply (from Russian enrichment and fabrication, creating permanent dependency), training and workforce development, waste take-back arrangements, and strategic political alignment.

This model has been extraordinarily effective. Rosatom has reactor construction contracts or proposals in Turkey, Egypt, Bangladesh, India, Hungary, and several other countries. The lock-in mechanism (VVER fuel dependency) creates relationships that outlast individual political cycles. China now possesses every component needed to replicate this model, and in some areas it is already ahead of Russia.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!9GFB!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F344dde4f-a1ff-4166-9a16-39609d3bc128_1405x788.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!9GFB!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F344dde4f-a1ff-4166-9a16-39609d3bc128_1405x788.png)

On reactor technology, the Hualong One is a proven 1,000+ MWe PWR with 41 units operational or under construction and 100% domestic supply chain localization achieved in 2024. The Linglong One SMR, on track for 2026 completion, provides a smaller-scale export option for countries whose grids cannot absorb a large reactor. The Hualong Two, under development, targets a four-year build time and 25% cost reduction.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!hnyI!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F3908cb5d-641a-471f-bcf9-e0a84a49d828_1404x747.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!hnyI!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F3908cb5d-641a-471f-bcf9-e0a84a49d828_1404x747.png)

On financing, China Development Bank and Export-Import Bank of China have collectively financed hundreds of billions in Belt and Road infrastructure. Nuclear project finance, at $5-10 billion per reactor, is well within their capacity, and Beijing can offer concessional terms that Western export credit agencies cannot match.

On the fuel cycle, CNNC operates 8-11 million SWU of enrichment capacity targeting 23 million SWU by 2030. China has conversion, fabrication, and a reprocessing capability that enables it to offer a cradle-to-grave fuel cycle service. The 132,000-tonne strategic uranium stockpile provides buffer for fuel supply commitments to export customers. On construction workforce, China has maintained continuous construction (37 reactors in 10 years, 32 more underway) and has the only nuclear construction workforce in the world that is simultaneously expanding in both scale and capability.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!-zLG!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fa9d4dcd2-c37e-4602-b7fd-e30e6800c165_1403x788.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!-zLG!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fa9d4dcd2-c37e-4602-b7fd-e30e6800c165_1403x788.png)

The target market is countries that want nuclear power but cannot afford Western prices, cannot access Western financing, and are not geopolitically aligned with the US-led friend-shoring alliance. The pipeline is more concrete than most Western analysts appreciate.

The UAE is the proof of concept. Barakah’s four APR-1400 units are operational, generating roughly 40 TWh per year, approximately 25% of the UAE’s total electricity. This is the first new nuclear power program successfully completed in the developing world in decades, and it happened on something close to schedule and budget. The UAE now has a trained nuclear workforce, an operational regulator (FANR), and a demonstrated ability to integrate large-scale nuclear into a grid that was previously 100% gas-fired. Barakah’s fuel demand, roughly 400-500 tonnes of enriched uranium per year across four units, is fully contracted through KEPCO’s supply chain, but those contracts will eventually roll, adding to the global term market.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!acuo!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F920491cc-f15a-4e70-915b-e00dc825324c_1405x621.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!acuo!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F920491cc-f15a-4e70-915b-e00dc825324c_1405x621.png)

Saudi Arabia is further behind but moving with purpose. The Kingdom selected France’s EDF for its first large reactor project in early 2025, a two-unit EPR at a Red Sea coastal site, with target operations in the mid-2030s. Riyadh’s nuclear ambitions are intertwined with its broader energy transition strategy (Vision 2030) and its desire to free up domestically consumed oil and gas for export. Saudi Arabia has also engaged with China, South Korea, and the US on reactor technology, playing the same multi-vendor game that the UAE played before selecting KEPCO. If Saudi builds 4-6 large reactors by 2040 (the upper end of publicly discussed plans), that adds roughly 4,000-6,000 tonnes of annual enriched uranium demand.

Egypt’s El Dabaa project, four VVER-1200 reactors being built by Rosatom with $25 billion in Russian financing, is the live example of the Rosatom lock-in model in action. First unit targeted for 2028-2030. Turkey’s Akkuyu (also Rosatom) is under construction with first unit commissioning targeted for 2025-2026. Bangladesh’s Rooppur (Rosatom again) is advancing. Each of these projects adds roughly 200-250 tonnes of enriched uranium demand per reactor per year, locked into Rosatom’s fuel supply chain for the reactor’s lifetime.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!m6ec!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fdc3426b5-4769-4503-a2c0-70a02200ed43_1405x788.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!m6ec!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fdc3426b5-4769-4503-a2c0-70a02200ed43_1405x788.png)

Vietnam revived its nuclear plans in late 2024, reversing a 2016 cancellation. Indonesia and the Philippines are both exploring SMRs as a pathway to nuclear that avoids the massive capital commitment of large reactors. Kenya, Ghana, and Nigeria have all engaged with the IAEA on nuclear power feasibility studies. Argentina has cooperated with China on small reactor technology and operates two CANDU-derivative reactors.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!_tY7!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc671cc82-0ab3-4b6e-a1b1-0ae0f98c894d_1405x789.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!_tY7!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc671cc82-0ab3-4b6e-a1b1-0ae0f98c894d_1405x789.png)

Across Southeast Asia, Sub-Saharan Africa, the Middle East, and parts of Latin America, there are 15 to 20 countries where nuclear deployment is plausible within the next decade, and another 10-15 where it is possible within two decades. The aggregate demand implication is not trivial. If even half these programs execute at their most conservative projections, they add 15,000-25,000 tonnes of incremental annual uranium demand by 2040, roughly 10-15% above current global consumption. The question is not whether the Global South will go nuclear. It is who builds the reactors, who supplies the fuel, and whether that fuel comes from the Western ecosystem or the Chinese/Russian one.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!-I05!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd347995d-4c25-4483-baa5-5c7044e01daa_1405x725.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!-I05!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd347995d-4c25-4483-baa5-5c7044e01daa_1405x725.png)

The investment implications cut in several directions, not all of them bearish. Uranium demand increases regardless of who builds the reactor. Each Hualong One requires approximately 200 tonnes of enriched uranium per year. Twenty export reactors add roughly 10 million lbs of annual U3O8 demand. This is additive to the supply-demand balance. Enrichment demand bifurcates rather than loosens.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!oQSR!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd7e5e48b-0e90-4287-8e8d-1d8937e03a89_1403x789.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!oQSR!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd7e5e48b-0e90-4287-8e8d-1d8937e03a89_1403x789.png)

Chinese-exported reactors will use Chinese-enriched fuel, which means China’s enrichment expansion (from 9 million to 23 million SWU) may be consumed by export obligations rather than freeing up SWU for Western spot markets. This is actually bullish for Western enrichment pricing, because it removes the scenario where Chinese overcapacity spills westward. The friend-shoring premium widens. If the nuclear world bifurcates into a Western ecosystem and a Chinese/Russian ecosystem, the geopolitical premium on Western-aligned assets increases. Canadian uranium, Urenco/Orano enrichment, and Westinghouse/Framatome fabrication become even more strategically critical.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!G5s8!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F842ae5ec-a1e8-448b-8b06-62f9500020d8_1405x621.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!G5s8!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F842ae5ec-a1e8-448b-8b06-62f9500020d8_1405x621.png)

But SMR competition intensifies. If the Linglong One is successfully exported at Chinese construction costs (roughly $2.5-3 billion per unit), Western SMR developers face a competitor that can deliver at a fraction of the cost. The Western SMR thesis depends on achieving NOAK economics that may never be competitive with Chinese FOAK economics. This is the dynamic that killed Western solar panel manufacturing, and it could repeat in nuclear. The counter-argument is that China’s actual nuclear export track record is modest. Completed exports are limited to Pakistan’s seven units.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!AuA-!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F602b51b3-3022-4a33-a8ae-00f4f75899f7_1403x761.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!AuA-!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F602b51b3-3022-4a33-a8ae-00f4f75899f7_1403x761.png)

The UK cancelled CGN’s participation in Bradwell B on security grounds. Many countries are reluctant to accept Chinese infrastructure dependency after Belt and Road debt controversies. Nuclear exports require decades of regulatory engagement, safety case development, and workforce training that China has not yet demonstrated outside Pakistan. But the capability exists. The financing exists. The political will exists. Investors who assume the Western friend-shoring architecture faces no competition from an integrated Chinese nuclear export ecosystem are making an assumption that deserves explicit stress-testing.

### **5.10 The Geopolitical Scorecard**

Let us summarize the board.

Kazakhstan: 39% of global production, increasingly locked into Chinese offtake, transit-dependent on Russia, persistent production shortfalls from acid constraints. Verdict: the swing producer, but swinging toward Beijing.

Russia: 40-44% of enrichment, sanctioned by the US, losing EU market share, retaliating with export bans, but still structurally irreplaceable until the early 2030s. Verdict: diminishing but dangerous.

Niger: nationalized, zero production for Western markets, aligned with Russia and potentially China. Verdict: write-off.

Australia: world’s largest reserves, politically paralyzed, modest production. Verdict: long-term option value, not near-term supply.

Canada: world-class deposits, stable jurisdiction, active regulatory support, two transformative projects in final permitting. Verdict: the West’s most important uranium supplier for the next two decades.

China: massive demand growth, expanding domestic fuel cycle, strategic stockpile of 132,000 tonnes, quietly consolidating control over Kazakh supply. Verdict: the biggest variable in the entire nuclear fuel equation.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!_91c!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F45228534-ef5e-4529-af5e-eb742fcb973d_1403x699.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!_91c!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F45228534-ef5e-4529-af5e-eb742fcb973d_1403x699.png)

The investment implication is straightforward. Companies with assets in politically aligned, geographically safe jurisdictions (Canada, Australia, to a lesser extent the US) will trade at structural premiums to companies with assets in contested or unstable jurisdictions (Niger, Uzbekistan, Kazakhstan). The “jurisdiction premium” in uranium equities is not a temporary risk-off trade. It is a structural repricing that reflects the new geopolitical reality of nuclear fuel supply.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Qyup!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe00a9c35-8170-4bd8-8ccc-f9f7e051f142_1404x694.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Qyup!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe00a9c35-8170-4bd8-8ccc-f9f7e051f142_1404x694.png)

The friend-shoring of nuclear fuel is not a slogan. It is a $5.6 billion funded program backed by five governments with legislation, appropriations, and construction timelines. The companies positioned to supply that program, Cameco, NexGen, Denison, Urenco, Orano, Centrus, Westinghouse, are not just commodity producers. They are strategic assets in a geopolitical realignment that will take a decade to play out and will define the economics of nuclear power for a generation.

## **Part 6: The New Build Landscape**

### **6.1 Why Can’t the West Build Things Anymore?**

This is the section where we stop being polite.

Between 2015 and 2024, China built 37 nuclear reactors with an average construction time of 6.3 years from first concrete to grid connection. The fastest took 4.1 years. The cost per unit averaged roughly $2.7 billion.

For context, the global average construction time over the same period was 9.4 years per IAEA PRIS data, meaning China was building roughly 33% faster than the rest of the world combined. This gap is not a function of lower safety standards (China’s National Nuclear Safety Administration applies IAEA requirements and conducts peer reviews). It is a function of continuous construction, design standardization, and an intact supply chain, which is precisely the institutional coherence argument we will develop in detail below.

China currently has 32 reactors under construction, approved 10 more in April 2025, and has 102 reactors either operating, under construction, or approved with a combined capacity of 113 GW. CNNC is already developing the Hualong Two, a second-generation variant of its flagship design that will reduce build time from five years to four and cut costs by a quarter.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!ueeR!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F1b5e3b40-8d61-411f-ac71-c56956a25225_1888x1060.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!ueeR!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F1b5e3b40-8d61-411f-ac71-c56956a25225_1888x1060.png)

Now let us look at the West. You have seen the headlines in Part 2. Here are the numbers that explain them.

The United States completed Vogtle Units 3 and 4: two AP1000 reactors, 7 years late, $36.8 billion (2.6x the original budget). Westinghouse went bankrupt during construction. A parallel project at V.C. Summer in South Carolina was abandoned with $9 billion in sunk costs.

France’s Flamanville 3: one EPR, 17 years from first concrete to full power, cost ballooned from €3.3 billion to €23.7 billion including interest. France had not opened a new reactor since 2002.

Finland’s Olkiluoto 3: one EPR, 17 years, cost rose from €3.2 billion to roughly €8.5 billion.

Hinkley Point C in the UK: two EPRs, enabling works from 2017 with first nuclear concrete in December 2018, now targeting 2030 at earliest, cost estimate risen to £35 billion ($46 billion) from an original £18 billion.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!4aQx!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F717afbd3-9631-40d2-9e90-68470e723cfe_1600x888.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!4aQx!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F717afbd3-9631-40d2-9e90-68470e723cfe_1600x888.png)

The pattern is not subtle. China builds a reactor in 5 years for $2.7 billion. The West builds one in 12-17 years for $15-25 billion. This is not a technology gap. The physics is the same. The concrete is the same. The steel is the same. The fuel is the same. The gap is institutional, regulatory, and organizational. And until you understand *why* this gap exists, you cannot assess whether the “nuclear renaissance” in the West is real or just another round of expensive disappointment.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!kNUa!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F1db0d09c-fda5-45b9-9ce7-90a66c27ef3b_1882x1036.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!kNUa!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F1db0d09c-fda5-45b9-9ce7-90a66c27ef3b_1882x1036.png)

### **6.2 The Coordination Freeze: Why Nobody Moves First**

One of the most incisive analyses of this dysfunction appeared in an essay by John Arden in *American Affairs* in late 2025, titled “The Sovereign Signal: Nuclear Energy as Strategic Infrastructure.” Arden’s argument, stripped to its essentials, is this:

Nuclear energy in the United States is not suffering from a technology problem, a capital problem, or even a political will problem. It is suffering from a *classification problem*. The US has been trying to deploy nuclear power using the institutional framework designed for market-based energy commodities (solar, wind, natural gas), when nuclear is actually sovereign infrastructure, more analogous to the Interstate Highway System, missile defense, or the Federal Reserve. The frame itself produces paralysis.

Arden models the dysfunction as a multiplayer coordination game. Every actor in the nuclear deployment chain has a rational reason to wait. Developers hesitate without guaranteed demand. Fuel suppliers delay without volume commitments. Investors require anchor customers. Utilities demand regulatory certainty. Regulators resist political exposure. Each player sees the risk of going first more clearly than the reward, and so nobody moves. The Nash equilibrium is stasis.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!InKm!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F8dece735-90c2-4f1d-95c0-05d2aacefd02_1888x888.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!InKm!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F8dece735-90c2-4f1d-95c0-05d2aacefd02_1888x888.png)

In game theory terms, this is a stag hunt. The payoff-dominant outcome is mutual cooperation (everyone builds, everyone benefits). But the risk-dominant strategy is defection (everyone waits, nobody loses money). Without a credible signal that shifts expectations, the rational strategy for every individual actor is to stand still, even though collective action would make everyone better off.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!d0gG!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F86e9f83a-0d03-4575-9810-25c580eb93b6_1884x1060.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!d0gG!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F86e9f83a-0d03-4575-9810-25c580eb93b6_1884x1060.png)

The historical evidence supports this framing. Arden cites Delmas and Heiman’s comparative study of the French and American nuclear programs, which demonstrated that the US combination of fragmented power, activist access to the courts, and weak bureaucratic cohesion destroyed credible government commitment. Construction lead times in the US lengthened from roughly three years to thirteen, while France held steady near six. Average project delays reached thirty-four months versus six in France. Interest during construction climbed to roughly 30% of capital expenditure. And 134 US reactors were cancelled while France built fifty-six, eventually reaching 75% nuclear electricity by 1990.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!PHdw!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fa5c8ccaa-b181-4b1d-a1b4-45662806ce0f_1886x888.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!PHdw!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fa5c8ccaa-b181-4b1d-a1b4-45662806ce0f_1886x888.png)

The critical insight: France did not have better technology. It did not have smarter engineers. It had *institutional coherence*. A unified technocratic bureaucracy (EDF/CEA) that could issue credible commitments, standardize designs, maintain a continuous construction workforce, and iterate on a single reactor platform. The US had none of these things after the dissolution of the Atomic Energy Commission in 1974 and the fracturing of authority across DOE, NRC, EPA, and the courts.

Arden’s prescription is a “sovereign signal,” a visible, irreversible, and costly commitment from the executive branch that realigns expectations across all actors simultaneously. Not a subsidy. Not a tax credit. Not a pilot program. A structural declaration that nuclear is permanent national infrastructure, backed by institutional reorganization, binding procurement, and financial commitments that make retreat more expensive than advance.

This is not political commentary. It is an analytical framework for understanding why the West’s nuclear construction record is what it is, and what would need to change for it to improve. The framework matters for investors because it helps you calibrate the probability that any given Western new-build announcement actually results in a completed reactor.

### **6.3 Why China and Korea Can Do It**

If the West’s problem is institutional fragmentation, the corollary is that countries with institutional coherence can build nuclear plants efficiently. The evidence is overwhelming.

**China** has built a nuclear construction machine that is, by any objective measure, the most productive in history. The State Council approves reactor batches annually (10 units in April 2025 alone, representing $27.4 billion in investment). The same design (Hualong One, with 41+ units operational or under construction) is replicated across sites, creating a manufacturing learning curve. The workforce moves from project to project without dispersing. Supply chain localization reached 100% in 2024, with 114 sets of domestically produced nuclear equipment delivered, double the 2023 figure. Each Hualong One unit is expected to reach power within 50-60 months of first concrete.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!2JAz!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fb0a89062-d312-48d5-86d2-0b32243cc13d_1884x866.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!2JAz!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fb0a89062-d312-48d5-86d2-0b32243cc13d_1884x866.png)

China has also achieved something no other country has: first-mover deployments across multiple advanced reactor types. The Shidaowan high-temperature gas-cooled reactor (the world’s first commercial Gen IV reactor) began operations in 2021. The Linglong One SMR (ACP100) is on track for completion by 2026. The CFR-600 sodium-cooled fast reactor began commissioning in 2023. And the world’s first thorium molten-salt reactor (TMSR-LF1) achieved first criticality in October 2023 and confirmed in-reactor breeding of U-233 from thorium in November 2025.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!5hEF!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F9994f0c4-b314-4841-b694-2f57125523dc_1886x702.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!5hEF!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F9994f0c4-b314-4841-b694-2f57125523dc_1886x702.png)

This is not because Chinese engineers are inherently superior. It is because China treats nuclear as sovereign infrastructure, exactly as Arden prescribes. The State Council provides the signal. CNNC and CGN execute. The National Nuclear Safety Administration licenses. The supply chain aligns because everyone knows the program is permanent. The stag hunt is resolved because the sovereign has committed.

**South Korea** tells the same story, slightly differently. Korea’s APR-1400 design (a 1,400 MWe PWR) was delivered at the four-unit Barakah plant in the UAE roughly on schedule, a remarkable achievement by the standards of any recent large reactor project. All four units were connected by 2024. The initial contract value was approximately $24 billion for 5,600 MW (~$4,300/kW at financial close), though subsequent reporting suggests the all-in cost including financing and scope changes may have reached $30-32 billion (~$5,400-5,700/kW). Even at the higher figure, this is roughly half the per-kW cost of Vogtle or Hinkley, and remains the strongest evidence that serial construction of a standardised design can achieve competitive nuclear economics.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!oMF2!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F08db91f2-358f-443d-adea-a265ee823ce7_1886x1058.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!oMF2!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F08db91f2-358f-443d-adea-a265ee823ce7_1886x1058.png)

Korea achieved this through design standardization (one design, replicated), a retained construction workforce (the same teams that built Korea’s domestic fleet), and a government that treated nuclear exports as a strategic national priority (the Korea Electric Power Corporation, KEPCO, is state-owned). After a brief flirtation with nuclear phase-out under President Moon Jae-in, Korea reversed course and now plans to increase nuclear’s share to 30%+ of the electricity mix by 2036.

The lesson from China and Korea is not “authoritarianism builds reactors.” It is “institutional coherence builds reactors.” France proved this in the 1970s-80s under a democratic system. The common thread is not political regime type. It is the presence of a sovereign commitment that resolves the coordination game and allows the supply chain to mobilize with confidence.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!a6qD!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F6bcba2a3-6295-48b6-8ff2-d2ba3e5eb0af_1884x1028.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!a6qD!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F6bcba2a3-6295-48b6-8ff2-d2ba3e5eb0af_1884x1028.png)

### **6.4 The Existing Fleet: Where the Near-Term Growth Actually Is**

While everyone is talking about new build and SMRs, the near-term growth in nuclear capacity is coming from a much less glamorous source: life extensions and restarts of existing reactors.

The US nuclear fleet of 94 reactors is aging, with a median reactor age around 42 years. But the NRC has been granting 20-year license renewals that extend operating lifetimes from 40 to 60 years, and subsequent license renewals (SLRs) that take them to 80 years.
