As of 2025, several reactors have already received SLRs, and the industry expects the majority of the fleet to apply. At 80 years, a reactor that entered service in 1985 would operate until 2065. The fuel demand from these extensions is already baked into procurement plans but is rarely counted as “growth” because no new reactor is being built.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!8t7U!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F1168f2f5-70d1-4a44-a637-e633f6c1563d_1886x1052.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!8t7U!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F1168f2f5-70d1-4a44-a637-e633f6c1563d_1886x1052.png)

Restarts are the more dramatic story. Three Mile Island Unit 1 (the reactor that was *not* involved in the 1979 accident) is being restarted under a 20-year PPA with Microsoft for its data center needs. Palisades in Michigan, which was shut down in 2022, received a $1.5 billion DOE loan guarantee for restart and plans to return to service. Holtec, which owns Palisades, also plans to build an SMR-300 alongside the restarted large reactor.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!pNA-!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F2341e2ea-fb94-4164-84bf-714ac181fe8b_1884x986.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!pNA-!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F2341e2ea-fb94-4164-84bf-714ac181fe8b_1884x986.png)

Japan is restarting its idled fleet. After shutting down all 54 reactors post-Fukushima, Japan has gradually restarted 12 as of early 2026, with more in the pipeline. Each restart adds roughly 1 GW of demand for fuel services.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!pVNo!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fce39b46e-1df9-44f3-a44f-b5af3c34ecc4_1884x872.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!pVNo!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fce39b46e-1df9-44f3-a44f-b5af3c34ecc4_1884x872.png)

Life extensions and restarts represent the lowest-risk, fastest, and cheapest path to more nuclear capacity. You already have the site, the grid connection, the cooling water, the workforce, and (in many cases) the operating license. The fuel demand from these units is incremental to the market and largely uncovered by existing contracts, which is why utilities are being pushed into the term market.

### **6.5 SMRs: The Promise, the Hype, and the Reality**

There is no topic in nuclear energy where the gap between narrative and reality is wider than small modular reactors. SMRs are Silicon Valley’s favorite nuclear talking point. They are the subject of breathless announcements from tech CEOs. They are invoked in every data center power strategy presentation. And as of March 2026, not a single SMR is producing commercial electricity anywhere in the Western world.

Let us be precise about what exists, what is under construction, and what is still a PowerPoint slide.

**What actually exists (operating SMRs, globally):**

Russia’s Akademik Lomonosov, a floating nuclear power plant with two 35 MWe reactors, has been operating in Pevek, Russia since 2020. China’s Shidaowan HTGR (a 210 MWe high-temperature gas-cooled reactor, arguably the first commercial Gen IV reactor) has been generating electricity since 2021. China’s Linglong One (ACP100, a 125 MWe light water SMR) is under construction in Hainan and on track for completion by 2026.

That is it. Russia and China have operating or near-operational SMRs. The West has zero.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!2MF5!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F3068c3c8-2d3e-49a3-8327-19f004acbc61_1882x1060.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!2MF5!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F3068c3c8-2d3e-49a3-8327-19f004acbc61_1882x1060.png)

**What is under construction or in advanced permitting in the West:**

Ontario Power Generation’s Darlington New Nuclear Project in Canada, using GE Hitachi’s BWRX-300 (300 MWe light water SMR). Construction began in May 2025. First unit expected late 2029. Four units total. This will be the Western world’s first operating SMR if it stays on schedule. The US DOE awarded a $400 million grant to TVA to accelerate deployment of the BWRX-300 at its Clinch River site in Tennessee, but that project is further behind, with commissioning targeted for 2032.

Kairos Power’s Hermes test reactor in Oak Ridge, Tennessee: a 35 MWt (non-power) demonstration of its fluoride salt-cooled high-temperature reactor. Construction permit granted December 2023. Scheduled for operation in 2026. This is a test reactor, not a commercial power plant. Google has partnered with Kairos to deploy 500 MW of commercial capacity, but those units are for the 2030s.

TerraPower’s Natrium (345 MWe sodium-cooled fast reactor with molten salt energy storage) in Kemmerer, Wyoming: the NRC issued a construction permit on March 4, 2026, the first commercial non-light-water reactor construction permit in over 40 years. Non-nuclear construction began in mid-2024, with the sodium test facility’s 167-foot steel frame already rising. Total project cost is approximately $4 billion, with DOE providing up to $2 billion via ARDP and Bill Gates contributing roughly $1 billion. Operations targeted for 2030 to 2031. The critical dependency is HALEU fuel at 19.75% enrichment, supply that barely exists.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!ERsM!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F6cce90ba-2188-4f12-b2c7-0a09e31bbbc8_1886x1046.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!ERsM!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F6cce90ba-2188-4f12-b2c7-0a09e31bbbc8_1886x1046.png)

X-energy’s Xe-100 (80 MWe helium-cooled pebble bed) at Dow Chemical’s Seadrift site in Texas: NRC accepted the construction permit application for review with an 18-month timeline. The critical milestone: TRISO-X received the first-ever NRC Part 70 HALEU fuel fabrication license in February 2026, a significant de-risking event for the advanced fuel supply chain. X-energy has raised over $1.1 billion. But the Dow partnership for four Xe-100 units (320 MWe) remains subject to Dow’s final capital commitment decision, expected around 2028.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!fwaV!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fb2c39800-1d06-449f-a3d0-65542143db44_1884x972.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!fwaV!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fb2c39800-1d06-449f-a3d0-65542143db44_1884x972.png)

NuScale remains the only SMR developer with NRC-approved designs: its original 50 MWe module received a full Design Certification (Part 52 rulemaking) in 2023, while the uprated 77 MWe version received a Standard Design Approval (SDA) in June 2025, a technically distinct but functionally equivalent NRC authorization.

But its first planned deployment (the Idaho CFPP project with UAMPS) was cancelled in November 2023 after costs nearly doubled. NuScale has since partnered with ENTRA1 Energy and TVA on a 6 GW deployment program announced September 2025, but no concrete construction timeline has been disclosed. NuScale has also signed deals in Ghana, Romania, and other countries, all in early planning stages.

**What is still a press release:**

Larry Ellison’s announcement of a 1 GW data center campus powered by three SMRs. Amazon’s nuclear data center plans. Meta’s 4 GW nuclear RFI. These are expressions of interest, not funded construction projects. They signal directional intent, which matters for market sentiment, but they do not represent committed capital or secured permits. Most of these are a decade away at best.

**The honest assessment:**

SMRs will contribute to the nuclear mix in the 2030s. The BWRX-300 at Darlington has the best chance of being the Western world’s first operating commercial SMR, probably in 2029-2030. The advanced designs (Kairos, TerraPower, X-energy) will follow in the early-to-mid 2030s. NuScale’s commercial timeline remains unclear.

But SMRs will not meaningfully contribute to the nuclear capacity or uranium demand picture within this decade. The IEA’s base case has the first SMRs coming online around 2030, with nuclear’s contribution to data center electricity supply growing “notably after 2030.” Goldman Sachs projects SMRs will be “generally cheaper to build” than traditional reactors once designs are “fully standardized,” but acknowledges this is contingent on achieving manufacturing scale that does not yet exist.

The hype cycle is running 5-10 years ahead of the engineering reality. This is not a criticism of the technology or the companies. It is a statement about timelines. If you are making investment decisions based on SMR deployment happening before 2030, you are making a bet against every nuclear construction timeline in history.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!v5Pw!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F98c763cf-7ba9-42c1-9521-b111aa5634f9_1878x908.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!v5Pw!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F98c763cf-7ba9-42c1-9521-b111aa5634f9_1878x908.png)

A technical wrinkle that SMR critics have raised and that proponents have not fully resolved: spent fuel characteristics.

A 2022 study published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences (Krall, Macfarlane, and Ewing) found that SMR designs may produce waste that is “more voluminous and chemically/physically reactive” per unit of electricity than conventional large reactors. The core physics reason is neutron leakage: smaller reactors have a higher surface-area-to-volume ratio, which means a greater fraction of neutrons escape the fuel and irradiate the surrounding structural steel rather than causing fission.

The result is more activated structural waste per MWh generated, and a requirement for higher initial fuel enrichment to compensate for the lost neutrons.

Argonne National Laboratory published a 2023 rebuttal finding SMR waste volumes “roughly comparable” to large reactors when measured on consistent assumptions. The debate is unresolved and matters for two reasons: first, waste volume directly affects disposal costs and public acceptance; second, if SMR waste profiles require different handling or disposal pathways, they could face additional regulatory burden that affects deployment timelines and economics. This does not invalidate the SMR thesis, but it introduces a variable that most SMR investment analyses do not account for.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!nfpA!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F0c32fa53-43e4-4f83-aa17-abbd2bd30de9_1882x920.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!nfpA!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F0c32fa53-43e4-4f83-aa17-abbd2bd30de9_1882x920.png)

### **6.6 The Construction Problem Is a Workforce Problem**

One of the least discussed but most critical constraints on Western nuclear new build is the construction workforce. Or rather, the absence of one.

The United States has not sustained continuous nuclear construction since the 1980s. The skilled trades (nuclear-grade welders, pipe fitters, boilermakers, concrete specialists, I&C technicians) required for reactor construction have either retired, moved to other industries, or were never trained in the first place. When Vogtle tried to build two AP1000s after a 30-year construction gap, it discovered that the workforce simply did not exist. Workers had to be recruited from across the country, trained on site-specific requirements, and managed through a project management structure that had not built a reactor in a generation.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!S0cy!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F2c3f0ac2-75ae-4996-8cdd-487a04e332a1_1882x1058.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!S0cy!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F2c3f0ac2-75ae-4996-8cdd-487a04e332a1_1882x1058.png)

This is the problem Arden’s framework addresses at the institutional level. Without a “sovereign signal” that makes clear the nuclear construction program is permanent (not a one-off project that might be cancelled after the next election), the workforce has no reason to invest in nuclear-specific skills. A welder who trains for nuclear qualification (which takes years and is significantly more demanding than commercial welding certification) needs confidence that there will be a pipeline of projects to work on. Without that confidence, the rational decision is to stay in oil and gas, or LNG construction, or shipbuilding, where the project pipeline is visible.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!qXEs!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F39ba7592-36b9-4964-a141-6ef83a7fbec1_1884x1040.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!qXEs!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F39ba7592-36b9-4964-a141-6ef83a7fbec1_1884x1040.png)

China solved this by maintaining continuous construction (37 reactors in 10 years, with 32 more underway). France solved it in the 1970s-80s by building 58 reactors in roughly two decades with the same workforce moving from site to site. Korea solved it by maintaining both domestic and export construction programs.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!IFVZ!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F76915dcc-f162-4088-a006-d9b3d4955fe7_1882x1054.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!IFVZ!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F76915dcc-f162-4088-a006-d9b3d4955fe7_1882x1054.png)

The West has not solved it. And no amount of SMR announcements or federal funding changes that until someone builds a pipeline of projects large enough to justify workforce investment. This is why Arden calls for a “Nuclear Deployment Command” and an “Atomic Corps” to recruit, train, and deploy the construction workforce as permanent national infrastructure. Without it, each new project reinvents the wheel, retrains the workforce from scratch, and suffers the cost and schedule overruns that come with institutional amnesia.

### **6.7 What This Means for Investors**

The new build landscape creates a tiered investment framework:

**Tier 1: Existing fleet operators** (Constellation, EDF, KEPCO, Exelon legacy units) benefit from life extensions and restarts with relatively low execution risk. These companies capture the nuclear thesis through fuel procurement, not construction. Constellation’s stock has been one of the best performers in US utilities precisely because the market recognizes the value of its 21 GW nuclear fleet in a power-constrained world.

**Tier 2: Near-term fuel demand** (Cameco, Kazatomprom, Urenco, Orano, Centrus) benefits regardless of whether new build succeeds or fails. The existing fleet plus life extensions plus restarts generate enough demand growth to support the bull case in uranium and enrichment. New build is upside, not the base case.

**Tier 3: Construction-leveraged plays** (Westinghouse via Cameco/Brookfield, Framatome via Orano, BWXT, Fluor, Bechtel, GE Vernova) benefit if and when the construction pipeline materializes. These are higher-risk, higher-reward, and dependent on the political and institutional variables that Arden’s framework highlights.

**Tier 4: SMR developers** (NuScale, Oklo, X-energy, Kairos, TerraPower) are venture-stage bets on technology commercialization with 5-10 year timelines. They carry execution risk, regulatory risk, and financing risk. The potential payoffs are enormous if they succeed, but the base rates for first-of-a-kind nuclear construction projects are not encouraging.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!qh-t!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5d723448-c6f0-4853-abda-25cddb5e53df_1886x1048.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!qh-t!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5d723448-c6f0-4853-abda-25cddb5e53df_1886x1048.png)

The key question for Tiers 3 and 4 is whether the “sovereign signal” Arden describes will be issued, and whether it will be credible enough to break the coordination freeze. The May 2025 executive orders from the Trump administration moved in this direction (streamlining NRC licensing, directing federal site deployments, announcing $80 billion in new reactor funding). But as Arden notes, these created “visibility without irreversibility.” The orders reintroduced momentum but did not resolve the stag hunt. Until the signal is structural (binding procurement, organizational restructuring, multi-decade commitments), the smart money remains cautious on Western new build and concentrated in Tiers 1 and 2.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!3dB5!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe344bc9c-afb1-4b53-8bf9-0b9e79fb077b_1886x944.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!3dB5!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe344bc9c-afb1-4b53-8bf9-0b9e79fb077b_1886x944.png)

The nuclear renaissance is real. But it is unevenly distributed. In China and Korea, it is an industrial program delivering results. In the West, it is a policy aspiration contending with institutional inertia. The investment strategy should reflect that asymmetry.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!9ofQ!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe29bd822-316f-447e-a9d5-3fd7303f0ba6_1884x1058.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!9ofQ!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe29bd822-316f-447e-a9d5-3fd7303f0ba6_1884x1058.png)

## **Part 7: Regulation, Safety, and Public Perception**

### **7.1 The Regulator That Ate the Industry**

Between 1954 and 1978, the United States authorized the construction of 133 nuclear reactors. Since 1978, two have entered commercial operation. Both were at Vogtle. Both were late. Both were over budget. Together they cost $36.8 billion.

That is the single most important statistic in this entire primer. Not because the technology changed (it improved). Not because the physics changed (it did not). But because the regulatory and institutional environment that governs nuclear construction in the United States was transformed in the late 1970s in ways that made building reactors functionally impossible for a generation.

Understanding how this happened, and whether it is changing, is essential for any investor attempting to price the “nuclear renaissance.”

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!QCJM!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff82faecc-9f99-44c3-9761-5d284054788a_1882x1058.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!QCJM!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff82faecc-9f99-44c3-9761-5d284054788a_1882x1058.png)

### **7.2 A Brief History of How We Got Here**

The Atomic Energy Commission (AEC), established in 1946, was the original US nuclear regulator, promoter, and fuel cycle custodian, all in one institution. This unified mandate was coherent but carried an obvious conflict of interest: the same body that promoted nuclear energy was responsible for ensuring its safety. After the environmental movement of the early 1970s and growing public concern about nuclear safety, Congress dissolved the AEC in 1974 and split its functions between the Department of Energy (promotion, fuel cycle, weapons) and the newly created Nuclear Regulatory Commission (safety and licensing).

This split was well-intentioned. It was also the moment nuclear deployment in the United States began to die.

Not because the NRC was incompetent. It was and is staffed by serious, technically capable professionals. But because the NRC was created in an era of *adversarial legalism*, a term coined by political scientist Robert Kagan to describe the distinctly American regulatory style in which policy disputes are resolved not through bureaucratic consensus but through litigation, public hearings, and procedural challenges.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!kx-U!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff92391e0-ea33-4681-9fa2-bdccb471e6a1_1882x800.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!kx-U!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff92391e0-ea33-4681-9fa2-bdccb471e6a1_1882x800.png)

The practical effect on nuclear licensing was devastating. Environmental statutes, particularly NEPA (the National Environmental Policy Act), introduced layers of mandatory review that could be challenged in court at every stage. Standing rules were relaxed, allowing virtually any interested party to intervene in licensing proceedings. Citizen suits multiplied. Each reactor became a bespoke legal battle. And because every challenge added months or years to the licensing timeline, and because interest during construction (IDC) compounds on a multi-billion-dollar project at rates that can reach 30% of total capital expenditure, each delay made the reactor more expensive, which made it harder to justify, which invited more legal challenges, which created more delays.

This was not a conspiracy against nuclear. It was a systemic outcome of a regulatory philosophy that prioritized procedural rights over institutional coherence. The same legal and administrative framework that gummed up nuclear reactors also slowed highway construction, pipeline permitting, and transmission line development. Nuclear just suffered the most because its capital costs are the highest and its construction timelines are the longest. In a system where any intervenor can add three years to a licensing review, a $5 billion project becomes a $10 billion project through accumulated IDC alone.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!SCnE!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5596b6e7-f318-4812-b6b9-e5f23358e70d_1884x1056.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!SCnE!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5596b6e7-f318-4812-b6b9-e5f23358e70d_1884x1056.png)

Delmas and Heiman’s comparative study, which we referenced in Part 6, quantified the result. US construction lead times tripled from roughly three years in the 1960s to thirteen years by the 1980s. France’s held steady near six. The US cancelled 134 reactors. France built 56. Same decade. Same technology. Different institutional architecture.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!EGS9!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fea50c4e1-2c3e-40c8-ad9f-cd64bd958131_1886x1038.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!EGS9!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fea50c4e1-2c3e-40c8-ad9f-cd64bd958131_1886x1038.png)

### **7.3 The NRC Today: Reform Is Real, but the Stag Hunt Persists**

The NRC is in the middle of the most significant reform effort in its 50-year history, driven by three overlapping mandates: the ADVANCE Act of 2024 (bipartisan legislation that explicitly added “enabling the deployment of nuclear power for the benefit of society” to the NRC’s mission statement), NEIMA (the 2019 Nuclear Energy Innovation and Modernization Act, which required modernized fee structures and performance metrics), and Executive Order 14300 (President Trump’s May 2025 order titled “Ordering the Reform of the Nuclear Regulatory Commission”).

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!mGD3!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fbfa2fd5b-651f-479f-922f-7902605d127b_1884x1036.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!mGD3!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fbfa2fd5b-651f-479f-922f-7902605d127b_1884x1036.png)

EO 14300, signed May 23, 2025 and published in the Federal Register on May 29, is the most aggressive federal intervention in nuclear regulatory policy in decades. It mandates binding licensing deadlines: no more than 18 months for new reactor applications and no more than 12 months for license renewals. It directs NRC to reorganize staff in consultation with DOGE, expand capacity in new reactor licensing, establish a 20-member team to draft new regulations, and reduce the Advisory Committee on Reactor Safeguards to its statutory minimum. Proposed rules must be issued within nine months; final rules within eighteen months.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!dN-7!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5cb214c6-6187-4797-8357-6a921394f64f_1882x1002.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!dN-7!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5cb214c6-6187-4797-8357-6a921394f64f_1882x1002.png)

The NRC has responded with measurable improvements.In 2024, it completed review of the Hermes 2 construction permit (a Kairos Power test reactor) in approximately 16 months from application submission to permit issuance (July 2023 to November 2024), making it one of the fastest NRC construction permit reviews in the agency’s history. NRC Chair Hanson described the review as taking “less than 18 months.”

It renewed 13 reactor licenses (each for an additional 20 years, preserving 12,000 MW on the grid). It authorized the first-ever return-to-operation of a decommissioned reactor (Palisades). It established an 18-month-or-shorter review schedule for X-energy’s Xe-100 construction permit at Dow Chemical’s Seadrift site. It extended AP1000 design certifications from 15-20 years to 40 years. And it realized up to a 50% reduction in resources and schedules for license renewals, with 35% schedule reductions and 40% cost reductions for new reactor reviews.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!MVjQ!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F9a1fde03-5215-4aa9-ac17-f26699c48599_1888x952.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!MVjQ!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F9a1fde03-5215-4aa9-ac17-f26699c48599_1888x952.png)

The Part 53 rulemaking, a technology-inclusive licensing framework designed for advanced (non-light-water) reactors, has been in development for five years and must be finalized by law before the end of 2027. If implemented well, it could reduce licensing costs per applicant by $53-68 million and provide a regulatory pathway that does not require dozens of exemptions from LWR-specific rules. But as of early 2026, most developers (including TerraPower) are filing under the existing Part 50 framework because Part 53 is not yet available.

Here is the honest assessment: the NRC reforms are real, directionally positive, and more aggressive than anything attempted in the past two decades. But they do not, by themselves, resolve the coordination freeze that Arden describes. Faster licensing is necessary but not sufficient. A utility that can get a permit in 18 months instead of 60 months still needs to solve the financing problem, the construction workforce problem, the fuel supply problem, and the political sustainability problem. The NRC is one node in a multi-player coordination game, and reforming one node does not unfreeze the game.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!dWwq!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Faaa5cbdd-7d1f-4aa0-bc4f-8fbea4f688a2_1888x1020.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!dWwq!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Faaa5cbdd-7d1f-4aa0-bc4f-8fbea4f688a2_1888x1020.png)

This is the nuance that most nuclear commentary misses. Pro-nuclear advocates celebrate NRC reform as if it were the binding constraint, and once removed, reactors will spring from the ground like mushrooms after rain. Anti-nuclear advocates warn that reforms are “rubber-stamping” safety, as if the only thing standing between America and a nuclear accident is a lengthy review timeline. Both are wrong. The binding constraint is systemic, not regulatory. And the safety case for nuclear is independent of how long the NRC takes to issue a permit.

### **7.4 The Safety Record: Numbers That Nobody Wants to Hear**

This is the section that kills arguments at dinner parties.

Nuclear power, measured by deaths per unit of energy produced, is the safest large-scale energy source humanity has ever deployed. It is safer than solar (rooftop installation falls). It is safer than wind (turbine maintenance fatalities). It is dramatically, overwhelmingly safer than coal, oil, and natural gas (air pollution, mining accidents, pipeline explosions).

The numbers below draw on two separate studies compiled by Our World in Data: Markandya and Wilkinson (2007) for fossil fuels (covering air pollution and accidents, European data) and Sovacool et al. (2016) for renewables (covering accidents only, global data). The methodologies differ, the geographic scope differs, and the fossil fuel figures are likely conservative for global averages (Our World in Data’s global estimates for coal range from 93 to 224 deaths per TWh).

But even acknowledging these caveats, the relative ordering is obvious and the magnitude of the gap between nuclear and fossil fuels is not in dispute:

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!riUN!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fa55f74ca-7505-4db9-8358-d7e4ec60b393_1884x1058.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!riUN!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fa55f74ca-7505-4db9-8358-d7e4ec60b393_1884x1058.png)

Nuclear and solar are statistically tied for the safest energy sources on Earth. Coal kills roughly 800 times more people per unit of energy than nuclear. Natural gas kills roughly 90 times more. These are not abstract statistical constructs. They represent real human beings who die from particulate matter inhalation, mercury poisoning, black lung disease, and methane explosions, versus the vanishingly small number who have died from radiation exposure in the entire history of commercial nuclear power.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!DKZr!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff14d2171-e832-4ebe-8833-9cf627825bfc_1888x1020.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!DKZr!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff14d2171-e832-4ebe-8833-9cf627825bfc_1888x1020.png)

The total confirmed death toll from radiation exposure across all commercial nuclear accidents in 70 years is fewer than 50, all from Chernobyl. The WHO’s most aggressive long-term estimate projects approximately 4,000 additional cancer deaths over the lifetimes of the most exposed Chernobyl populations. Three Mile Island caused zero radiation deaths and zero measurable increase in cancer rates in the surrounding population (confirmed by multiple epidemiological studies over 40 years). Fukushima caused zero deaths from radiation (the deaths associated with the disaster, approximately 2,300, were caused by the evacuation itself, not by radiation exposure, a fact that has prompted significant debate about whether the evacuation was proportionate to the actual radiological risk).

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!NPr-!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F1303f30c-07eb-49b5-8fc6-cd5c42b51d54_1884x918.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!NPr-!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F1303f30c-07eb-49b5-8fc6-cd5c42b51d54_1884x918.png)

These numbers are not disputed by the scientific community. They are disputed by the public imagination, which is a very different thing.

### **7.5 Radiation: The Thing Everyone Gets Wrong**

The public perception of radiation risk is, to put it charitably, untethered from empirical reality. This is not because people are stupid. It is because radiation is invisible, the word “nuclear” is culturally fused with “weapon,” and the human brain processes risk through narrative, not statistics.

Here is what the science actually says.

Radiation is everywhere. You are being irradiated right now. The average American receives approximately 6.2 millisieverts (mSv) of radiation per year from natural and medical sources. Of this, roughly 3 mSv comes from natural background radiation (cosmic rays, radon gas in your basement, potassium-40 in your bananas and your own body) and roughly 3 mSv comes from medical procedures (CT scans, X-rays, nuclear medicine).

A chest X-ray delivers about 0.02 mSv. A CT scan delivers about 7 mSv. A transatlantic flight delivers about 0.08 mSv (cosmic ray exposure increases with altitude). Living within 50 miles of a nuclear power plant adds approximately 0.0001 mSv per year to your exposure (the NRC’s figure is 0.01 millirem, not millisieverts), which is roughly 70,000 times less than a single CT scan.

The “banana equivalent dose” is a popular science communication tool: a banana contains enough potassium-40 to deliver about 0.1 microsieverts per banana eaten. You would need to eat roughly 10 million bananas in rapid succession to receive a lethal dose. This is obviously absurd, but it makes the point: radiation is not binary (safe/lethal). It is a continuum, and the doses associated with normal nuclear power plant operations are negligible compared to the radiation you voluntarily accept from medical imaging, air travel, and living in a stone house.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!wqct!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F66db354a-f81e-4678-b129-a9ecaa4e352f_1884x1030.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!wqct!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F66db354a-f81e-4678-b129-a9ecaa4e352f_1884x1030.png)

The health effects of low-dose radiation (below roughly 100 mSv) are the subject of genuine scientific debate.

The “linear no-threshold” (LNT) model, which underpins most radiation protection regulations worldwide, assumes that any amount of radiation carries some cancer risk, proportional to dose, with no safe threshold. LNT remains the consensus regulatory standard, endorsed by the ICRP, UNSCEAR, the IAEA, the US NRC, and the National Academies (BEIR VII, 2006). The NRC rejected a formal petition to abandon LNT as recently as 2021. It is not a fringe position. It is the institutional default, and for good reason: it provides a conservative, precautionary framework for occupational and public dose limits that has served the industry well for decades.

That said, there is genuine scientific debate at the low-dose frontier. A comprehensive 2024 review in Health Physics found that roughly 62% of animal and cellular studies support non-linear or hormetic effects at low doses, while human epidemiological data broadly supports LNT (or at least cannot rule it out, given the statistical difficulty of detecting tiny excess cancer rates against large natural background rates). The “hormesis” hypothesis, which suggests that very low doses may actually stimulate protective biological responses, remains controversial and has not achieved regulatory acceptance.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!A7jF!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F339e87e1-c601-4232-8065-3c87f9df58a0_1886x1006.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!A7jF!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F339e87e1-c601-4232-8065-3c87f9df58a0_1886x1006.png)

For investors, the practical consequence of LNT matters more than the biological debate. LNT drives the evacuation thresholds, cleanup standards, and decommissioning costs that affect the economics of every nuclear facility. Fukushima’s 154,000-person evacuation, which caused approximately 2,300 deaths from the dislocation itself while radiation caused zero confirmed fatalities, was ordered under LNT-derived dose limits. If regulators eventually adopt a threshold model (a Trump-era executive order has directed the NRC to reconsider LNT), it would reduce decommissioning costs, narrow evacuation zones, and improve public acceptance. That regulatory shift is speculative but worth monitoring.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!FiHH!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5280d48b-5357-490d-8f8c-c11b7953aadf_1888x1058.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!FiHH!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5280d48b-5357-490d-8f8c-c11b7953aadf_1888x1058.png)

What is not controversial: the health risk from low-dose radiation exposure near nuclear power plants is unmeasurably small. It is lost in the statistical noise of background radiation variation, dietary differences, and other environmental factors. The NRC’s regulatory limit for public dose from nuclear plant operations is 1 mSv/year, and actual doses to members of the public are typically less than 1% of that limit.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!7yc0!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5c211dd4-0e1c-4113-be79-8ccd5bf7c381_1882x960.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!7yc0!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5c211dd4-0e1c-4113-be79-8ccd5bf7c381_1882x960.png)

#### **7.5.1 Water: The Operational Constraint Nobody Talks About**

Here is an operational risk that nuclear’s advocates consistently underweight and that climate change is making progressively more material: nuclear power plants need enormous quantities of water for cooling, and they are vulnerable to the same heatwaves and droughts that drive peak electricity demand.

Nuclear is the most water-intensive major electricity generation technology. A typical 1 GW PWR with once-through cooling withdraws approximately 25,000-60,000 gallons per MWh (though most of this is returned to the source at elevated temperature). Even with cooling towers (which consume rather than just withdraw water), consumption runs roughly 500-800 gallons per MWh, compared to roughly 200-300 for a combined cycle gas plant and near-zero for solar PV and wind.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Dbka!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F64b2553c-166f-4c3d-95f0-97365642f9dc_1884x1024.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Dbka!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F64b2553c-166f-4c3d-95f0-97365642f9dc_1884x1024.png)

This is not a theoretical concern.

Peer-reviewed research by Linnerud, Mideksa, and Eskeland (2011, The Energy Journal) found that a 1 degree Celsius increase in ambient water temperature reduces nuclear power output by approximately 0.5%, a finding corroborated by Ibrahim et al. (2014) at 0.44% per degree. Losses can exceed 2% per degree during extreme heat events. A separate study in Nature Energy (Ahmad, 2021) documented increased outage frequency and duration at nuclear plants during heatwaves, reinforcing the operational concern from a different angle.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!2ljH!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F2cad2ee6-9811-4f13-aa9c-de5720e12650_1884x860.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!2ljH!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F2cad2ee6-9811-4f13-aa9c-de5720e12650_1884x860.png)

France, the poster child for nuclear power, has repeatedly been forced to curtail reactor output during summer heatwaves because the rivers that supply cooling water (the Rhone, the Garonne, the Loire) get too warm to absorb the waste heat without exceeding environmental discharge limits. In the scorching summer of 2022, EDF curtailed output from multiple plants while simultaneously France was experiencing record electricity demand from air conditioning. Germany and Switzerland have experienced similar constraints.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!KRJx!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F92778665-f3c3-4a17-b8e5-e4cdda0cfd38_1888x960.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!KRJx!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F92778665-f3c3-4a17-b8e5-e4cdda0cfd38_1888x960.png)

For the existing US fleet, most plants are sited on large rivers, lakes, or coastlines with adequate water supply, and the operational impact has been modest. But several plants in the southeastern US (where water availability is declining and summer temperatures are rising) may face increasing curtailment risk over their remaining operating lifetimes, which could extend to the 2060s and 2070s under 80-year license renewals.

For new build, water availability is a binding site selection constraint. This is one reason SMR developers are exploring air-cooled designs (which eliminate the water dependency entirely but at a thermal efficiency penalty) and why several proposed SMR sites are at existing nuclear facilities or former coal plants that already have cooling water infrastructure.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!mq0t!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F8a8f0815-0c9c-4e0b-8949-8e3c0967e9d6_1888x964.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!mq0t!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F8a8f0815-0c9c-4e0b-8949-8e3c0967e9d6_1888x964.png)

The investment implication: water risk does not change the fundamental thesis for nuclear, but it does affect which specific plants will maintain their 92%+ capacity factors over the coming decades of climate warming, and which may experience declining performance. Investors in nuclear fleet operators should assess the cooling water vulnerability of the specific plants in the portfolio, not just the fleet-average statistics.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!7aiy!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F65fa0354-223a-4b41-9c06-3720c93ce30b_1888x952.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!7aiy!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F65fa0354-223a-4b41-9c06-3720c93ce30b_1888x952.png)

### **7.6 Three Mile Island, Chernobyl, Fukushima: What Actually Happened**

These three accidents form the trinity of public nuclear fear. Each is invoked as evidence that nuclear power is inherently dangerous. Each, properly understood, tells a story that is more nuanced, more instructive, and frankly more reassuring than the popular narrative suggests.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Gbvr!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F50b57ebf-d682-4733-a4ec-4854ea752b21_1886x1000.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Gbvr!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F50b57ebf-d682-4733-a4ec-4854ea752b21_1886x1000.png)

**Three Mile Island (1979): The Accident That Proved the Containment Worked**

A sequence of equipment failures and operator errors caused a partial meltdown of the reactor core at TMI Unit 2 in Pennsylvania. About half the core melted. And then the containment building, the massive reinforced concrete and steel structure that surrounds every Western reactor, did its job. It contained the vast majority of the radioactive material.

The average dose to the roughly 2 million people living within 50 miles was approximately 0.08 mSv, comparable to a chest X-ray. The maximum individual offsite dose was estimated at approximately 1 mSv, roughly equivalent to a few chest X-rays and still well below any threshold for measurable health effects.

No injuries. No deaths. No measurable health effects in 40+ years of follow-up studies.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!w7h4!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F43b88000-445f-4e2e-89f2-9be8133fbcc7_1882x922.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!w7h4!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F43b88000-445f-4e2e-89f2-9be8133fbcc7_1882x922.png)

TMI was a design validation event disguised as a disaster. The containment performed as engineered. The public was not harmed. But the psychological and political damage was catastrophic, because The China Syndrome had been released twelve days earlier, and the media narrative framed TMI as a narrowly averted Armageddon rather than a demonstration of defense-in-depth working exactly as intended.

**Chernobyl (1986): The Reactor That Should Never Have Existed**

We covered this in Part 2, but it bears repeating in the safety context. Chernobyl was an RBMK reactor with a positive void coefficient, no containment building, and operators who disabled safety systems during a poorly designed test. The explosion and graphite fire released massive amounts of radioactive material. Two plant workers died in the explosion. 28 emergency responders died of acute radiation syndrome within months. The long-term WHO estimate is approximately 4,000 additional cancer deaths among the most exposed populations.

Chernobyl was a Soviet-specific failure that could not occur in any Western reactor design. No PWR, BWR, CANDU, or Gen III+ reactor has the physics that produced Chernobyl. The comparison is technically illiterate, and yet it dominates public discourse about nuclear safety to this day.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!0-A8!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F59a9ccd1-0841-4c74-9b8d-86eec49678a9_1888x986.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!0-A8!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F59a9ccd1-0841-4c74-9b8d-86eec49678a9_1888x986.png)

**Fukushima (2011): When the Backup to the Backup Failed**

The Fukushima accident was caused not by a reactor failure but by a beyond-design-basis natural disaster: a magnitude 9.0 earthquake (the fourth largest ever recorded) followed by a 14-meter tsunami that overwhelmed the seawall and flooded the backup diesel generators. Without power for cooling, three reactor cores melted.

The radiological release was significant but the direct health impact was minimal. The UN Scientific Committee on the Effects of Atomic Radiation (UNSCEAR) concluded that “no discernible increased incidence of radiation-related health effects are expected among exposed members of the public or their descendants.” Zero deaths from radiation exposure. The World Health Organization’s assessment found that the risk of additional cancers above natural rates was too small to be statistically detectable.

The human cost of Fukushima was real, but it came overwhelmingly from the evacuation, not the radiation. Approximately 2,300 people, mostly elderly, died from the stress, dislocation, and interrupted medical care caused by the forced evacuation of 154,000 people. A growing body of research, including a major 2024 study in the journal Risk Analysis, suggests that the evacuation of low-contamination zones was not justified by the radiological risk and may have caused more harm than the radiation would have.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!FT6l!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fb11afa98-9f5c-4d77-b677-ebf66a910e9a_1884x974.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!FT6l!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fb11afa98-9f5c-4d77-b677-ebf66a910e9a_1884x974.png)

This is not to minimize the accident. Fukushima was a massive industrial disaster that contaminated land, disrupted communities, and will take decades to fully decommission. But the radiation health effects, the thing the public fears most, were negligible. The lesson of Fukushima is not “nuclear is dangerous.” It is “tsunamis are dangerous, and you should not put your backup generators in the basement of a plant on a coastline in the most seismically active region on Earth.”

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!E9Lo!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ffb11904c-34c5-4893-b23d-e0922a1a92cd_1880x1056.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!E9Lo!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ffb11904c-34c5-4893-b23d-e0922a1a92cd_1880x1056.png)

### **7.7 The Waste Question: A Political Problem in a Technical Costume**

We covered this in Part 3, but given that waste is the anti-nuclear movement’s favorite argument, it is worth addressing frontally in the safety context.

The total volume of high-level spent nuclear fuel generated by the entire US commercial nuclear fleet in 60+ years of operation fits on a single football field stacked about 10 yards high. It sits in dry cask storage at reactor sites, in concrete and steel containers designed to withstand earthquakes, tornadoes, floods, and aircraft impacts. It has never harmed a single member of the public.

We have already covered the coal comparison in detail (Part 1 and Part 3), but one additional fact belongs here because it is genuinely surprising: coal plants release more radioactivity into the environment per kWh than nuclear plants do. The naturally occurring thorium and uranium in coal concentrate in the fly ash, and because that ash is dispersed into the atmosphere and surrounding land (even with modern capture systems recovering over 99% of particulates), the total radiological dose delivered to the public from coal combustion exceeds the dose from a properly operating nuclear plant. Spent nuclear fuel is far more radioactive per unit mass, but it is contained in engineered storage rather than dispersed.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!2jJM!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd7af8e43-6a53-4767-9ec9-e879e49080ed_1886x972.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!2jJM!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd7af8e43-6a53-4767-9ec9-e879e49080ed_1886x972.png)

The volume comparison is even more damning: 300,000 tons of toxic ash per year from a single coal plant, disposed of in unlined ponds, versus 90,000 metric tons total from the entire US nuclear fleet across 60 years of operation, sitting safely in engineered dry cask storage.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!LZ-q!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F62e851a3-4773-4d1d-85b9-97ba500d347f_1886x1060.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!LZ-q!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F62e851a3-4773-4d1d-85b9-97ba500d347f_1886x1060.png)

Finland is building Onkalo, the world’s first permanent deep geological repository for spent nuclear fuel, carved into 1.8-billion-year-old granite bedrock on the island of Olkiluoto. It is designed to isolate waste for 100,000 years. Sweden has approved a similar repository at Forsmark. France’s Cigéo repository in clay formations is scheduled for operations in the 2030s.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!5Bro!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F01b29cce-d087-4f20-afd7-d5e977ff819f_1882x1030.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!5Bro!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F01b29cce-d087-4f20-afd7-d5e977ff819f_1882x1030.png)

The United States has none of these things, not because the technology does not exist, but because Yucca Mountain became a political football and no politician has had the courage (or the institutional mandate) to resolve it. This is Arden’s classification problem in miniature. Waste custody is a sovereign function. No market mechanism, no private company, and no state government can solve it. Only the federal government can declare custodianship, fund a repository, and implement it against local opposition. The refusal to do so is not a technical failure. It is a failure of sovereign posture.

For investors, the waste question is a negligible risk factor. The existing fleet has operated for decades with on-site storage, which is safe, licensed, and unconstrained by capacity limits within the investment horizon that matters. The political unresolvability of permanent disposal is a talking point for anti-nuclear advocates, not a binding constraint on nuclear operations or investment returns.

#### **7.7.1 The Price-Anderson Act: The Liability Architecture Nobody Discusses**

One element of the nuclear regulatory framework that belongs in any honest assessment, and that most nuclear primers omit because it complicates the narrative, is the Price-Anderson Nuclear Industries Indemnity Act.

Price-Anderson, originally enacted in 1957 and most recently extended through 2045 by the ADVANCE Act, establishes a two-tier insurance system for nuclear accidents. Each reactor operator must carry roughly $500 million in primary insurance. If damages exceed that, all US reactor operators contribute to a secondary pool totaling roughly $15 billion (currently about $166 million per reactor, payable in annual installments). Claims exceeding this combined pool would require Congressional action. The total coverage, after the 5% surcharge and post-2023 inflation adjustments to retrospective premiums, now exceeds $16 billion (the Congressional Research Service estimates approximately $16.6 billion; the NRC’s current published figure states “more than $16 billion”).

This is, functionally, a liability cap. And it is a subsidy. If a nuclear accident caused damages exceeding $15.5 billion (which a severe accident plausibly could, depending on the modeling assumptions), the excess falls on the federal government and, ultimately, taxpayers. No private insurer would cover the tail risk of a nuclear accident at any commercially viable premium, which is why Price-Anderson exists: without it, no utility would build or operate a reactor.

This matters for investors not because it changes the operational thesis (Price-Anderson has been in place for nearly 70 years and is deeply embedded in the industry’s financial structure) but because anti-nuclear critics correctly identify it as a hidden subsidy, and because any honest LCOE comparison between nuclear and alternatives should, in principle, include the actuarial value of the liability cap. It also matters for the new-build thesis: Price-Anderson’s extension through 2045 was a prerequisite for any utility considering a construction commitment.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!PmWP!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fa8b8ea39-8382-47dc-ac61-1832fceda262_1882x1056.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!PmWP!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fa8b8ea39-8382-47dc-ac61-1832fceda262_1882x1056.png)

### **7.8 Public Perception: Why the Vibes Are Finally Shifting**

For decades, nuclear energy had the worst brand in the energy business. Mushroom clouds. Geiger counters. Hazmat suits. Homer Simpson. The cultural firmware was set to “fear,” and no amount of safety data could overwrite it.

Something has changed. Not gradually. Abruptly.

A 2024 Pew Research Center survey found that 56% of Americans now favor expanding nuclear power, up from 43% in 2020. Gallup found similar trends. Among younger demographics (18-29), support has grown even faster, driven partly by climate concern and partly by the tech industry’s enthusiastic embrace of nuclear for data center power.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!tUWE!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F11ba6157-5df8-439c-98de-5912c83caff7_1880x906.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!tUWE!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F11ba6157-5df8-439c-98de-5912c83caff7_1880x906.png)

The shift is not driven by better messaging or public education campaigns. It is driven by *perceived purpose*. When Microsoft signs a 20-year PPA to restart Three Mile Island for its data center, that signals to the public that nuclear energy is not an artifact of the Cold War but a technology of the future. When Google partners with Kairos Power to deploy advanced reactors, that reframes nuclear from “scary legacy technology” to “cutting-edge clean energy.” When Sam Altman invests in Oklo, nuclear gets Silicon Valley credibility.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!XghK!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F77036943-10f2-4610-9b71-082b9e5e7366_1888x870.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!XghK!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F77036943-10f2-4610-9b71-082b9e5e7366_1888x870.png)

Arden’s framework predicts this. He argues that public legitimacy is not primarily a function of information but of perceived purpose. “When a technology is seen as essential to national survival, as spaceflight was during the Cold War, or the internet during the post-9/11 cybersecurity build-out, public ambivalence generally yields to alignment.” Nuclear’s wavering public legitimacy reflects its institutional ambiguity. Until it is declared, visibly and authoritatively, as a permanent pillar of national infrastructure, the public will mirror the system’s uncertainty.

The tech industry’s embrace of nuclear is doing something that decades of industry PR could not: it is giving nuclear a narrative of *inevitability*. When the richest, most innovative companies on Earth choose nuclear, it signals that nuclear has been chosen. That signal matters more than any safety statistic.

But let us not overstate the shift. Opposition remains concentrated and politically powerful in specific geographies (Nevada, parts of New England, Germany, Austria, Australia). NIMBY dynamics around waste storage and new reactor siting are real and will not disappear because Bill Gates tweets about Natrium. The permitting and litigation infrastructure that Arden describes as the legacy of 1970s adversarial legalism is still fully operational, and any new reactor project will face legal challenges from well-funded environmental organizations.

The difference now is that the political wind has shifted from headwind to tailwind. Bipartisan support for nuclear in the US Congress (the ADVANCE Act passed with overwhelming bipartisan margins). State-level support in key jurisdictions (Tennessee, Wyoming, Michigan, Ontario, Saskatchewan). International support (30+ countries pledging to triple capacity at COP28). And the perception that opposing nuclear is no longer the “green” position but the “obstructionist” position, at least among the centrist and center-left voters who increasingly see nuclear as essential to decarbonization.

For investors, the public perception shift matters because it reduces the political risk premium on nuclear investments. A reactor project that would have been politically toxic in 2015 is politically viable in 2025. That does not mean it will be built on time or on budget. But it means the political kill switch, which destroyed nuclear construction in the 1980s and could always be pulled again, is less likely to be pulled in the current environment than at any point in the past 40 years.

### **7.9 The Regulatory Edge: What TSCS Readers Need to Know That Others Don’t**

Most nuclear primers cover regulation as a boring procedural topic and move on. Here is what actually matters for positioning:

**The 18-month mandate is a regime change, not a tweak.** EO 14300’s binding 18-month review deadline for new reactor applications, if actually enforced, compresses a process that historically took 5-7 years. This does not just save time. It changes the financial math. Interest during construction on a $5 billion project at 7% is roughly $350 million per year. Cutting 3-4 years off the pre-construction licensing phase saves $1-1.4 billion in IDC alone. That is the difference between a project that pencils and one that does not.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!CDlx!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4a9cd24b-72af-4844-8476-1ff6f604cb23_1880x1004.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!CDlx!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4a9cd24b-72af-4844-8476-1ff6f604cb23_1880x1004.png)

**Part 53 is the sleeper catalyst.** The technology-inclusive licensing framework, which must be finalized by end-2027, would allow advanced reactor developers to file applications without dozens of exemptions from LWR-specific rules. The NRC estimates savings of $53-68 million per applicant. But the real value is in standardization: once a design is licensed under Part 53, subsequent deployments of the same design at different sites require only site-specific reviews, not full design re-reviews. This is how France built 58 reactors in 20 years, one design reviewed once and then replicated.

**The ADVANCE Act made deployment part of the NRC’s statutory mission.** Before 2024, the NRC’s mandate was purely safety-focused. The ADVANCE Act explicitly added language requiring the NRC to “enable the deployment of nuclear power for the benefit of society.” This is not symbolic. It changes the legal framework within which NRC staff make discretionary decisions. A regulator whose mission is solely to prevent harm optimizes for delay (you cannot be blamed for approving too slowly, but you can be blamed for approving too quickly). A regulator whose mission includes enabling deployment has institutional permission to balance speed and safety. The cultural shift this implies within the NRC bureaucracy is significant and will compound over years.

**The reduced hourly rate for advanced reactor applicants is a direct subsidy of regulatory risk.** The ADVANCE Act required NRC to establish a reduced fee for advanced reactor pre-applicants and applicants: $148/hour versus the standard $318/hour (a 53% reduction). For a multi-year licensing review that might consume thousands of staff-hours, this reduces the regulatory cost burden by millions. More importantly, it signals that the federal government wants these applications to happen.

**The 40-year design certification extension is a quiet game-changer.** NRC extended AP1000 design certifications from 15-20 years to 40 years. This means Westinghouse does not need to periodically re-certify its design, which removes a recurring regulatory risk that hung over the AP1000 program and, by extension, over every utility that might consider building one. For Korean APR-1400 or other standardized designs seeking US certification, this precedent matters enormously.

The sum of these reforms does not guarantee a nuclear construction renaissance. As Arden argues, regulatory reform is necessary but not sufficient in a multiplayer coordination game. But it removes one of the most frequently cited excuses for inaction, and it shifts the calculus for utilities, developers, and investors who were previously deterred by regulatory uncertainty.

The question is no longer “will the NRC let you build a reactor?” The question is “can you finance it, staff it, fuel it, and sustain the political will to complete it?” Those are harder questions. But they are the right questions, and the fact that we have moved past the regulatory blocker is itself a form of progress.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!39SU!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F37abacb9-2c4f-4574-8cfe-29f49cedba1e_1888x1028.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!39SU!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F37abacb9-2c4f-4574-8cfe-29f49cedba1e_1888x1028.png)

## **Part 8: The Investable Universe**

### **8.1 The Map Before the Territory**

You have read seven parts of this primer. You understand the physics. You understand the fuel cycle. You understand the bottlenecks, the geopolitics, the regulatory terrain, and the construction dynamics. Now we deploy all of it.

This section is not a stock-picking exercise. TSCS does not give price targets, and anyone who gives you a price target on a uranium miner is either lying about their confidence interval or does not understand what a confidence interval is. What we are going to do is something more valuable: give you a decision architecture. A framework for evaluating every nuclear investment on the same axes, so you can assess where each one sits in the risk-return spectrum and, critically, why it trades where it does.

The nuclear investment landscape is not one market. It is five distinct markets with different drivers, different risk profiles, and different relationships to the underlying thesis. Most investors treat it as a monolith (”I’m long nuclear”) and wonder why their portfolio does not perform the way the thesis suggests it should. The answer is almost always that they are in the wrong part of the chain.

Here are the five markets:

1. **Physical uranium** (commodity exposure, no operational risk)
2. **Uranium producers** (mining execution + commodity leverage)
3. **Fuel cycle services** (conversion, enrichment, fabrication, structural bottleneck exposure)
4. **Nuclear utilities and fleet operators** (power price leverage, political stability)
5. **New build and SMR developers** (venture-stage, binary outcomes, long duration)

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!r-oP!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F59be168d-574e-4116-af00-2d455064fb62_1888x1056.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!r-oP!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F59be168d-574e-4116-af00-2d455064fb62_1888x1056.png)

Each demands a different analytical framework, a different time horizon, and a different tolerance for execution risk. We will walk through each one and identify the names that matter, the drivers that move them, and the traps that catch the unwary.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!FsWF!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F2d047c1e-7d5c-459f-a761-24e962aca99a_1886x1054.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!FsWF!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F2d047c1e-7d5c-459f-a761-24e962aca99a_1886x1054.png)

### **8.2 Physical Uranium: The Cleanest Exposure**

If you believe the uranium price is going higher but do not want to underwrite mine execution risk, geological risk, permitting risk, or management risk, physical uranium vehicles are the purest play.

**Sprott Physical Uranium Trust (TSX: U.U / OTCQX: SRUUF)**

SPUT is the largest physical uranium holding vehicle in the world. It is effectively a closed-end trust that buys and holds physical uranium, storing it in licensed facilities. When SPUT trades at a premium to its net asset value, it issues new units and uses the proceeds to buy more uranium. When it trades at a discount, it stops buying.

This mechanism is important because SPUT is not just a passive vehicle. It is an active demand participant in the spot market. Its buying removes physical pounds from the market, tightening the spot balance. In weeks when SPUT is actively purchasing, spot volumes and prices both tend to move higher. In weeks when it is sidelined (trading at a discount), the spot market can soften. Understanding SPUT’s premium/discount dynamics is essential for anyone trading uranium equities short-term.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!oidT!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F02bf9f87-8663-42bb-9916-2c1a74484580_1888x1022.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!oidT!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F02bf9f87-8663-42bb-9916-2c1a74484580_1888x1022.png)

The bull case: SPUT gives you 1:1 exposure to the uranium spot price with zero execution risk. No mine needs to work. No permit needs to be granted. No CEO needs to execute.

The bear case: SPUT is tied to the spot price, which we established in Part 4 is the less important of the two uranium markets. The term price, where the real business happens, can diverge from spot for extended periods. In 2025, spot traded $63-83 while the term price climbed to $86 and continued rising. SPUT captures the spot, not the term. Also, SPUT charges a management fee (roughly 0.35% annually) and you are paying for physical storage of a commodity that generates no yield.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!nGLU!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Feb202283-c3ec-4855-b7d7-509140d47b06_1886x1052.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!nGLU!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Feb202283-c3ec-4855-b7d7-509140d47b06_1886x1052.png)

**Yellow Cake plc (LSE: YCA)**

The UK-listed equivalent, Yellow Cake holds physical uranium purchased under a long-term supply agreement with Kazatomprom. Smaller than SPUT but with a structural advantage: its Kazatomprom agreement provides a mechanism for purchasing uranium at spot prices regardless of market conditions, which gives it a reliable acquisition channel.

**The framework:** Physical uranium is a macro trade. You are betting on the direction of the commodity, full stop. It is the lowest-risk way to express the nuclear fuel thesis, and for investors who want nuclear exposure without the idiosyncratic risks of individual companies, it is the right starting point.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!OK_s!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fa2fd5c1f-0ac9-4dba-8059-96968a1f4f3b_1884x1036.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!OK_s!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fa2fd5c1f-0ac9-4dba-8059-96968a1f4f3b_1884x1036.png)

### **8.3 Uranium Producers: The Heart of the Sector**

This is where most nuclear investors live, and where the analytical complexity is highest. Not all miners are created equal, and the axes of differentiation are: jurisdiction, stage of development, cost curve position, contract book, and integration with downstream fuel cycle services.

**Tier 1: The Majors**

**Cameco Corporation (TSX: CCO / NYSE: CCJ)**

Cameco is the Western world’s vertically integrated nuclear fuel company. This distinction matters enormously and is the primary reason Cameco trades at a premium multiple that occasionally frustrates value-oriented investors.

The business has three segments: uranium mining (McArthur River/Key Lake, Cigar Lake, Inkai JV), fuel services (Port Hope conversion, fuel fabrication), and a 49% equity stake in Westinghouse Electric Company (acquired in late 2023 with Brookfield for an enterprise value of $8.2 billion).

McArthur River/Key Lake has persistently underproduced relative to its 25 million pound licensed capacity due to ground freezing challenges and development delays in new mining areas, a recurring operational constraint that tempers the bullish production narrative even as Cigar Lake and Inkai continue delivering. Port Hope conversion has been running at record output levels and climbing.

The company holds a contract book of roughly 220 million committed pounds, rolling legacy pricing into progressively higher term rates as older contracts expire. Cameco’s 49% share of Westinghouse is contributing growing EBITDA at a mid-to-high single digit compound annual growth rate, with additional upside from Westinghouse’s role in the Czech Republic’s Dukovany new-build project.

Then there is the $80 billion US government partnership announced in October 2025, which requires careful framing because it is the single most consequential development for Cameco’s Westinghouse stake and also the one most susceptible to over-reading. The binding term sheet was still being finalized at announcement. This is a framework agreement with aspirational scale, not a funded construction order. Under the framework, at least $80 billion of new reactors are to be constructed using Westinghouse AP1000 and AP300 technology, with the Department of Commerce arranging financing and facilitating permitting. The government receives a participation interest entitling it to 20% of cash distributions exceeding $17.5 billion, though this vests only upon a final investment decision and execution of definitive agreements.

It represents the most significant federal commitment to commercial nuclear construction since the Atomic Energy Act, and it is, in Arden’s framework, the closest thing to a “sovereign signal” for nuclear construction that the US has issued in decades.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!gw5S!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F1e7ca3bd-5d0b-4c71-b907-6ae1ec90730a_1886x1022.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!gw5S!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F1e7ca3bd-5d0b-4c71-b907-6ae1ec90730a_1886x1022.png)

Why Cameco matters for the thesis: it captures value at three points in the fuel chain (mining, conversion, and downstream services/fabrication via Westinghouse). When the uranium price rises, Cameco’s mining segment benefits. When conversion prices rise (they are up 6x since 2021), Port Hope benefits. When new reactors are ordered, Westinghouse benefits from reactor services, fuel fabrication, and new-build construction management. This triple exposure is why Cameco trades at roughly 17x forward EBITDA versus 5-8x for pure-play miners. The premium is structural, not speculative.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Hw0g!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F935ef9e1-bccb-4d49-9f00-a551531f236b_1884x1014.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Hw0g!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F935ef9e1-bccb-4d49-9f00-a551531f236b_1884x1014.png)

The risk: Cameco is a Canadian company selling into US markets, and the Trump administration’s tariffs on Canadian goods could add approximately 10% to costs. Most US customers are locked into long-term contracts through 2026, cushioning the near-term impact, but the tariff overhang creates volatility. Also, Cameco’s Inkai JV production in Kazakhstan remains vulnerable to the acid supply and transit risks we discussed in Part 5.

**Kazatomprom (LSE: KAP / OTC: NATKY)**
