The world’s largest uranium producer by volume, the lowest-cost major producer on the planet, and the single name that most perfectly illustrates why jurisdiction matters more than geology in this cycle. We covered the operational details extensively in Part 5, so here is the investment summary.

Kazatomprom has latent capacity well above current output. It has been persistently underproducing relative to its subsoil use agreement ceilings, constrained by sulfuric acid supply and infrastructure bottlenecks rather than by geology or capital. In theory, it is the closest thing to a “central bank of uranium,” with the ability to move the global supply dial by simply choosing to produce more.

In practice, it is a state-owned entity whose output is increasingly locked into Chinese offtake agreements (north of half its production now flows east), whose transit logistics depend on Russian rail and port infrastructure, whose JV equity stakes are being transferred from Russian to Chinese ownership, and whose geopolitical positioning makes it a fundamentally different risk than a Canadian or Australian miner. The jurisdiction discount Kazatomprom trades at relative to Cameco is not a market inefficiency. It is the market correctly pricing the fact that this company’s uranium is being redirected away from the West, one long-term contract at a time. Western investors who own Kazatomprom are, indirectly, financing a supply chain realignment that works against Western energy security. That may not bother you. But you should know what you own.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!kfB-!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F87855293-ede9-4208-91b0-fae4024842aa_1886x1028.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!kfB-!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F87855293-ede9-4208-91b0-fae4024842aa_1886x1028.png)

**Tier 2: The Developers (High Conviction, Pre-Revenue)**

**NexGen Energy (TSX: NXE / NYSE: NXE)**

NexGen’s Arrow deposit in the Athabasca Basin is, by any measure, the most important undeveloped uranium project in the world. 256.7 million pounds of measured and indicated resource. Projected average production of 28.8 million pounds per year in years 1-5, which would represent roughly 20% of current global fuel supply from a single mine. The Canadian Nuclear Safety Commission approved the construction licence in early 2026, the first new uranium mine approved in Canada in approximately 20 years.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!BuAs!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F8e9f77b3-7b7d-41db-9729-93df783a4654_1884x726.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!BuAs!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F8e9f77b3-7b7d-41db-9729-93df783a4654_1884x726.png)

NexGen is well-capitalized and targeting a four-year build timeline from construction start to first production. If it executes, Arrow will be a generational asset, one of the lowest-cost, highest-grade uranium mines on Earth, located in the safest jurisdiction on the planet.

The analytical question for NexGen is always the same: at what uranium price and discount rate does Arrow’s NPV justify the market cap? The feasibility study economics are compelling, but NexGen is pre-revenue, and the stock trades on NAV expectations driven by three variables: the assumed long-term uranium price, the discount rate, and the execution probability. The spread between “Arrow on time at $100+ uranium” and “Arrow two years late at $70 uranium” is the entire investment debate in one sentence.

**Denison Mines (TSX: DML / NYSE: DNN)**

Denison’s Wheeler River project (Phoenix ISR deposit) is the second most exciting new development in the Athabasca Basin. The feasibility study shows an after-tax IRR of 90%, a 10-month payback, and operating costs of $6.28/lb, among the lowest globally. The CNSC approved the construction licence in late 2025/early 2026, and first production is targeted for mid-2028.

Denison is smaller than NexGen in scale but arguably lower-risk in execution because ISR mining is simpler and cheaper than the underground mining NexGen will employ at Arrow. For investors who want Athabasca Basin exposure with faster time-to-production, Denison is the more conservative bet.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!ZPfT!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff2ef46cd-a05d-4ca1-b39a-1c0bdd24900a_1886x1054.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!ZPfT!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff2ef46cd-a05d-4ca1-b39a-1c0bdd24900a_1886x1054.png)

**Tier 3: The Restarters and Mid-Caps**

**Paladin Energy (ASX: PDN / OTC: PALAF)**: Restarted the Langer Heinrich mine in Namibia. Commercial production achieved in early 2024. Paladin offers near-term production growth in a jurisdiction that is politically stable but geographically distant from Western fuel cycle infrastructure.

**Boss Energy (ASX: BOE)**: Restarted Honeymoon ISR mine in South Australia at a cash cost of roughly $23/lb, making it the lowest-cost ISR producer in the Western world. Small scale but high margins, with dual-jurisdiction exposure through a 30% interest in the Alta Mesa ISR project in Texas. Boss is the proof of concept that ISR uranium mining can be restarted quickly and cheaply when price signals justify it.

**Uranium Energy Corp (NYSE: UEC)**: The largest US-based uranium mining company. Owns ISR projects in Texas and Wyoming plus the high-grade Roughrider project in the Athabasca Basin (acquired from Rio Tinto). UEC benefits disproportionately from US-centric policies (Section 232 investigations, critical minerals designation, domestic procurement preferences) but has yet to demonstrate sustained production at meaningful volumes.

**enCore Energy (TSX: EU / NYSE: EU)**: Another US ISR producer with multiple projects in Texas. Small scale, early-stage production ramp. Similar policy-driven thesis to UEC but smaller.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!0qa1!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff8e2ae83-6e91-4f81-a28a-ec24784089c1_1886x1012.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!0qa1!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff8e2ae83-6e91-4f81-a28a-ec24784089c1_1886x1012.png)

**The framework for producers:** The key variables are jurisdiction (Canada and Australia command premiums over Kazakhstan and Africa), stage (producing assets are valued differently than development assets), cost curve position (all-in sustaining cost relative to spot/term price), and contract book (contracted pounds at locked prices vs. uncontracted production exposed to spot). The best producers combine friendly jurisdictions with near-term production, low costs, and a growing contract book. The worst combine hostile jurisdictions with long development timelines, high costs, and no contractual visibility.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!_t3W!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5cff1970-8225-4a2e-a6c0-effea3a81323_1886x952.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!_t3W!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F5cff1970-8225-4a2e-a6c0-effea3a81323_1886x952.png)

### **8.4 Fuel Cycle Services: The Structural Bottleneck Plays**

This is where TSCS’s earlier analysis (Parts 3 and 5) converts most directly into investment positioning. The thesis is simple: the tightest bottleneck in the fuel chain captures the most pricing power, and the companies positioned at those bottlenecks trade at structural premiums.

**Enrichment**

**Centrus Energy (NYSE: LEU)**

Centrus is the only publicly traded Western enrichment company. Full stop. Urenco is privately held by three governments. Orano is state-owned. Rosatom is sanctioned. CNNC is Chinese and inaccessible. If you want equity exposure to the enrichment bottleneck, your list has one name.

Centrus operates two businesses: a legacy SWU and uranium brokering business (buying enrichment services from Urenco, Orano, and others and reselling to US utilities) and a nascent HALEU production program at its American Centrifuge Plant in Piketon, Ohio. The DOE awarded Centrus $900 million in January 2026 to expand HALEU enrichment, with first new capacity targeted for 2029.

The bull case: SWU spot prices have quadrupled from $47.50 to $193 in five years, and the structural deficit in Western enrichment capacity will take until the early 2030s to resolve. Centrus’s brokering margins expand as the SWU price rises. The HALEU contract provides a multi-year revenue floor. And if the US government decides to rebuild commercial-scale domestic LEU enrichment (which the $2.7 billion DOE program is designed to enable), Centrus’s centrifuge technology and NRC-licensed facility give it a first-mover advantage.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!XTLC!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F594822d5-8b14-4456-8f59-15f94e3a129f_1884x834.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!XTLC!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F594822d5-8b14-4456-8f59-15f94e3a129f_1884x834.png)

The bear case requires understanding the business model’s central vulnerability with precision. Centrus’s LEU segment functions primarily as a middleman: it purchases SWU from enrichers (historically dominated by Russia’s TENEX at favorable pricing) and resells to US utilities at a spread. That spread, applied across millions of SWU annually, generates the bulk of segment earnings. The problem is structural: the Prohibiting Russian Uranium Imports Act phases out Russian LEU imports by January 2028. If Centrus cannot replace Russian volumes with Urenco or Orano supply at comparable input prices (and both are themselves capacity-constrained, with order books extending deep into the 2030s), the brokerage margin compresses or the backlog becomes undeliverable. The backlog is an asset only if the supply side holds. If it does not, those contracts become liabilities.

The HALEU program is a demonstration project, not a commercial enrichment plant, and the transition from brokerage to domestic production is a multi-billion-dollar, multi-year execution challenge that has not begun at commercial scale. The stock prices in the destination; the risk lives in the journey. If SWU prices soften (unlikely near-term but possible if the China backdoor persists and Western capacity additions accelerate faster than expected), the entire margin structure compresses simultaneously.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!xJtu!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc694c23e-3ba0-4de0-8ed4-92e8dc34528e_1882x956.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!xJtu!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc694c23e-3ba0-4de0-8ed4-92e8dc34528e_1882x956.png)

**Conversion**

Conversion does not have a clean publicly traded equity proxy. Cameco’s Port Hope facility is the closest thing (it is Cameco’s fuel services segment, reported separately). Conversion prices have risen 6x since 2021 and the Western market operates at 91%+ utilization with no buffer. If you own Cameco, you own conversion exposure. There is no standalone play.

**Fuel Fabrication**

Westinghouse (via Cameco’s 49% stake) and Framatome (via Orano, state-owned) dominate Western fuel fabrication. BWXT Technologies (NYSE: BWXT) is the US leader in naval nuclear reactor fuel and components, with growing exposure to commercial nuclear via the eVinci microreactor program and DOE contracts. BWXT trades at a premium (roughly 30x+ forward earnings) that reflects its defense-industrial positioning as much as its nuclear exposure.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!tc83!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc91d4dd9-e151-4a8d-baec-cf99eeb848b8_1882x838.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!tc83!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc91d4dd9-e151-4a8d-baec-cf99eeb848b8_1882x838.png)

### **8.5 Nuclear Utilities: The Power Price Play**

If you believe that nuclear power plants are the most valuable generation assets in a power-constrained world, the obvious investment is the companies that own and operate them.

**Constellation Energy (NASDAQ: CEG)**

Constellation is the single largest nuclear fleet operator in the United States with approximately 22 GW of nuclear capacity across 12 stations (21 reactors). It was created from the 2022 separation of Exelon’s competitive generation business and is, effectively, the US nuclear pure-play.

Constellation has been one of the best-performing utility stocks in recent memory, driven by the recognition that nuclear baseload power is increasingly scarce and increasingly valuable in a world of AI-driven electricity demand growth. Microsoft’s 20-year PPA to restart Three Mile Island Unit 1 (a Constellation station) was the landmark deal that signaled to the market what TSCS readers already know: tech companies will pay whatever it takes for reliable, carbon-free baseload.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Bbua!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F97698221-f31c-4c07-86eb-47347cc60d7a_1886x806.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Bbua!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F97698221-f31c-4c07-86eb-47347cc60d7a_1886x806.png)

The bull case for Constellation is essentially the power price thesis. In deregulated markets (PJM, ERCOT, NYISO, ISO-NE), nuclear plants earn the market clearing price for electricity, which has been rising as demand growth outpaces supply additions. Nuclear’s 92%+ capacity factor and zero marginal fuel cost means nearly every MWh sold is profit above fixed costs. As gas prices rise, as renewables hit curtailment walls, and as data center demand pushes load growth to levels not seen in decades, the scarcity premium for reliable baseload increases.

There is a regulatory risk here that most nuclear commentary ignores and that TSCS readers need on their radar. In November 2024, FERC rejected Talen Energy’s proposed behind-the-meter arrangement with Amazon at the Susquehanna nuclear plant, ruling that directly serving a 960 MW data center from a grid-connected nuclear plant without going through the transmission system raised reliability concerns and could shift costs to other ratepayers. This was not a one-off ruling. FERC subsequently opened a Notice of Inquiry (Docket No. RM25-3-000) on large load co-location at generating facilities, signaling that the commission is evaluating whether to impose broader constraints on how nuclear plants can contract directly with hyperscalers.

If FERC determines that behind-the-meter co-location at grid-connected generators creates systemic reliability risk or cost-shifting, it could force all hyperscaler nuclear arrangements into front-of-meter retail structures. Talen’s restructured Amazon deal already uses this workaround (a retail power contract that bypasses FERC wholesale jurisdiction). But front-of-meter retail deals face state-level regulatory scrutiny, including public utility commission review, interconnection studies, and potential ratepayer advocacy challenges. The path from signed PPA to delivered electrons gets longer and less certain. The proceeding is ongoing. No final rule exists. But investors treating the utility re-contracting wave as a regulatory-free trade are underpricing a live risk.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!TtRP!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff47f8464-21ee-4a56-a22e-bbae762d17b1_1888x860.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!TtRP!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff47f8464-21ee-4a56-a22e-bbae762d17b1_1888x860.png)

The bear case requires quantifying what the current valuation already embeds. Constellation’s roughly 17 GW fleet at 92% capacity factor generates roughly 137 TWh per year. However, not all of that capacity is available for premium re-contracting. A significant portion serves regulated load-serving obligations, existing bilateral contracts at legacy rates, and merchant sales into PJM and other markets that cannot simply be redirected to hyperscalers. The re-contractable capacity, the megawatts that can realistically migrate from merchant wholesale to premium bilateral PPAs as existing commitments roll off, is probably closer to 8 to 12 GW over the next 3 to 7 years, depending on contract expiration schedules and the FERC co-location outcome.

The re-contracting math is straightforward. Of Constellation’s roughly 22 GW fleet, only a portion is re-contractable at any given time (the rest serves regulated load-serving obligations, existing bilateral deals, and merchant commitments that cannot simply be redirected). As existing commitments roll off over the next several years, each GW that migrates from legacy merchant pricing to premium PPA rates represents hundreds of millions in incremental annual revenue. The analytical question is not whether re-contracting happens (it will) but how much of the eventual re-contracting economics the market has already priced in. The risk is that investors are paying for the destination rather than the journey, and any recession-driven demand destruction, unexpected gas price collapse, or adverse FERC ruling that slows co-location could compress the multiple significantly against an elevated starting valuation.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!_kQk!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F17e8994d-a53d-498e-bc7a-b61fd66d8953_1886x794.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!_kQk!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F17e8994d-a53d-498e-bc7a-b61fd66d8953_1886x794.png)

#### **8.5.1 The Economics That Separate Upstream from Downstream**

Before we continue through the utility landscape, two sector-level dynamics need to be made explicit, because they determine how every name in this section should be valued relative to the producers and enrichers in the sections above.

It is also worth making explicit what most nuclear commentary leaves implicit: nuclear utility PPAs are, structurally, a short on long-term natural gas prices. Here is why.

US wholesale electricity prices are set by the merit order, and in most hours the marginal generator setting the clearing price is a gas-fired combined cycle plant. At $2.50/MMBtu gas (roughly where Henry Hub traded through much of 2024 to 2025), the marginal cost of gas generation is approximately $25 to $30/MWh. This means wholesale power clears at $30 to $45/MWh, and a nuclear PPA reportedly structured at $80 to $100/MWh represents a $45 to $60 premium the hyperscaler pays for carbon-free, firm, 24/7 attributes. That premium looks reasonable if you believe carbon regulation is tightening and grid reliability has value, and it looks expensive if you think gas stays cheap indefinitely.

But the sensitivity runs both directions. At $5 to $6/MMBtu gas (which the US experienced in 2022 and which most long-term forecasts project as plausible under LNG export growth and declining domestic production), marginal gas generation costs rise to $50 to $65/MWh, and wholesale power follows. At that gas price, a $90/MWh nuclear PPA is barely above market clearing, meaning the hyperscaler locked in near-market pricing with the bonus of carbon-free attributes and baseload reliability. At $7+/MMBtu gas (a tail scenario, but one that occurred during the 2022 European energy crisis), wholesale power could reach $80 to $100/MWh, and the nuclear PPAs become below-market contracts.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!tHSs!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F3aba2a3a-feab-4485-827f-7c099e86aa30_1882x836.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!tHSs!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F3aba2a3a-feab-4485-827f-7c099e86aa30_1882x836.png)

Given the 20-year time horizon of these PPAs, the bet that $90/MWh for firm, carbon-free power will look cheap before the contracts expire is probably right. But it is a bet on gas fundamentals over two decades, and your position in Constellation should reflect your conviction on that variable.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!6Fdm!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F61fad39e-fc36-43f9-b8ef-08a881b41aaa_1880x1032.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!6Fdm!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F61fad39e-fc36-43f9-b8ef-08a881b41aaa_1880x1032.png)

To make the fuel cost economics concrete, and to illustrate why Cameco and Constellation are fundamentally different bets despite both being “nuclear,” consider the annual fuel cost for a single 1 GW PWR at 92% capacity factor (generating roughly 8,060 GWh per year). The reactor consumes roughly 500,000 lbs of U3O8 per year (purchased under long-term contracts, not at spot). At a blended contract price of $70/lb, that is roughly $35 million. It requires roughly 18,000 kgU of conversion services at roughly $65/kgU, adding roughly $1.2 million. It needs approximately 120,000 SWU of enrichment at a blended contract rate of roughly $140/SWU (well below spot), totaling roughly $17 million. Fuel fabrication adds another $10 to $15 million per annual reload equivalent. Total fuel cost: roughly $65 to $70 million per year, or about $8 to $9/MWh. Uranium feedstock is roughly 50% of fuel cost. Enrichment is roughly 25%. Fabrication and conversion split the rest.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!zvYz!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F6c535dcd-8980-44a2-805d-86abbdd8637a_1880x884.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!zvYz!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F6c535dcd-8980-44a2-805d-86abbdd8637a_1880x884.png)

The critical insight: a $20/lb swing in uranium spot price (say, from $80 to $100) changes this reactor’s annual fuel bill by roughly $10 million, against total generation revenue of $400 to $800 million. That is noise for the utility. It is everything for the miner. A reader who buys Cameco and Constellation as a “nuclear basket” is actually making two very different bets: Cameco is a leveraged commodity play on uranium prices and fuel cycle bottlenecks. Constellation is a contracted infrastructure play on power prices and PPA terms. Their correlation to the “nuclear theme” is high. Their correlation to the same financial drivers is low. Structure your portfolio accordingly.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!xaRu!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F524cd76d-19b2-4b98-a398-c3c611476b77_1888x1012.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!xaRu!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F524cd76d-19b2-4b98-a398-c3c611476b77_1888x1012.png)

#### **8.5.2 The Rest of the Utility Landscape**

The nuclear utility story is not a US-only story, and investors who treat it as one are leaving exposure on the table.

**Korea Electric Power Corporation (NYSE: KEP / KRX: 015760)**

KEPCO is the state-owned Korean utility that operates Korea’s nuclear fleet and, through its subsidiaries, builds and exports reactors (APR-1400). KEPCO has been a value trap for years (burdened by regulated electricity prices that did not cover costs), but the reversal of Korea’s nuclear phase-out policy and the potential for APR-1400 exports (following the successful Barakah project in the UAE) have improved the outlook.

For investors who want exposure to the nuclear construction thesis in a jurisdiction where reactors actually get built on time and on budget, KEPCO is the most direct play. The Barakah project in the UAE, four APR-1400 units delivered and operational, is the strongest evidence in the world that serial construction of a standardized design achieves competitive economics.

But the state-owned structure, the regulated pricing dynamics that historically suppressed returns, and the Korean market’s structural discount to Western utilities make KEPCO a more complex investment than Constellation. It trades like a regulated utility with construction optionality, not like a growth company, and investors need to accept that framing.

**Électricité de France (EPA: EDF)** was delisted in 2023 after the French government’s full nationalization. It is no longer investable in public markets but remains the world’s largest nuclear fleet operator (57 reactors) and the key player in every European nuclear initiative.

**Kansai Electric Power (TYO: 9503)** is the most nuclear-leveraged utility in Japan. It operates the Takahama and Ohi stations, which were among the first to clear Japan’s post-Fukushima restart process, and has the highest nuclear share of generation among the major Japanese utilities. As Japan continues restarting its idled fleet (12 units back online as of early 2026, with more in the pipeline), Kansai captures the restart thesis more directly than any other listed Japanese name. Kyushu Electric Power (TYO: 9508) was the first Japanese utility to restart a reactor post-Fukushima (Sendai Units 1 and 2) and has the operational track record to prove the restart model works. Both trade at valuations that reflect Japan’s historically suppressed utility returns rather than the scarcity value of their nuclear fleets.

**CEZ Group (PRG: CEZ)** is arguably the most interesting nuclear utility in Europe that almost nobody in the Anglophone investment world is watching. CEZ operates the Dukovany and Temelin nuclear stations in the Czech Republic, both VVER designs that historically depended on Rosatom fuel assemblies. The transition to Westinghouse fuel, which we covered in Part 5, is live and underway. More importantly, CEZ is the developer of the Dukovany 5 new-build project, a 1,200 MWe unit for which the Czech government selected Korea’s KHNP in a closely contested tender. CEZ sits at the intersection of three themes: the European VVER-to-Westinghouse fuel diversification story, the Central European nuclear new-build story, and the broader European energy security repricing. It trades at a fraction of Constellation’s multiples for a fleet that occupies a similarly irreplaceable position in its domestic grid.

**Fortum (HEL: FORTUM)** owns the Loviisa VVER-440 units in Finland, the same reactor type that received its first Westinghouse fuel loading in late 2024. Smaller nuclear exposure than Kansai or CEZ, but relevant for the VVER diversification thesis and as a liquid European utility with nuclear optionality

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!YOrY!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc7a4cddd-e189-4713-92c8-1bc8e4cc5528_1890x1046.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!YOrY!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc7a4cddd-e189-4713-92c8-1bc8e4cc5528_1890x1046.png)

### **8.6 New Build and SMR Developers: Venture Nuclear**

This is the part of the market where the narrative is strongest and the fundamentals are weakest. SMR developers are, at this point, pre-revenue venture-stage companies that happen to be publicly listed. They should be sized accordingly.

**NuScale Power (NYSE: SMR)**: The only SMR with full NRC design certification. The Idaho pilot project was cancelled. NuScale has since partnered with ENTRA1 and TVA on a 6 GW deployment program and signed international deals (Ghana, Romania, Czech Republic). The 77 MWe design received its uprated approval in June 2025. Revenue is minimal and the path to profitability is multi-year and execution-dependent. NuScale’s edge is regulatory (no other Western SMR has full NRC design certification), but regulatory approval without a construction site is a credential, not a business.

**Oklo (NYSE: OKLO)**: Sam Altman-backed fast microreactor developer. Plans for demonstration units at Idaho National Laboratory. No NRC license yet (a previous application was denied for insufficient information; Oklo is re-applying). The stock has been a meme-grade performer, driven almost entirely by the Altman association and AI-nuclear narrative.

**Nano Nuclear Energy (NASDAQ: NNE)**: Developing ultra-small portable microreactors. Extremely early stage. More of a concept stock than an operating company.

**The framework for SMR/new build developers:** These are call options on nuclear construction, priced as such. The implied probability of success embedded in current valuations varies wildly (NuScale’s market cap implies significant near-term commercialization; Oklo’s implies moonshot potential). If you want exposure, size it like a venture bet: small enough that a total loss does not impair the portfolio, large enough that a 5-10x return is meaningful.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!g5mM!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F8c436524-41fb-49ef-a8e3-ec434acb05ea_1886x1046.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!g5mM!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F8c436524-41fb-49ef-a8e3-ec434acb05ea_1886x1046.png)

### **8.7 Adjacent and Overlooked Plays**

The nuclear supply chain does not end at uranium miners and reactor operators. There is an entire ecosystem of companies that make the components, build the structures, and provide the services without which no reactor operates. These names are underrepresented in most nuclear portfolios because they are not “pure play,” but several of them are better businesses than the pure plays, with higher margins, more defensible moats, and less binary risk profiles.

**Curtiss-Wright Corporation (NYSE: CW)** is the name most nuclear-focused institutional investors own that never appears in retail-oriented nuclear primers. Curtiss-Wright manufactures reactor coolant pumps, control rod drive mechanisms, naval nuclear components, spent fuel management systems, and instrumentation for essentially every Western reactor type. Their equipment sits inside PWRs, BWRs, and naval propulsion reactors across the US fleet and allied navies. The investment logic is elegant: regardless of which reactor design wins the next decade, regardless of whether SMRs succeed or fail, regardless of whether new build happens on schedule or stalls, Curtiss-Wright supplies the existing fleet and every plausible future fleet. Life extensions, uprates, maintenance, and refurbishment all flow through their order book. It is the “sells ammunition to both sides” play in nuclear, and it trades accordingly.

**BWX Technologies (NYSE: BWXT)** is the US monopoly supplier of naval nuclear reactor components and fuel. Every US Navy aircraft carrier and submarine runs on BWXT fuel. This is a defense-industrial franchise that happens to have growing commercial nuclear exposure through the eVinci microreactor program, DOE contracts, and medical isotope production. BWXT trades at a defense-sector premium that reflects the naval nuclear monopoly; the commercial nuclear optionality is essentially free in the current stock price.

**Doosan Enerbility (KRX: 034020)** manufactures the reactor pressure vessels, steam generators, and heavy forgings for Korea’s APR-1400. If you believe the Korean construction thesis (and the Barakah evidence says you should), Doosan is the company that physically builds the hardware. It is the most direct play on Korean nuclear export success. For investors who can access the Korean exchange, Doosan offers exposure to the part of the nuclear value chain where execution risk has actually been demonstrated to be manageable.

**Rolls-Royce Holdings (LSE: RR)** has two nuclear angles. The legacy business builds and services submarine reactor plants for the Royal Navy. The growth bet is Rolls-Royce SMR, a consortium targeting deployment of a 470 MWe small modular reactor in the UK, with the first unit targeting operations in the early 2030s. The UK government has backed the programme with significant development funding. Rolls-Royce is an enormous company and the SMR programme is a small fraction of revenue, but for investors who want UK nuclear exposure without concentrating in a single-product SMR developer, it provides optionality within a diversified industrial.

**GE Vernova (NYSE: GEV)** is the parent of GE Hitachi Nuclear Energy, which makes the BWRX-300, arguably the Western SMR closest to commercial deployment (construction started at Darlington, DOE funding for TVA deployment). GE Vernova is a massive power and energy company; nuclear is a modest contributor to overall revenues. But the BWRX-300’s progress makes GEV the lowest-risk way to own a piece of the SMR story, because even if the SMR programme disappoints, the rest of the business (gas turbines, grid equipment, wind) provides a floor. You are not paying a venture multiple for an unproven reactor design. You are paying an industrial multiple with SMR upside attached.

**Fluor Corporation (NYSE: FLR)** is one of the few Western EPC contractors with demonstrated nuclear construction capability. If the Western new-build pipeline materializes at anything close to the $80 billion framework scale, Fluor is one of a handful of companies that can actually manage the construction. Bechtel (private) is the other. The constraint is that nuclear EPC is a small and historically painful part of Fluor’s business (Vogtle was not a happy experience for anyone involved), but the scarcity of nuclear-capable contractors means Fluor’s optionality here is real.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!UKNX!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F0af62fc5-9c01-4533-b40d-295ae73b5bc7_1880x1056.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!UKNX!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F0af62fc5-9c01-4533-b40d-295ae73b5bc7_1880x1056.png)

The Additional Developers

Beyond the Tier 2 names (NexGen, Denison), there is a long tail of uranium developers at various stages of advancement. Most will never become mines. Some will become very good mines. The challenge for investors is distinguishing geology from execution.

**IsoEnergy (TSX: ISO)** holds the Hurricane deposit in the Athabasca Basin, the highest-grade uranium discovery since NexGen’s Arrow. Hurricane is still at an early stage relative to Arrow or Wheeler River, but the grade (33.9% U₃O₈ in the summer 2022 discovery holes) is extraordinary. IsoEnergy merged with Consolidated Uranium to build a broader portfolio, but Hurricane is the asset that matters.

**Ur-Energy (TSX: URE / NYSE: URG)** operates Lost Creek, one of the few producing ISR uranium mines in the United States. Small scale, but directly leveraged to US domestic procurement policy (Section 232, critical minerals designation, DOE reserve purchases).

**Bannerman Energy (ASX: BMN)** holds the Etango project in Namibia, one of the largest undeveloped uranium deposits outside Canada and Kazakhstan. Bannerman is a scale play on higher uranium prices in a stable African jurisdiction.

**Deep Yellow (ASX: DYL)** merged with Vimy Resources, giving it the Tumas project in Namibia and the Mulga Rock project in Western Australia. Dual-jurisdiction exposure across two of the more politically stable uranium-hosting countries.

**Lotus Resources (ASX: LOT)** acquired the Kayelekera mine in Malawi from Paladin. Restart economics are leveraged to sustained higher uranium prices.

A note on the developer tail: these names are leveraged bets on higher uranium prices, full stop. They do not have the resource quality of NexGen, the jurisdictional premium of Denison, or the integrated value chain of Cameco. What they have is optionality. In a world where uranium prices sustain above $80 for years and Western utilities are scrambling for supply diversity, some of these developers will be acquired, partnered, or funded into production. In a world where uranium stalls or retreats, most of them will remain exploration companies with nice drill results and no revenue. Size accordingly.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!j4RF!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F849163a8-666f-475f-8c7a-e4bfdb5ef5be_1888x826.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!j4RF!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F849163a8-666f-475f-8c7a-e4bfdb5ef5be_1888x826.png)

**ETFs and Basket Vehicles**

For investors who agree with the nuclear thesis but do not want to pick individual names, or who want a liquid core position to complement specific high-conviction bets, three ETFs offer meaningfully different expressions of the same theme.

**Sprott Uranium Miners ETF (NYSE: URNM)** is the most concentrated. It holds uranium miners and physical uranium vehicles (SPUT is typically the largest holding), giving you direct commodity and producer exposure. This is Proposition 1 (uranium price higher) in ETF form.

**Global X Uranium ETF (NYSE: URA)** is broader. It includes miners, developers, and some nuclear technology companies, providing more diversified nuclear exposure with less commodity concentration than URNM. It is a blend of Propositions 1 and 4.

**VanEck Uranium+Nuclear Energy ETF (NYSE: NLR)** is utility-heavy, with significant holdings in Constellation, utilities, and nuclear technology companies alongside miners. This maps most closely to Proposition 3 (fleet operators mispriced) with some commodity exposure layered in.

The compositional differences matter. If you own URNM, you are making a uranium commodity bet. If you own NLR, you are making a nuclear power generation bet. If you own URA, you are somewhere in between. Most investors who buy “a nuclear ETF” do not check which one they own. They should.`

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!HePN!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fa795b1d7-3a93-496a-aab4-f016fa32a6b0_1880x814.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!HePN!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fa795b1d7-3a93-496a-aab4-f016fa32a6b0_1880x814.png)

### **8.8 The TSCS Nuclear Portfolio Framework**

**The Tier Structure**

Rather than prescribing specific allocations (which would go stale before you finish reading this sentence), here is how to think about the tiers of nuclear exposure. The tiers are ranked by the probability-weighted robustness of the thesis, not by expected return. The highest-expected-return names are in Tier 3 and 4 precisely because they carry the most execution risk.

Tier 1: Own the Existing Machine. Physical uranium, the integrated major (Cameco), and the fleet operators (Constellation, Kansai, CEZ). These names do not require a single new reactor to be built anywhere in the West. They require the existing fleet to keep running (it will), fuel demand to grow (it is), and supply bottlenecks to persist (they will). This is the base case, and the base case is enough.

Tier 2: Own the Bottleneck. Centrus for enrichment. Cameco again for conversion (Port Hope). The structural deficit in Western SWU and conversion capacity is the highest-conviction call in this primer, and these are the only public equities that capture it directly. If you own nothing else in nuclear, you should understand why these bottlenecks exist and what they mean for pricing power.

Tier 3: Own the Next Mine. NexGen, Denison, and the better-positioned developers (IsoEnergy, Boss, Ur-Energy). These are leveraged bets on higher uranium prices AND successful project execution. The “AND” is the key word. Uranium can go to $120 and a developer with permitting problems still goes to zero. Geology is necessary. Execution is sufficient.

Tier 4: Own the Future. SMR developers (NuScale, Oklo, Kairos), advanced reactor plays (TerraPower, X-energy), and construction-leveraged names (GE Vernova for BWRX-300, BWXT for eVinci, Fluor and Curtiss-Wright for EPC and components). These are call options on nuclear construction with 5-10 year expiry dates. Size them like options. Define your maximum loss before you enter. Do not average down on hope.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!ei0m!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F078f0e71-f44e-4f68-82e7-43d59291e6d6_1880x1024.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!ei0m!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F078f0e71-f44e-4f68-82e7-43d59291e6d6_1880x1024.png)

**What to avoid:**

Jurisdiction traps. A uranium deposit in Niger is a geological fact and a commercial fiction. A mine in Kazakhstan whose offtake is locked into Chinese contracts and whose product transits through Russian rail infrastructure is not a “Western supply” play, no matter what the investor presentation says. The jurisdiction premium is not a temporary risk-off trade. It is a structural repricing of where nuclear fuel can and cannot flow, and it is widening, not narrowing. If you cannot trace the supply chain from pit to reactor through friendly jurisdictions, you do not own what you think you own.

Binary event stocks. A company whose entire valuation depends on a single catalyst (this specific licence, this specific contract, this specific government decision) is not an investment. It is a lottery ticket with a narrative attached. The nuclear sector is full of these because permitting timelines create natural “catalyst dates” that attract speculative capital. Some of them will pay off. Most will not. If you take binary bets, size them as binary bets.

Nuclear cosplay. The nuclear theme has attracted a constellation of companies that rebrand themselves as nuclear-adjacent to capture retail enthusiasm. New ticker, new investor deck, same empty revenue line. The test is simple: open the most recent quarterly filing and look at the revenue number. If it says zero, or if the revenue comes entirely from government grants rather than commercial operations, you are not investing in a nuclear company. You are investing in a nuclear story. Stories can be worth owning at the right price. Just do not confuse them with businesses.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!pMlK!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F3eccc2b3-ecb8-41ff-b617-c41f4748cc2f_1884x950.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!pMlK!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F3eccc2b3-ecb8-41ff-b617-c41f4748cc2f_1884x950.png)

The “I’m long nuclear” basket. Buying one of everything in the nuclear sector is not a thesis. It is a confession that you do not have one. Cameco and Oklo are both “nuclear” in the same way that ExxonMobil and a wildcat driller are both “oil.” They share a theme. They share almost nothing else: not risk profile, not time horizon, not the variables that drive their returns. If you cannot articulate which of the five propositions each name in your portfolio maps to, you have a basket, not a portfolio. Baskets are for ETFs. Portfolios are for investors who did the work.

### **8.9 The Meta-Framework: What Are You Actually Betting On?**

Every nuclear investment is, at root, a bet on one or more of the following propositions. Understanding which proposition each name maps to is the difference between a coherent portfolio and a grab bag of tickers.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!fk8V!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F58bec957-e2bf-4827-ab62-e21af4533cff_1882x1022.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!fk8V!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F58bec957-e2bf-4827-ab62-e21af4533cff_1882x1022.png)

**Proposition 1: Uranium prices go higher.** If this is your primary thesis, own the commodity (SPUT, Yellow Cake) and the lowest-cost producers (Cameco, Kazatomprom, Boss, Denison). Enrichment and utility plays are secondary.

**Proposition 2: The fuel cycle bottleneck persists.** If you believe enrichment and conversion scarcity is the binding constraint, own Centrus and Cameco (for Port Hope). This thesis does not require uranium prices to spike; it requires SWU and conversion prices to stay elevated, which is a higher-probability bet.

**Proposition 3: Nuclear fleet operators are mispriced in a power-constrained world.** If you believe data center demand and grid stress will push power prices structurally higher, own Constellation. This is a power market bet that happens to express through nuclear assets.

**Proposition 4: Western governments will actually build new reactors.** If you believe the “sovereign signal” has been issued and will be sustained, own Cameco (for Westinghouse), BWXT, Fluor, and the SMR developers. This is the highest-conviction, highest-risk proposition, the one most dependent on political variables that are, by definition, unpredictable, and the one where the $80 billion framework’s conversion from aspirational to binding will be the key signal.

**Proposition 5: The nuclear theme attracts persistent capital flows regardless of near-term fundamentals.** If you believe the narrative momentum (AI + data centers + energy security + climate) will continue attracting generalist capital into nuclear equities, own the liquid large-caps (Cameco, Constellation, NuScale, Centrus) and the physical vehicles (SPUT). This is a momentum/flows thesis, not a fundamental thesis, and it should be traded, not held.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!GwuK!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F9abcff63-ca10-4c79-8400-4c887bbc2479_1884x1034.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!GwuK!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F9abcff63-ca10-4c79-8400-4c887bbc2479_1884x1034.png)

Most thoughtful nuclear investors are betting on some combination of Propositions 1, 2, and 3, with Proposition 4 as upside optionality. The mistake most commonly made is over-indexing to Proposition 4 (new build) at the expense of Propositions 1-3 (commodity, bottleneck, fleet value), because the new-build narrative is more exciting but the commodity and bottleneck theses are more probable and more investable today.

The nuclear renaissance does not require a single new reactor to be built in the West for the investment thesis to work. It requires the existing fleet to keep running (it will), life extensions to proceed (they are), fuel demand to grow (it is), and supply bottlenecks to persist (they will). Everything else is upside.

Build your portfolio from that base, not from the press releases.

Part 2 of this primer takes each of these propositions and stress-tests it against live market data, specific equity positioning, scenario analysis, and the quantitative framework you need to actually deploy capital rather than just understand the sector. That is next.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!on88!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F96da8ab0-0716-4545-b120-133cb42eea0b_1884x1030.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!on88!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F96da8ab0-0716-4545-b120-133cb42eea0b_1884x1030.png)

## **Part 9: Risks and Bear Cases**

### **9.1 The Section the Bulls Don’t Want You to Read**

Every serious bull case is the one that has survived its own bear case. If you cannot articulate exactly what would make you wrong, you do not have a thesis. You have a religion. TSCS does not publish religions.

What follows is an honest attempt to enumerate everything that could go wrong with the nuclear investment thesis, ranked roughly by probability and severity. Some of these risks are real and underappreciated. Some are loudly debated but ultimately manageable. And some are tail risks that would redefine the sector if they materialized. Your job, as an investor, is to assign probabilities and position accordingly.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!A7ue!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fce63997b-755d-4358-9a5e-8162499f8259_1600x767.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!A7ue!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fce63997b-755d-4358-9a5e-8162499f8259_1600x767.png)

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!-9qg!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F6601dc4b-f044-4dc3-8aa4-d5ba926d8e54_1600x720.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!-9qg!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F6601dc4b-f044-4dc3-8aa4-d5ba926d8e54_1600x720.png)

### **9.2 Risk 1: Natural Gas Stays Cheap and Kills the Economic Case**

**Probability: Moderate. Severity: High.**

This is the bear case that nuclear bulls dismiss too quickly.

The entire power-price thesis for nuclear (which underpins Constellation’s valuation, the restart economics of Three Mile Island and Palisades, and the new-build business case) depends on electricity prices staying elevated relative to the operating costs of nuclear plants. The single largest determinant of wholesale electricity prices in the United States is the price of natural gas, because gas-fired combined cycle plants are the marginal price setters in most deregulated markets.

If the US natural gas market enters a sustained period of oversupply (which is not implausible given the massive Permian associated gas production, the Haynesville ramp, and LNG export terminal buildout that could be curtailed by demand weakness), Henry Hub prices could stay in the $2-3/MMBtu range for years. At those prices, combined cycle gas plants produce electricity at roughly $25-35/MWh. Nuclear’s operating cost advantage narrows considerably, and the scarcity premium that Constellation captures evaporates.

The counter-argument: gas prices have historically been volatile, and any sustained increase in demand (AI load growth, LNG exports, coal retirements) would tighten the gas market. Also, nuclear’s value is not purely economic; its 92%+ capacity factor, zero carbon emissions, and baseload reliability carry premiums that gas cannot replicate, especially as carbon pricing and clean energy standards become more stringent. But if your thesis depends on power prices, you need to stress-test it against a world where gas stays cheap.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!zzcN!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fffc85e35-e401-4882-bcdb-f57235105892_1600x897.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!zzcN!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fffc85e35-e401-4882-bcdb-f57235105892_1600x897.png)

### **9.3 Risk 2: The Cost of Capital Is the Hidden Variable in Every Nuclear LCOE Comparison**

**Probability: High (rates are already elevated). Severity: High for new build, Low for existing fleet.**

This is the bear case that most nuclear investment theses simply ignore, and it is arguably the most important variable in the entire new-build economic analysis.

Nuclear power is the most capital-intensive energy technology in commercial deployment. A 1 GW AP1000 has an all-in construction cost of $8-12 billion assuming NOAK economics (which, for the record, have never been demonstrated in the West). A comparable 1 GW solar plus 4-hour battery installation costs roughly $1.5-2.5 billion.

The operational economics favor nuclear (near-zero marginal fuel cost, 92% capacity factor, 60-80 year lifetime), but the upfront capital requirement is 3-5 times larger. This means nuclear’s levelized cost of electricity is extraordinarily sensitive to the discount rate.

Here is the math that most nuclear advocates prefer you not see.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Yqxh!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F903f423c-f4b4-430e-864a-b50b2ed309b7_1600x895.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Yqxh!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F903f423c-f4b4-430e-864a-b50b2ed309b7_1600x895.png)

The sensitivity is not symmetrical. Moving from 3% to 5% adds roughly $20-25/MWh. Moving from 5% to 7% adds another $25-30/MWh. Moving from 7% to 10% adds $40-50/MWh. The relationship between cost of capital and LCOE is convex, not linear, because IDC compounds on a larger and larger base as the project takes longer and costs more.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!kcuT!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F2561a908-2709-46de-9049-936965ce9d06_1600x850.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!kcuT!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F2561a908-2709-46de-9049-936965ce9d06_1600x850.png)

Without at least one of these, no private developer will break ground. The $80 billion Westinghouse partnership framework explicitly includes “the US Government arranging financing,” which is an acknowledgment of this reality, not an incidental detail. If interest rates stay elevated (the 10-year Treasury yield has been above 4% since 2023), the government’s cost of facilitating that financing increases, reducing the political appetite for nuclear subsidies and increasing the effective cost to taxpayers.

Every 100 basis points on a $10 billion project adds roughly $100 million per year in interest expense during a 5-7 year construction period. That compounds into billions over the project lifecycle.

For investors, this risk is most acute for Tier 3 and Tier 4 plays (construction-leveraged companies and SMR developers). The existing fleet is unaffected: those plants are already built, already depreciated, and generate electricity at $25-30/MWh operating cost regardless of where the 10-year Treasury sits. Constellation’s fleet is actually a beneficiary of higher rates, because elevated rates make it harder for competitors to build new capacity, reinforcing the scarcity premium on existing nuclear baseload.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!hYXr!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F6996c225-90c5-41f2-9e95-2bd90769d592_1600x891.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!hYXr!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F6996c225-90c5-41f2-9e95-2bd90769d592_1600x891.png)

The interest rate bear case is a new-build bear case, not a sector bear case, and your portfolio construction should reflect that distinction.

The counter-argument: government-facilitated financing (DOE LPO loans, sovereign guarantees) effectively removes rate sensitivity for the projects that receive support. The Inflation Reduction Act’s nuclear PTC ($15/MWh) and the DOE’s expanded lending authority provide a floor. And in countries like China and Korea, where the government is the developer, the cost of capital is whatever the sovereign decides it is. But for anyone modeling Western new-build economics, stress-testing against a range of discount rates (3%, 5%, 7%, 10%) is not optional. It is the single most important variable in the analysis.

### **9.3 Risk 3: SMR Cost Overruns Replicate Large Reactor Problems at Smaller Scale**

**Probability: High (for first-of-a-kind projects). Severity: Moderate to High.**

The entire promise of SMRs is that they will be cheaper, faster, and more financeable than large reactors. The theory is compelling: factory fabrication enables manufacturing learning curves, smaller modules reduce on-site construction risk, standardized designs eliminate bespoke engineering, and shorter build times reduce interest during construction.

The problem is that NuScale’s Idaho pilot, the most advanced Western SMR project, was cancelled in November 2023 after cost estimates nearly doubled, from roughly $5.3 billion to $9.3 billion, largely due to inflation, rising interest rates, and the inherent challenge of costing a first-of-a-kind project. The cost per MWe for the NuScale CFPP had reached roughly $20,000/kW, which is not competitive with anything.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!E_w5!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd708dee7-4fe3-4422-94c0-b287a492682f_1600x880.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!E_w5!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd708dee7-4fe3-4422-94c0-b287a492682f_1600x880.png)

Every SMR project currently advancing is a first-of-a-kind deployment. FOAK projects in nuclear have a 100% historical track record of cost overruns. Not 80%. Not 90%. One hundred percent. Vogtle, Flamanville, Olkiluoto, Hinkley Point C, even the relatively well-managed Barakah project in the UAE experienced schedule adjustments. The learning curve that makes Nth-of-a-kind (NOAK) units cheaper requires surviving the FOAK pain, and there is no guarantee that investors and utilities will have the patience or capital to push through FOAK disappointments to reach NOAK economics.

If the BWRX-300 at Darlington comes in late and over budget (which is the base case for any honest analyst given historical precedent), the narrative damage to the SMR thesis could be severe, even if the long-term economics remain sound. Markets do not distinguish between “temporary FOAK growing pains” and “SMRs don’t work.” They react to the headline.

The counter-argument: China’s Linglong One and Shidaowan HTGR are demonstrating that advanced and small reactor designs can be built on time, and the BWRX-300 is an evolutionary design based on 65 years of BWR operating experience, not a speculative Gen IV concept. The risk is real but not existential; it delays the timeline rather than killing the thesis.

### **9.4 Risk 4: A Nuclear Accident Resets Public Sentiment**

**Probability: Low. Severity: Extreme.**

This is the tail risk that haunts the sector. The global nuclear fleet has operated for over 18,000 reactor-years with three significant accidents (TMI, Chernobyl, Fukushima), two of which involved reactor designs (RBMK and early BWR) that are either extinct or dramatically improved. The probability of a major accident at a modern PWR or BWR is estimated at roughly 1 in 10 million reactor-years by probabilistic risk assessment. This is an extraordinarily low probability.

But the consequences of a single accident, anywhere in the world, would be catastrophic for the sector regardless of the actual radiological harm. Fukushima killed zero people from radiation, and it still shut down Japan’s entire nuclear fleet and catalyzed Germany’s nuclear exit. The public does not process nuclear risk through probability distributions. It processes nuclear risk through narrative and fear. One mushroom cloud photograph, even metaphorical, can destroy a decade of positive momentum.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!bpy0!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fb3edf5db-4886-4227-898a-fbaf9fda4d81_1600x890.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!bpy0!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fb3edf5db-4886-4227-898a-fbaf9fda4d81_1600x890.png)

The countries most vulnerable to an accident-driven sentiment reversal are those with large, aging fleets (US, France) and those with new, untested designs entering service (China, which is deploying more novel reactor types simultaneously than any country in history). An accident at a Chinese Gen IV reactor, for instance, would not just affect China; it would trigger a global policy reassessment.

The counter-argument: modern reactor designs have multiple, independent, passive safety systems that make severe accidents orders of magnitude less likely than at TMI, Chernobyl, or Fukushima. The defense-in-depth principle (multiple barriers between radioactive material and the environment) has been validated across 70 years of commercial operation. And the post-Fukushima regulatory response (seismic re-evaluations, flood protection upgrades, portable emergency equipment) has further reduced residual risk. But “low probability” is not “zero probability,” and anyone who tells you nuclear is risk-free is selling something.

### **9.5 Risk 5: The “Structural Deficit” Is Smaller Than the Bulls Claim**

**Probability: Moderate. Severity: Moderate.**

We covered this honestly in Part 4, but it bears repeating in the risk section because it is the most common source of disappointment for uranium investors.

The bull case rests on a structural gap between primary uranium production (~150-160 million lbs/year) and reactor demand (~185 million lbs/year), with secondary supplies depleting. But the deficit is measured against *requirements*, not actual purchases. Utilities routinely undercontract, defer procurement, manage inventory cycles, and optimize fuel assemblies to stretch the life of existing fuel loads. The “deficit” in any given year is partly a real physical gap and partly a reflection of timing decisions by sophisticated buyers who have years of fuel inventory and no urgency.

Additionally, supply elasticity is not zero. At $80+/lb, projects that were uneconomic at $40 are restarting. ISR operations in Wyoming and Texas are ramping. Australia’s Honeymoon is producing at $23/lb cash cost. Paladin’s Langer Heinrich is back. NexGen and Denison are advancing toward production. Kazatomprom has latent capacity it could bring online (if acid supply and political will permit). The question is not whether new supply responds to price signals but whether it responds fast enough to prevent price spikes, and history suggests that commodity supply responses always lag the cycle by several years, which creates the volatility that makes uranium both attractive and treacherous.

The specific scenarios where the deficit thesis disappoints:

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!8gmn!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F81952085-e474-42ab-a027-9a3998cd6f77_1600x766.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!8gmn!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F81952085-e474-42ab-a027-9a3998cd6f77_1600x766.png)

China’s reactor construction decelerates. The demand projections throughout this primer assume China’s reactor program executes at planned pace: 10 units approved per year, 110 GW by 2030, 150 GW by 2035. But China’s economy is navigating a property crisis, demographic decline, potential deflation, and industrial overcapacity. If electricity demand growth undershoots plan, the reactor approval pipeline slows, and Chinese uranium/SWU demand misses projections, the global supply-demand picture loosens materially. Given that China is responsible for essentially all growth in global nuclear generation outside the existing Western fleet, a Chinese demand miss is not a tail risk. It is a plausible scenario that deserves explicit weight in any supply-demand model. The 132,000-tonne strategic stockpile provides additional buffer: if Beijing draws it down rather than procuring on the open market, it removes a major demand source from the global balance for years.

Kazakhstan resolves its acid supply problems and ramps to full subsoil use agreement capacity (possible by 2027-2028). A major undiscovered secondary supply source emerges (government stockpile release, accelerated reactor decommissioning freeing fuel). Japan’s restarts stall again (reducing demand growth from the second-largest historical nuclear fleet).

The counter-argument: even accounting for all of these factors, the directionality of the deficit is robust. The demand trajectory (reactor life extensions, Chinese construction, data center PPAs) is growing faster than supply can respond. The secondary supply buffer is thinner than at any point in the last two decades. And the geopolitical bifurcation of the market (Western vs. Eastern supply chains) means Western utilities are competing for a shrinking pool of accessible uranium, even if the global aggregate looks more balanced.

### **9.6 Risk 6: The Western Coordination Freeze Never Actually Breaks**

**Probability: Moderate. Severity: High for new-build plays, low for commodity plays.**

This is the Arden risk, and it is the most intellectually interesting bear case.

Suppose the US government issues all the right signals (executive orders, $80 billion partnership frameworks, NRC reform, HALEU funding) but fails to follow through with the institutional restructuring that Arden argues is necessary. Suppose the “sovereign signal” turns out to be visible but not irreversible: a subsequent administration reverses the executive orders, defunds the DOE contracts, and the nuclear construction program dies in its cradle. Suppose the coordination freeze is not a temporary political condition but a permanent feature of American institutional architecture, baked into the separation of powers, adversarial legalism, and fragmented regulatory structure that Delmas and Heiman diagnosed two decades ago.

In this scenario, the new-build thesis fails. The $80 billion AP1000 program does not deliver. SMR deployments slip from the 2030s to the 2040s. The US remains a country that operates its existing nuclear fleet (well) but cannot build new reactors (at all). The investment implications: Tiers 3 and 4 from Part 8 (construction plays and SMR developers) underperform, while Tiers 1 and 2 (commodity, producers, fleet operators) continue to benefit from the existing fleet’s insatiable fuel demand and irreplaceable power generation.

This is why we structured the portfolio framework the way we did. The core positions (Cameco, Constellation, SPUT) do not require a single new Western reactor to be built. They require the existing fleet to keep running, fuel demand to grow, and supply bottlenecks to persist. All of those conditions are already true. New build is upside optionality, not the base case.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!cDqs!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fbfb5fbca-b6e1-4835-bdad-f7a2360b31ba_1600x898.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!cDqs!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fbfb5fbca-b6e1-4835-bdad-f7a2360b31ba_1600x898.png)

The counter-argument: China and Korea will build regardless of what the US does, and their construction programs drive the marginal demand for uranium, enrichment, and conversion. The thesis does not depend on Washington solving its coordination problem. It depends on global nuclear capacity growing, which the IAEA has projected upward for five consecutive years.

### **9.7 Risk 7: Fusion Disrupts the Thesis (Eventually)**

**Probability: Low within investment horizon. Severity: Theoretically extreme.**

Fusion is the permanent “30 years away” punchline of the energy industry. But genuine progress is being made. The NIF achieved ignition (more energy out than laser energy in, though not more than total facility energy) in December 2022. Commonwealth Fusion Systems has raised approximately $3 billion and is targeting Q>1 demonstration with its SPARC tokamak by 2027. Helion Energy demonstrated the first private deuterium-tritium fusion in February 2026 with its Polaris prototype, achieving plasma temperatures of 150 million degrees Celsius. China’s EAST tokamak has achieved increasingly long plasma confinement times.

If commercial fusion arrives, it renders fission obsolete. Unlimited fuel (deuterium from seawater), no long-lived radioactive waste, no meltdown risk, and energy densities that make even uranium look quaint.

But “arrives” means “delivers electricity to the grid at competitive cost,” which is an engineering and economic challenge of staggering complexity. No fusion device has ever produced net electricity. The most optimistic commercial timelines (Commonwealth Fusion Systems targets the early 2030s for a demonstration plant) imply grid-scale commercial deployment no earlier than the 2040s, and that assumes no setbacks.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!f0f0!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F01002239-0af2-474b-b64b-a56c70834f15_1600x901.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!f0f0!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F01002239-0af2-474b-b64b-a56c70834f15_1600x901.png)

For any investment with a 5-10 year horizon, fusion is not a relevant risk factor. For a 20-30 year horizon, it is the most important variable in the energy landscape. Nuclear investors should be aware of it, monitor it, and not lose sleep over it this decade.

### **9.8 Risk 8: Tariffs, Trade Wars, and Policy Reversals**

**Probability: High (they are already happening). Severity: Moderate.**

The Trump administration’s tariffs on Canadian goods directly affect Cameco, the largest Western uranium producer, by adding approximately 10% to costs for US customers. The Section 232 investigation on critical minerals (including uranium) could result in further trade restrictions. The ongoing uncertainty about whether uranium, conversion, and enrichment services will be included in future tariff regimes has already suppressed utility contracting activity through most of 2025 (as we documented in Part 4).

Beyond tariffs, the nuclear fuel sector is uniquely vulnerable to policy whiplash. The US ban on Russian uranium imports (effective 2024) could theoretically be modified or waived under a future administration that prioritizes engagement with Russia. DOE funding commitments ($2.7 billion for enrichment, $400 million for BWRX-300 deployment) could be rescinded if Congress claws back appropriations. The IRA’s nuclear production tax credits, which provide $15/MWh for existing nuclear plants, could be modified or eliminated.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!an9P!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fef6fce4d-6935-48b5-83d9-bc269bb7d5f3_1600x899.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!an9P!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fef6fce4d-6935-48b5-83d9-bc269bb7d5f3_1600x899.png)

The counter-argument: bipartisan support for nuclear is stronger than at any point in 40 years. The ADVANCE Act passed with overwhelming margins. Nuclear is one of the few energy topics where the progressive climate caucus and the conservative energy security caucus agree. Policy reversals are possible but would require a political environment that does not currently exist.

### **9.9 Risk 9: Proliferation Concerns Constrain Enrichment Expansion**

**Probability: Low to Moderate. Severity: Moderate.**

Enrichment is dual-use technology. The same centrifuges that produce 4% LEU for reactors can, with additional cascading, produce 90% HEU for weapons. As the West expands enrichment capacity (Urenco, Orano, Centrus, General Matter), and as HALEU production (5-19.75% enrichment) grows for advanced reactors, proliferation concerns will inevitably intensify.

The Nuclear Suppliers Group’s debate over export controls for carrier and coolant salts (used in advanced reactor designs) has already delayed a US rulemaking on advanced reactor exports. If proliferation hawks succeed in imposing tighter export controls, it could constrain the international market for enrichment services and slow the deployment of advanced reactors that require HALEU fuel.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!GQD2!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F05197c8a-bdcf-4c50-b158-1952b2f03a63_1600x702.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!GQD2!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F05197c8a-bdcf-4c50-b158-1952b2f03a63_1600x702.png)

This risk is most relevant for Centrus (which is building HALEU production capacity) and for the advanced reactor developers (Kairos, X-energy, TerraPower) whose designs require fuel enriched above the 5% threshold that has been the commercial standard for decades.

### **9.10 Risk 10: Long-Duration Storage Is Closer Than the Bulls Acknowledge**

**Probability: Moderate (technology is real; commercial scale is unproven). Severity: Gradual erosion of new-build economics, not acute disruption.**

The standard nuclear bull rebuttal to the “renewables plus storage” bear case goes like this: batteries can only store 4 hours, therefore you need nuclear for baseload. This was accurate in 2023. It is becoming less accurate in 2026, and it may be materially wrong by 2030. Solar panel costs have fallen 99% since 1976. Lithium-ion battery costs have fallen 97% since 1991. These cost curves show no sign of flattening. Solar plus 4-hour battery storage is approaching $30/MWh all-in in the sunniest regions.

On a pure LCOE basis, new-build nuclear is already two to three times more expensive than utility-scale solar in most geographies. If the gap widens further, the political will to subsidize new nuclear construction could erode. But the real threat is not 4-hour lithium-ion. It is 100-hour iron-air. Form Energy, backed by $405 million in recent funding from GE Vernova, Breakthrough Energy Ventures, and Energy Impact Partners, has developed an iron-air battery capable of 100 hours of discharge. The chemistry is elegantly simple: the battery “rusts” iron during discharge (absorbing oxygen from the air) and reverses the process during charging. The raw materials are iron, water, and air. Form’s target cost is $20/kWh, roughly one-fifth of current lithium-ion on a per-kWh basis. At 100 hours of duration, this translates to approximately $2,000/kW of firm capacity, which, if achieved, would be cost-competitive with new-build nuclear on a firmed-capacity basis.

[![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!TygB!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F82b9baee-4b6a-43fa-a7bb-8a4ea12fddb1_1600x844.png)](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!TygB!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F82b9baee-4b6a-43fa-a7bb-8a4ea12fddb1_1600x844.png)

The deployment timeline is no longer theoretical. Form’s first commercial pilot (1.5 MW / 150 MWh with Great River Energy in Minnesota) broke ground in August 2024 and targets late 2025 commissioning. Three GWh-scale deployments are targeted for 2026: two projects with Xcel Energy (10 MW / 1 GWh each) and a 15 MW / 1.5 GWh facility for Georgia Power. In February 2026, Google and Xcel Energy announced a 300 MW / 30 GWh iron-air deployment, described as “the largest battery project by gigawatt-hour energy capacity announced to date in the world.” Form’s largest planned installation, an 85 MW / 8.5 GWh project in rural Maine funded by a $147 million DOE grant, targets construction start in 2027 and operations in 2028.

Form’s Weirton, West Virginia factory is producing batteries now, with 14 GWh of projects announced. The nuclear bulls are correct on two technical limitations. First, round-trip efficiency is low, roughly 40-50% compared to 90% for lithium-ion. You lose half the energy in the charge-discharge cycle. For a grid with abundant cheap solar where curtailed energy is effectively free, this penalty is tolerable.

For a grid where every MWh costs money, it is significant. Second, the manufacturing scale needed to meaningfully affect grid-scale storage (tens of GWh per year) has not yet been demonstrated. The factory exists. The production rate does not. But it would be analytically dishonest to dismiss iron-air as irrelevant when it has $405 million in funding, contracts with six major utilities, a DOE grant, a collaboration with GE Vernova, a 30 GWh project with Google, and a cost target that, if achieved, fundamentally changes the firmed-renewable cost calculation.
